<meta>The transcription is a title page for a 17th-century edition of the Cyranides, a famous Greco-Egyptian hermetic text. It introduces both the ancient source material and the modern (1638) scholarly additions by Andreas Rivinus.</meta>

->Under the Guiding Help of the Supreme Redeemer,<-

# THE CYRANIDES OF CYRANUS, <note>original: "Kirani Kiranides"; the Cyranides is a well-known ancient compendium of "physika" (natural powers) traditionally attributed to King Cyranus of Persia, though likely of Greco-Egyptian origin.</note>

->And added to them<-

# THE COMPLETIONS OF RHYAKINUS. <note>original: "Rhyakini Koronides"; "Rhyakinus" is a pseudonym for the editor, Andreas Rivinus. "Koronides" refers to a finishing flourish or scholarly completion of a work.</note>

->Of which the former [Cyranus]<-
->In a fourfold arrangement of both Books and<-
->Elements, from just as many languages,<-
->Deals firstly **with 24 GEMS, 24 HERBS, 24 BIRDS,**<-
->**and 24 FISH,** always in four parts,<-
->and mostly mixed together to establish a **Fourfold Remedy**;<- <term>Tetrapharmacum: historically, a medical ointment made of four ingredients (wax, resin, pitch, and fat), but here referring to the magical/medicinal combination of a stone, plant, bird, and fish.</term>

Then in Book II, concerning 40 Animals; in Book III, concerning 44 Birds
individually; and in Book IV, again concerning 74 Fish,
and their medicinal powers:
This [editor], however, with a similarly fourfold effort,
1. published it first from a manuscript after half a millennium of years in
a most corrected form; 2. illustrated it with notes interspersed and subjoined; 3. adorned it with an introductory preface;
and 4. finally, expanded it with indexes.

Inside the central hexagonal area are the letters 'M' (left), 'V' (right), 'V' (bottom left), and 'A' (bottom right). At the absolute center is a complex vertical sigil featuring a circle topped by a cross and a crescent moon, flanked by two stylized serpents or wings. Below this central emblem is the planetary symbol for Saturn (♄) followed by another letter 'A'.</image-desc>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues from the title page of the 1638 edition of the Cyranides. The editor, Andreas Rivinus (writing under the name Rhyakinus), begins his personal introduction, setting the scene in Germany during the devastating Thirty Years' War and the accompanying outbreaks of the plague.</meta>

# THE EDITOR’S INTRODUCTORY PREFACE.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut drop-cap initial 'C' containing ornate foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>
When the horrendous plague of the Pestilence was spreading everywhere through a lamented and already sufficiently exhausted Germany, and was laying low in great numbers everywhere the bodies of wretched mortals—already worn out by so many and such great calamities of war—or rather, corpses that were still barely breathing, and when it indeed utterly spurned all medical treatment: I myself, along with others, wished to withdraw for a while from the Academy of the Lindens <note>original: "Academo Tiliarum"; this refers to the University of Leipzig, as "Lipsia" (Leipzig) was traditionally associated with the Slavic word for linden tree.</note> and sought to catch freer and purer air in my native salt-works. <note>original: "patriis Salinis"; Rivinus was born in Halle, a city famous for its salt springs.</note> For thus, with my beloved Ovid <note>original: "NASONE"; Publius Ovidius Naso, the Roman poet.</note> from Book VII of the *Metamorphoses* (the passage where a most accurate description of the pestilence is provided, along with its physical as well as metaphysical causes), I publicly proclaimed:

> While the evil seemed mortal, and the harmful
> Cause of such a great slaughter lay hidden, it was fought with art. <note>original: "arte"; referring to the medical arts or skill.</note>
> The destruction surpassed the aid, which lay defeated.
> Now the healer is gone, and the savage slaughter
> Breaks out upon the healers themselves; their arts fail their authors. <note>This passage is from Ovid's Metamorphoses, Book VII, lines 523–527, describing the Plague of Aegina.</note>

Therefore, after God the Averter, <term>Averruncus: a Roman deity invoked to "avert" or turn away calamity and disease.</term> no remedy was believed to be left to us except in headlong flight, according to the proverb of the Italians: **"Nothing was ever done well in haste, except fleeing the plague."** <note>original: "Che non ſi fece mai nulla benè in frettâ, ſalvo che il fuggirè la peſte."</note> And yet this very contemplation—the fact that Libitina <term>Libitina: the Roman goddess of funerals and burial, often used as a metaphor for Death itself.</term> spared not even the physicians, but that so many most celebrated practitioners, together with Sennert, <note>Daniel Sennert (1572–1637) was a world-renowned physician and professor at Wittenberg who died of the plague just a year before this book was published.</note> in that same

<meta>catchword: summer (aestate)</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the editor Andreas Rivinus describes fleeing the plague-ridden city of Leipzig for his hometown of Halle. He reflects on the helplessness of medicine against the epidemic, citing Ovid and noting the death of the famous physician Daniel Sennert.</summary>

<keywords>Pestilence, Thirty Years' War, Leipzig, Halle, Ovid, Metamorphoses, Daniel Sennert, Libitina, Averruncus, medical history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the editor’s preface. On the previous page, Andreas Rivinus described the plague's arrival in Germany and quoted Ovid on how the pestilence kills even the healers. This page begins mid-sentence, concluding the thought that even those meant to cure were being struck down.</meta>

...time, and within barely a single month—as if they were about to perform funeral rites for the spirits of the dead—they miserably collapsed from the plague, and were forced to hear that:

->**PHYSICIAN, SAVE YOURSELF!**<-
<note>original Greek: "ΣΩΣΟΝ ΙΑΤΡΕ ΣΕΑΥΤΟΝ" (Sōson iatre seauton). This is a variation of the famous biblical proverb "Physician, heal thyself" (Luke 4:23), here used mockingly to suggest the doctors could not even protect their own lives from the pestilence.</note>

Yet, so far was this from being able to withdraw or alienate me from my medical study and pursuit—to which I have been more favorably inclined since my youth, after my sacred and philosophical studies—that it instead stimulated me to press on more fervently in the future with what had been interrupted for some time. I felt this all the more as I saw fewer <term>Students</term> <note>original: "Alumnorum"; literally "foster-sons," referring here to medical students or practitioners-in-training.</note> remaining in this Faculty of Medicine. Indeed, unless every person knows how to properly take account of and give a reckoning for their own health, very many from now on must risk their lives. In these times, health must be most desperately committed to mere <term>Empirics</term> <note>original: "Empiricis"; in this period, "Empiric" was often a derogatory term for unlicensed doctors or "quacks" who practiced by trial and error without formal theoretical education.</note>, braggarts, or rather <term>False-surgeon Barbers and Bath-keepers</term> <note>original: "Pseudo-chirurgis Tonsoribus & Balneatoribus"; in the 17th century, barbers and bath-house attendants often performed minor surgeries and bloodletting, but were frequently criticized by university-trained physicians.</note>, and even to any kind of buffoons.

For if, as found in <term>Aelian</term> <note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175 – c. 235 AD), a Roman author and teacher of rhetoric who wrote in Greek. His "Varia Historia" (Various History) is a collection of anecdotes.</note> (Book II of *Various History*, Chapter 23), it was considered a credit and given as praise to a <term>Master-Philosopher</term> <note>original: "Archiphilosopho"; likely referring to Anaxagoras or a similar figure from Aelian’s anecdotes.</note> that when he was once unwell and a certain thing was prescribed by a physician, he refused to take the medicine unless that cup-bearer first explained what it was he was offering. He added this aphorism: "I have called for a **Physician**, not a **Veterinarian**," for a veterinarian would treat an ox, a mule, or even a herdsman, but a **Philosopher**—that is, one who is not entirely ignorant of the things and powers of Nature—requires a higher level of care. How much more in this unrefined age must all learned men take care, lest they fall into the hands of drunken Physicians, reckless <term>Chymicasters</term> <note>original: "Chymicastrorum"; a mocking term for fraudulent or incompetent alchemists/chemists, similar to "poetaster" for a bad poet.</note>, and Pharma...

<summary>The editor argues that because the plague has killed so many doctors, individuals must study medicine themselves to avoid falling into the hands of "quacks" and "chymicasters." He cites an ancient anecdote from Aelian to justify why a learned person should understand the nature of their own treatment.</summary>
<keywords>plague, medical education, Empirics, Aelian, Chymicasters, Physician heal thyself, health autonomy</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s preface, comparing the work of "Kiranus" (the Kiranides) to the better-known writings of Albertus Magnus. The editor is arguing that Albertus actually copied much of his magical and botanical knowledge from the ancient source he is now editing.</meta>

...that it had been written before, and afterwards thus corrupted. Who does not immediately notice, upon comparison, that *Albertus* <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a famous medieval scholar and theologian often associated with alchemy and natural magic.</note> was an imitator and follower of these works step by step <note>original Greek: "κατὰ πόδας" (kata podas); literally "at the heels," meaning following closely or imitating exactly.</note>? Since he first deals with twenty-four Herbs—no fewer than our own author—among which he lists the last seven as belonging to the seven Planets, according to the Emperor **ALEXANDER THE GREAT** (whose books are also mentioned in the old Preface of our *Kiranus*); from here he decided to recount matters concerning Stones, and finally certain Animals (among which he included some Birds and Fish).

This can be read in the *Trinum Magicum* on page 231, where Caesar Longinus, Philosopher—or rather, the most celebrated Doctor of Medicine and Arts, **Rodolphus Goclenius**, himself—did not hesitate to publish that most barbaric and quite foolish little work of *Albertus* in **Frankfurt, 1616**. We would refer more delicate readers to that work, so that they might gradually grow accustomed to our author and, as it were, develop a thick skin <note>original: "callum quendam obducant"; literally "to draw a callus over," suggesting the reader should harden their "fastidious stomach" against the strange or crude style of these ancient texts.</note> over their squeamish stomachs. 

Certainly, we could not be so discouraged by the bitter flavor of the outer rind <note>A common scholarly metaphor: the "rind" is the difficult or "barbaric" language, while the "marrow" is the valuable hidden knowledge.</note> as to discard the inner marrow. Instead, following the example of those who have frequently cited *Albertus* as a rival and almost a plagiarist of our *Kiranus*, we were moved to produce this work publicly. To this were added the clear testimonies and honorable judgments of other writers regarding our *Kiranus*—writers who, though more recent, are not to be disregarded. Indeed, I have seen how much **C. BARTHIUS**, the [most...]

<summary>The editor argues that Albertus Magnus’s works on herbs, stones, and animals were largely plagiarized from the Kiranides, and encourages readers to overlook the "barbaric" style to find the medical value within.</summary>
<keywords>Albertus Magnus, Kiranides, Plagiarism, Trinum Magicum, Rodolphus Goclenius, Planetary Herbs, Alexander the Great, Caspar Barthius</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s preface, moving from a comparison with Albertus Magnus to a list of other prestigious scholars and naturalists who have cited or relied upon the "Kiranides" (the author Kiranus). The editor is building a "chain of authority" to justify publishing this ancient and sometimes controversial text.</meta>

...had attributed to this Author (in his book *Adversaria* <note>A common title for a scholar’s collection of miscellaneous notes or "notebooks."</note> XI, chapter 16) the title of a "singular, living, and walking Library of dead Libraries" <note>original Latin: "demortuarum... Bibliothecarum Bibliotheca unica viva & ambulans"</note>; as if this man alone among Physicians—like that certain Historian formerly among the Greeks—had ceased to trifle, while the rest deserved to be called not so much Writers as Copyists <note>original: "Exſcriptores"</note>, in whose publication there would be no great reward for the labor, as there would be in this one. 

The most learned Lord **P. J. MAUSSAC** <note>Philippe Jacques de Maussac (c. 1590–1650), a French scholar known for editing ancient Greek texts.</note>, in his commentary on Harpocration’s Lexicon under the entry *Leka* <note>original Greek: εις Λέκα</note>, had seen a warning regarding this *Kiranus* that should not be disregarded. I had seen that an ancient person—whether a Translator or a Scribe—had called this book truly "golden and bejeweled." 

I had also seen that the **ORNITHOLOGIST** <note>Likely referring to Conrad Gessner (1516–1565), whose "History of Animals" was the standard reference of the age.</note> often based his descriptions on this single Author, as attested by **ULYSSE ALDROVANDI** <note>Ulisse Aldrovandi (1522–1605), a titan of Renaissance natural history who created one of the world's first massive "cabinets of curiosities."</note> in book V, chapter 16. He himself cites the *Kiranides* numerous times—indeed, more than a hundred times—in his works; (which is why we have cited Aldrovandi so often in our own Notes, as his work was almost the only one at hand at that time). This is true even though, in Book III, chapter 1, page 125, he calls the author "Obscure," elsewhere "Superstitious," and in several places "more fabulous than a Poet"; and in Book XV, page 27, and Book XIII of his *Ornithology*, page 15, he says that the author "was often accustomed to mix fables with truths," where he certainly admits that the man is not entirely devoid of truth. 

I had also seen that by **BAUDOUIN RONSSE** <note>Baudouin Ronsse (1525–1597), a Belgian physician famous for his work on scurvy and medicinal plants.</note>, a very elegant and sharp Belgian Physician, *KIRANUS* is brought forward among the authors in his medical pamphlets; indeed, Ronsse’s own *Medical Huntings* <note>original: "Venationes Medicas"</note>, written in most sweet meter, were enriched not a little by the second book of *Kiranus*. I had also seen in what high regard—namely as a "treasured heirloom" <note>original Greek: κειμήλιον (keimelion); a valuable possession or keepsake often passed down through generations.</note>...

<summary>The editor defends the "Kiranides" by citing its use by famous Renaissance naturalists like Aldrovandi and Ronsse, admitting that while the text is often "fabulous" or "superstitious," it remains an essential source of ancient knowledge. He frames the book as a "living library" that preserves lost wisdom which other writers merely copied.</summary>
<keywords>Kiranides, Ulisse Aldrovandi, Baudouin Ronsse, P. J. Maussac, natural history, ornithology, medical literature, authority, fables vs. truth</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s account of the manuscript’s provenance, tracing its ownership through several distinguished 16th and 17th-century figures to establish the text's legitimacy and value.</meta>

...great [thing], the Chief Physician <note>original: "Archiater"</note> of the Magdeburg court, **HENRICH ELLENBERGER** from Hesse, had this same [book]. The recently cited Barth counts him among the "Princes of Medicine," and in that same passage he revealed that this [work by] Kiranus lay hidden in the possession of this most great man. He, moreover, had recorded there in his own hand that he had received this manuscript codex from the most Illustrious Lady, the Lady **ELEONORA**, Landgravine of Hesse, who was descended from the lineage of the Dukes of Württemberg, in Darmstadt in the year 1609, as a singular gift. 

And I received it from the son of that same most Noble Physician—a man distinguished in both Art and War <note>original: "Arte & Marte," implying both scholarship and military service</note>—the **FAMOUS CHRISTIAN WILLIAM**. The love which he held, as if by inheritance, from his father and his elder brother **JAN HENRICH**—who was once my most missed companion through the lands of the Celts and the Belgians <note>Referring to travels through France and the Low Countries.</note>—he has continued toward me with the most kind familiarity, even amidst the clashing of arms <note>A reference to the ongoing conflicts of the Thirty Years' War.</note>, following their most bitter deaths. 

But a century ago, as was likewise inscribed, this book belonged to the Provost of S. L. <note>Likely an abbreviation for a specific church or monastery, such as St. Leonard or St. Lambert.</note>, **WALTHER VON DIELBEL**; by whose gift **DAVID DIELBEL** had possessed it in the year 1530. And in that same place, the Platonic Philosopher was bid to be seen among the Princes of Medicine, among whom this Author perhaps had been recorded. I had seen, moreover, in how many places the medicines recommended by our Writer agreed with the formulas and recipes of other medic<unclear>al men</unclear>, both ancient and modern.

Therefore, although neither 그at ridiculous cover, nor the fabulous painted images...

<summary>The editor traces the manuscript's history from 1530 to 1609, noting its ownership by German nobility and prominent physicians to prove its scholarly value despite its "ridiculous" outward appearance.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor’s justification for publishing the manuscript, acknowledging that while the text contains controversial or "superstitious" elements, its historical and medical value outweighs these concerns. It transitions from discussing the book's previous owners to the editor's process of refining the content for a Christian audience.</meta>

could have moved me from extracting the core or marrow <note>original: "enucleando vel emedullando," metaphors for the painstaking process of scholarly editing and interpretation</note> of this Author. However, there was one thing that could have delayed the press in the edition itself: that I found therein not everything to be divine (as if kept in **Socratic boxes** <note>original: "Socraticis pyxidiculis." This refers to the "Silenus boxes" mentioned by Plato—plain on the outside but containing golden images of gods within.</note>), but rather many things belonging to **Soothsayers**, **Haruspices**, and **Augurs**. <note>Diviners in ancient Rome who interpreted the will of the gods through entrails (haruspices) or the flight of birds (augurs).</note> These are, namely, the kind of things which—along with those "curious arts" and books which **ST. LUKE** records the faithful of Christ burned in the *Acts of the Apostles*, chapter 19—we too could have handed over to **Vulcan** <note>The god of fire; a poetic way of saying the books should have been burned.</note> rather than to the Printer.

Inasmuch as the text, due to its own superstition and curiosity, sometimes inclines toward Magic itself and forbidden cures; such as the one found on page 90 concerning the remains of a "conjured ass," where the author entreats the demons **Sanael**, **Seduel**, and **Castifanel**. Concerning these entities, unless I am mistaken, **JOHANNES TRITHEMIUS** <note>A famous 15th-century German abbot, magician, and lexicographer.</note> discussed something similar in his singular book dedicated to **MAXIMILIAN I**, the Pious and Wise Emperor, regarding the <term>Secundeis</term> <note>Translated as "Secondary Causes," these were the seven planetary intelligences or spirits that Trithemius believed governed the ages of the world.</note> or the intelligences and Spirits of the Planets ruling the world. A few pagan acclamations to **Bacchus** of this kind were also found on page 35. 

These specific passages have been marked by us on both sides with **Asterisks** and were deliberately passed over "with a dry foot" <note>original: "sicco pede," a Latin idiom meaning to pass over something quickly or without getting involved in the difficulties.</note>. If, however, some such things remained in the text so that the Author would not be completely mutilated, we believe they will pose little hindrance to us, who are sufficiently firm in our Christianity. Therefore, the "baby should not be thrown out with the bathwater" (as the saying goes); rather, the Writer must be somewhat excused by the custom of his age. For such was the spirit <note>original: "genius"</note> of that time, that unless he had mixed in such "curious" matters, he would not even have been read by his own fellow citizens.

<summary>The editor explains his decision to censor certain "magical" or "pagan" elements of the text (like demonic invocations) using asterisks, while arguing that the rest of the work remains valuable for Christian readers if viewed in its historical context.</summary>
<keywords>censorship, magic, Johannes Trithemius, Secondary Causes, Bacchus, superstition vs. science, editing process, Acts of the Apostles</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's introductory remarks. Having just mentioned the "superstitious" elements of the text and the influence of Johannes Trithemius, the editor now provides historical context for the author's era—the 12th-century Byzantine Empire—to explain why the work blends medicine with divination and folklore.</meta>

For he lived, as is stated on page 2, line 17, in Constantinople during the reign of Emperor **Manuel Komnenos**, the son of **John the Handsome** <note>John II Komnenos (reigned 1118–1143), known as "Kaloioannes" (John the Good/Handsome). Manuel I Komnenos reigned 1143–1180.</note>; for both of whom the end of life and empire had been predicted by Oracles. To the Father, this was foretold: *O, how you shall become food for terrible crows!* <note>original Greek: ὦ πῶς γεννήσῃ βρῶμα δεινῶν κοράκων!</note> To the son, however, it was foretold by this verse: *But the final seizure shall overtake you by the word.* <note>original Greek: Ἀλλ᾿ ὑσάτη σὲ κερδανεῖ λάβῃ λόγο.</note> **Manuel**, therefore, according to the last letters of his name (**ηλ**) which signify thirty-eight <note>In Greek numerals, eta (η) equals 8 and lambda (λ) equals 30. Manuel I Komnenos ruled for exactly 38 years.</note>, presided over the empire for that many years; but that **John the Handsome** died in a certain place which was nicknamed the "Crows' Nest." <note>John II died in the Taurus Mountains of Cilicia following a hunting accident; the editor links this to a folk-prophecy about "crows."</note> And the common **CHRONICLER** <note>term: *Chronicographus* — a writer of a chronicle or history.</note> adds in Book IV: That at that time the Greek nation was greatly devoted and addicted to **soothsayers** <note>original Greek: χρησμολόγοις (*chresmologoi*), professional collectors and interpreters of oracles.</note>, who practiced Magic under the pretext of "Natural Divination"; and from them, just as from the Oracles of old, responses were sought—as happens when true prayer is extinguished and the doctrine of the Church is corrupted.

For this reason, in the work of our Author, not everything pertains to medical practice, but some things are matters of Natural Philosophy intended for contemplation; just as the title "**Physico-Medical**," taken from page 2, line 20, indicates: that it is a *Book of Natural Virtues and Sympathies.* <note>original: *Physicalium Virtutum & Compassionum.* This refers to "sympathetic magic" or the hidden connections between natural objects.</note> Furthermore, some things appear therein which were pleasantly devised merely for the sake of holding the Reader’s attention. Just as our Author openly revealed his aim on page 3, line 14: That although he wrote these things primarily for the sake of healing the mortal body, he also included certain things for delight.

<meta>Ex</meta>

<summary>The editor explains that the author lived in 12th-century Constantinople, an era when the Byzantine court was obsessed with oracles and "natural divination." This historical context justifies the inclusion of non-medical, folkloric, or "sympathetic" elements in the text, which were intended both for natural contemplation and reader enjoyment.</summary>
<keywords>Manuel Komnenos, John II Komnenos, Byzantium, Constantinople, oracles, gematria, chresmologoi, natural philosophy, sympathetic magic</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's analysis of the *Cyranides* (or *Koiranides*). Having established the 12th-century Byzantine setting on the previous page, the editor now deconstructs the legendary origins of the text, specifically addressing the claims that it was authored by Hermes Trismegistus or a Persian king named Kiranus.</meta>

From this source, I believe, sprang the legendary narrative of the entire Preface, concerning the name of the author and of the work itself, as well as the reason for its writing. For regarding the fact that, among others on page 2, the last line, **HERMES TRISMEGISTUS**, a god known to all men and the Beginner or, so to speak, the first Receiver <note>The Latin *Exceptor* implies one who takes down a dictation or receives a revelation.</note>, is said to have received this book as a gift from the Agarenes <note>original: *Agarenis*; a medieval term for Muslims or Arabs, descendants of Hagar.</note>; I judge that this story flowed from the fact that many works were attributed to him several centuries ago. From him, even today, the Hermetic Chemical Philosophers <note>Alchemists who follow the tradition of Hermes Trismegistus.</note> delight in being named. As for the fact that they are called the *Cyranid Books* <note>original Greek: Κοιρανίδες Βίβλοι (*Koiranides Bibloi*).</note>, this was done not only patronymically <note>Named after a specific person, such as the mythical "Kiranus."</note> after the author, but also descriptively, as he himself gave the reason on page 3, the second to last line: namely, because these are like the **QUEENS** <note>The editor links the title *Koiranides* to the Greek *Koiranos*, meaning a ruler or lord, suggesting these books are the "ruling" or most important texts of their kind.</note> of other papers. However, the name **KIRANUS** is entirely fictional, used only so that a better recommendation and greater authority might be granted to this book. For although **CYRUS**, the monarch of the Persians, was "Thrice-Greatest"—from which authority, lord, and leader <note>original Greek: κύρος (*kyros*), κύριος (*kyrios*), and κοίρανος (*koiranos*)</note>, and even the royal title of the French, **SIRE**, are believed to be derived—nevertheless, no specific Prince or King of the Persians is read to have been uniquely named this way. But just as this entire book is Greek in style, so too it did not adopt a Persian name, but rather the general Greek name *Koiranos* <note>original: κοίρανος, a leader or king.</note> for itself. It cannot be denied, however, that due to the various mixing of languages, Greek as well as Germanic words are mingled in the Persian language, as is noted in the letters of Lipsius <note>Justus Lipsius (1547–1606), a renowned humanist scholar who studied the relationships between languages.</note>.

<summary>The editor argues that the name "Kiranus" and the association with Hermes Trismegistus are fictional devices intended to give the text more authority. He provides an etymological analysis of the title, linking it to Greek words for "ruling" or "king" rather than a historical Persian monarch.</summary>

<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Cyranides, Kiranus, Cyrus the Great, etymology, Hagarenes, Justus Lipsius, alchemy, linguistics</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the editor's justification for the title and classification of the *Cyranides*. On the previous page, the editor argued that the name "Kiranus" and the "Persian" origins were likely marketing tactics to give the book authority. Here, the editor explains why the author avoided the word "Magic" and warns the reader to look beneath the surface of the text.</meta>

But to better explain my thoughts on this matter, I hold this view: 
Our author—so that he might suit the spirit of the age in which 
he lived, a time when Magic no longer enjoyed a good reputation—
rightly feared to explicitly title this treatise with such 
a name. However, because it was indeed such a work (if 
"Magic" properly denotes a contemplative science through 
which the works of Nature are more clearly perceived, and 
the Persians were especially students of these arts, such that 
no one could rule among them unless he was judged a Magus, 
as **C. LONGINUS** <note>Cassius Longinus, a 3rd-century philosopher to whom various prefaces and commentaries were attributed in the Renaissance.</note> rightly asserted in the preface of the 
book cited above); therefore, he preferred to suggest the 
same thing indirectly through roundabout phrasing, which he 
could not conveniently state so bluntly. If he had called it 
a "Magical Book," no one would have deigned to look at it; 
but because he named it "Persian" and "Royal," it was 
thereafter judged by everyone to be of the highest value. 
From this, I gather it is clear enough that this little work 
perhaps hides more in its secret depths than it promises on 
its open face. It will certainly be necessary for the reader 
here to be discerning <note>original Greek: κριτικός (*kritikos*)</note> and wise, and not unskilled 
in knowing how copper coins differ from lupin seeds <note>original: *aera lupinis*; a classical idiom referring to the use of lupin seeds as "play money" in theater; it means knowing the difference between what is real and what is a valueless imitation.</note>; 
lest he receive everything indiscriminately, and embrace 
buried coals instead of treasure, or a cloud instead of 
Juno <note>A reference to the myth of Ixion, who attempted to seduce the goddess Juno but was tricked into embracing a cloud-double; it serves as a warning against being deceived by illusions.</note>. For although, as we mentioned just a moment 
ago, this "ivy" <note>original: *hedera*; ancient taverns hung ivy at their doors to advertise good wine; here, it refers to the book's attractive title or "branding."</note> was hung at the entrance of this manuscript <note>original: *manu-exarati*; hand-written.</note> 
claiming that it was truly golden and bejeweled, it is not 
however purely golden or bejeweled, but often... <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword *auratus* suggests the next page begins with "gilded," implying the book is sometimes only gold on the surface.</note>

...is only gilded and set with gems. For that gold is not everywhere refined <note>original: *obryzum*; a term for gold that has been tested and purified by fire</note>, and although the first book deals specifically with Gems, some however are discovered to be adulterated by a certain trickery <note>original: *mangonio*; referring to the dishonest practice of "faking" or beautifying goods to deceive a buyer</note>. So that here the saying of the goldsmiths truly holds: **"Little stones are little bones"** <note>original German: *Steinchen seynd Beinchen*; a rhyming proverb suggesting that what looks like a precious stone may actually be worthless material like bone or glass.</note>. One need only consult the *Mirror of Stones* by Camillus Leonardus <note>A 16th-century Italian physician who wrote on the virtues of stones.</note>, along with the *Sympathy of the Seven Metals and Seven Selected Stones for the Seven Planets* by Peter of Arles from Scadalupia, a priest of Jerusalem; as also the *Treatise on Gems* by Franciscus Ruæus of Lille <note>François de La Rue, who wrote a famous 1547 work on the gemstones mentioned in the Bible.</note>, which are listed in the penultimate chapter of the Revelation of John <note>Referring to the foundations of the New Jerusalem in Revelation 21.</note>, to which other gems were later added; there he especially teaches to which Gods, or rather Planets, those little stones were believed sacred by the ancients. More of this kind of material can be found in the works of Albertus Magnus <note>The 13th-century polymath and theologian.</note>, Cornelius Agrippa <note>The famous Renaissance occultist and author of *Three Books of Occult Philosophy*.</note>, Girolamo Cardano <note>A 16th-century Italian polymath who wrote on nature and subtleties.</note>, Petrus Messia <note>Pedro Mexía, a Spanish humanist.</note>, and Anselmus de Boodt <note>A Flemish mineralogist and physician.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the editor’s discussion of ancient authorities on the medicinal and magical use of gemstones. The previous page ended while discussing the Roman Emperor Caracalla’s habit of wearing amulets.</meta>

...the Emperor **Caracalla** used to hang such trinkets <note>original: *reculas*; referring to small, cheap ornaments or charms</note> on himself, as we know. Just as **Galen** <note>The most influential Greek physician of the Roman Empire.</note> himself is read to have condemned and rejected "all the foolish transformations of amulets as having no medical basis" <note>original Greek: *πάσας τῶν περιαπτῶν ληρώσεις μεταμορφώσεις, καὶ οὐδένα λόγον ἰατρικὸν ἐχούσας*</note>. Nevertheless, we accept the distinction and warning proposed by that same man in Book 10 of *On Simple Medicines*: namely, that one should trust in <term>periapta</term> <note>From the Greek *periapton*, meaning something "hung around" the neck; an amulet.</note> only so far as their physical substance provides help, and not in the spoken incantations of Magicians.

Therefore, we shall by no means deny to the **Students of Nature** the wonderful and even miraculous **Virtues of Gems**, and their most effective and hidden properties. These undoubtedly descend from the peculiar influence and power of the **Planets** and celestial stars, and from a superior balancing of the **Elements** and qualities within the subtler and purer matter of Gems. There is evident proof of this in the fact that **India** and **Ethiopia** export the most precious stones; because there, due to the proximity of the rising and midday Sun, the matter of the stones is better and more strongly "cooked" <note>original: *percoqvatur*; the alchemical and natural philosophical belief that the sun's heat "ripened" minerals into precious gems over time.</note> than in the western lands or our own northern <note>original: *Arctois*</note> regions. 

This is exactly what **Velcurio** <note>Johannes Velcurio, a 16th-century philosopher known for his commentaries on Aristotle’s Physics.</note> teaches in Book 3, chapter 15 of his *Commentaries on Physics*, along with other Physicists. I believe it is for this same purpose that our Author attributes fiery powers to Gems, and has therefore assigned them to one or another Planet—as to **Jupiter** (page 18), **Saturn** (pages 37 and 28), **Venus** (pages 23, 43, and 49), **Mercury** (page 22), **Mars** and the **Moon** (page 40, etc.). Thus, within the fourfold system of the Elements, he has categorized the plants of the earth, the fish of the water, and the birds of the [air]...

<meta>This page continues the editor’s scholarly introduction to the *Kyranides*, a famous ancient text on natural magic. ...of the air (as found on page 6, line 20) with the fiery **Virtues of Gemstones**. It was believable that he looked toward this end when, on page 3, line 23, he says:

> Therefore, dividing the whole composition into three *Kiranides* <note>The *Kyranides* (or *Cyranides*) is an ancient Greek compendium of lore regarding the "sympathies" and "antipathies" of nature, used for medicine and magic.</note>, I have set it forth through the *Element*.

It appears as though he proceeds according to the four principles—heat, moisture, earth, and air—from which all bodies are composed, and by whose mixtures in a natural balance <note>original: *temperatura*; the "temperament" or proportion of qualities in a body.</note> the qualities of all animals are shaped according to their kinds; and not <term>according to the elements</term> <note>original Greek: *κατὰ στοιχεῖα* (*kata stoicheia*); in Greek, the word "elements" can mean both the physical components of the world (earth, air, fire, water) and the letters of the alphabet.</note>, which is to say, according to the physical Elements and the order of the letters. However, the sequence of the books themselves and the alphabetical treatment of the subject matter reveal more than enough that our Kyranus <note>The legendary author to whom the work is attributed.</note> followed the latter method primarily, without entirely excluding the former. It is well known that this Author was so carried away by this single principle that he attributed such great power to plants, birds, fish, and stones almost entirely because they shared the same initial letter, and for that reason he joined them together in a group of four.

Consequently, we have also given the chapters of Book I the title of **Elements**, just as we found it written in the margin of the manuscript. Moreover, as to why he only lists *Three Kiranides* in this passage, even though four books are found in the manuscript, I believe it should be reconciled by saying that the first book is either "ancient" <note>original Greek: *ἀρχαϊκὸν* (*archaïkon*)</note> or belongs specifically to **HARPOCRATION** <note>Valerius Harpocration, an author associated with Hermetic and magical texts to whom parts of this work are often attributed.</note>, or was at least compiled jointly from him and **KYRANUS**, as is evident from page 7, the second to last and last lines, as well as page 70. For he always treats Birds, Fish, and other things in <term>fourfold</term> <note>original: *quadrifariam*; refers to the text's unique structure where one plant, one bird, one fish, and one stone are grouped together under each letter of the alphabet.</note> sets. But the following...

<summary>The editor explains that the Kyranides is organized alphabetically rather than by physical elements, grouping four different natural objects (plant, bird, fish, stone) under each letter. He also addresses why the text mentions three books while the manuscript contains four, suggesting a shared authorship between Kyranus and Harpocration.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the editor’s introduction, specifically moving from the internal structure of the work to the division of the books and the identity of the Latin translator. There appear to be three books of *Kyranus* alone, as is evident from the same **page 70.** These treat individually of **Animals** (the first, which is our second), of **Birds** (the second), and of **Fish** (the third, but in our arrangement the fourth and last). In these two latter books, not a few things recur which were already present in the first book, having been excerpted from them, as it seems. On the other hand, if anything is not repeated in those later books, it is ascribed to **Harpocrates** <note>Referring to Harpocrates of Alexandria, a legendary figure in hermetic and alchemical literature often cited alongside Kyranus.</note>, or to the earlier **Archaic** book, as seen on **page 30.**

Regarding **THE INTERPRETER** <note>The translator who first rendered the Greek text into Latin.</note>, although I had believed him to be hidden in the *Manuscript* under the letters **R. A. P. A.** placed after the title of the old Preface, nevertheless, because the illustrious men **Aldrovandus** <note>Ulisse Aldrovandi (1522–1605), a renowned Italian naturalist.</note> and **Barthius** <note>Caspar Barthius (1587–1658), a German philologist and scholar.</note> assert that he was called **GERARD OF CREMONA** <note>A famous 12th-century translator of scientific works from Arabic and Greek into Latin.</note>, we freely support their testimony. That he was certainly Italian is made manifest in very many places, as we see that he fashioned this translation using frequent Italian idioms and a Latin that is not always standard. He himself also confesses at the beginning of **page 2** that there are "certain harsh and broken things therein" <note>original: *aspera quædam... & prærupta*</note>; yet he wishes the fault to be imputed not to himself, but to the old Greek version, which was taken from the most ancient notes, titles, or shorthand. He does not deny, nonetheless, that the words of his translation were produced from a "barbaric sterility" <note>A self-deprecating reference to the plain or "unpolished" Medieval Latin used in technical translations.</note>, while he looked solely toward a useful meaning and a fruitful understanding of the subject matter. Whatever the case may be, know, *Kind Reader*, that I have unearthed these things at least for the sake of calling my mind back from the contemplation of such a dreadful time <note>Likely a reference to the social or political upheavals (such as war or plague) during which the editor was working.</note>; for the rest, I have organized all things—even those that might be condemned in matter or words—so that those which are of some utility may be counted as a...
<meta>catchword: gain.</meta>

<meta>This dedication follows the title page of the 1638 edition of the Cyranides. Having introduced the work as a "truly golden" but "semi-barbaric" medieval translation, the editor now formally presents the volume to the medical faculty of Leipzig, appealing to their expertise to see past the crude language to the valuable knowledge within.</meta>

## To the Most Distinguished & Most Experienced
# COLLEGE OF PHYSICIANS OF LEIPZIG <note>original: "Philuræorum" — a Neo-Latin term for Leipzig, derived from the Greek "philura" (linden tree), as the city's name comes from the Slavic word for linden.</note>
### M.A.R.S. <note>These are likely the initials of the editor, potentially Modestus Agricola.</note> Dedicates this book with Perpetual Greeting.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'M' featuring a detailed landscape scene with trees and a building in the background.</image-desc>
Medicine alone, we have learned from the wisdom of Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (Pliny the Elder), author of the Natural History.</note>, commands even Emperors themselves. Thus, it is no wonder if the kings of old were not only "philosopher-kings" according to the wish of Plato, but were also practitioners of medicine: lest they be forced to always see and hear through the eyes and ears of others, and thereby submit and entrust themselves and all their affairs to the judgment of others. Although this practice fell into disuse in later times, you Physicians—not without right—still claim that kingdom for yourselves, so that you may prescribe for all, and even completely proscribe <note>A play on words in Latin: "præscribatis" (to prescribe/order) and "proscribatis" (to outlaw/banish).</note> some who are especially disobedient. Therefore, those who would speak to you must bring words that are **either most pleasant or as few as possible** <note>original: "ἢ ἥδιστα ἢ ἥκιστα" — a Greek proverb often attributed to Solon, advising how to speak to rulers.</note>, just as they would when addressing Kings and Princes: lest your cares and busy occupations be disturbed by royal-level seriousness, or your "dictatorial" ears be offended. Regarding "pleasantness," I admit I doubt whether you will find much of it in this present treatise which I offer for your friendly assessment—for while it is of royal quality, it is still dressed in a barbaric garment <note>The editor refers again to the rough, "un-Ciceronian" Latin of the medieval translation.</note>. However, concerning "brevity," I hope that a mere glance at this little book, and especially this very page, will allow you to be as unconcerned as I am.

To this end, I appeal to you, Distinguished Gentlemen, by making only two protests. First, that you do not allow yourselves to be alienated by the unpolished Latin of this writing, which teems with the spirit of the time in which it was translated; for even Galen, our Father <note>Galen of Pergamon, the most influential physician of antiquity.</note>, once excused that same vice in his own mother tongue in an entire book which we still lack today. Certainly, it was a matter of religious scruple to change anything in a translation when the original archetype can be found nowhere else. Second, do not permit the superstitious curiosities or the "Ennian dross" <note>original: "scoriæ è foris Ennianæ" — a reference to the poet Virgil, who famously said he read the "clumsy" poetry of Ennius to find gold in the dung heap.</note> scattered throughout to corrupt the **Virgilian gold** that is to be read therein, nor let them pervert your judgment. You might also consider that saying of Pliny: *That no book is so small or so bad that it does not contain something of value;* let that suffice. Fare ye well.

<summary>In this dedication, the editor presents the text to the medical faculty of Leipzig. He acknowledges the "barbaric" (medieval) quality of the Latin translation but argues that, like gold found in dross, the medical and natural secrets it contains are of royal value.</summary>
<keywords>Leipzig, medical faculty, Pliny, Plato, Galen, dedication, Ennius, Virgil, Latinity, medical authority</keywords>

# DEDICATORY PREFACE
## OF THE ANCIENT TRANSLATOR.

---

->*To the Most Prudent Lord and Master*<-

->RA. PA. <note>The identity of "RA. PA." remains a mystery, though some scholars suggest these initials might refer to a specific medieval patron or a religious superior.</note> THE LOWLIEST CLERK.<-

<image-desc>Ornate woodcut initial 'A' decorated with vines, leaves, and a stylized floral motif.</image-desc>
I admire and commend the shrewdness of your wisdom, which, being learned and experienced in those matters that are above the nature of our circle <note>This refers to the "sublunary" or earthly sphere in medieval cosmology.</note>, and which has already, as if through contemplation, penetrated beyond the seven heavens, yet now does not disdain to look down upon the lowliest earthly experiments. For you have asked me to translate this medicinal book from the Greek tongue into the Latin language. This was a matter easy enough to ask for, but difficult to complete. However, moved by charitable love and your kindness, I did not refuse to obey. And since there are various translations of it from the Agarenic <term>Agarenic: A medieval term for the Arabic language, derived from Hagar, the mother of Ishmael.</term> language into Greek, as you know, I have zealously and faithfully emulated in all things the Greek book which you gave me. Even the two Prologues themselves,

<summary>This page contains the opening of a medieval dedication by an anonymous translator (the "lowliest clerk"), who explains that he has translated this medical text from Greek into Latin at his master's request. He acknowledges the work's complex history, noting that it had previously been translated from Arabic into Greek.</summary>
<keywords>translation, medieval medicine, Arabic to Greek, Greek to Latin, cosmology, seven heavens, Agarenic language</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Ancient Translator's Preface" from page 1. The translator is explaining his methodology—focusing on the meaning rather than literal word-for-word accuracy—and provides the historical context for the translation, dating it to 12th-century Constantinople.</meta>

although harsh, as if drawn from the most ancient titles, I did not wish to pass them over: focusing not on the words, which reflect a barbaric sterility <note>The translator is apologizing for the unpolished or "barbarous" style of the original Greek or the source material he is working from.</note>, but on understanding the usefulness of the meaning. If, therefore, you find anything altered, let it be attributed not to bad faith or malice, but to common error. For no one is so wise as to be found without the label of ignorance.

However, I want you to know that there is a certain book among the Greeks by Alexander the Great <note>Refers to a pseudo-Alexandrian text; many magical and medical works were attributed to Alexander the Great in the Middle Ages.</note> concerning the seven herbs of the seven Planets; and another, which is called the Mystery of Thessalus <note>Thessalus of Tralles, a 1st-century physician associated with the "Methodic" school and astrological medicine.</note> to Hermes (that is, Mercury) concerning the twelve herbs attributed to the twelve signs [of the zodiac], and concerning seven other herbs for the seven other stars. If these should happen to reach my hands or yours, because they imitate celestial dignity, they are rightly placed before this work. 

This book was thus translated in Constantinople while Manuel <note>Manuel I Komnenos, Byzantine Emperor (reigned 1143–1180), known for his interest in astrology and medicine.</note> was Emperor, in the Year of the World 6677, but in the Year of Christ 1168, in the second Indiction <term>Indiction: A 15-year cycle used in the Roman and Byzantine empires for tax and dating purposes.</term>. This is the Book of Physical Virtues, sympathies <note>original: "compassionum." In this context, it refers to "sympathies" or natural affinities between plants, minerals, and the human body.</note> and cures, collected from two books: namely, from the *Experience of the Kiranides* by Kiran, King of the Persians, and from the book of Harpocration of Alexandria to his own daughter. 

The first book of Kiran began like this, just as we will place below it the great gift of the Agarenes <term>Agarenes: A medieval term for Arabs or Muslims, believed to be descendants of Hagar.</term>: receiving it was Hermes Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus (the "Thrice-Great Hermes") was the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational texts of alchemy, astrology, and magic were attributed.</note>, a god to all men...

<summary>The translator cites his sources—specifically the "Kiranides" and Harpocration—and dates the translation to 1168 AD in Constantinople during the reign of Emperor Manuel I Komnenos. He frames the work within the tradition of Hermetic and astrological medicine, linking plants to planetary and zodiacal influences.</summary>

> *Once, when I was traveling through the region of Babylonia, I found a city named Seleucia <note>original: "Seleuticam," a major Mesopotamian city on the Tigris</note>. This is the Syrian History. In the third year, it happened that I found a certain old man steeped in foreign disciplines and Greek literature. He said he was Syrian by birth, but had been made a captive and lived there. He himself, walking around the whole city with me, showed me everything point by point. It disperses tumors. *Regarding epilepsy*, take one ounce every day for a year with oxymel <note>oxymel: a traditional medical preparation made of honey and vinegar.</note>. *For apoplexy, dizziness, snake bites, and abortion:* It is also beneficial for those suffering from apoplexy <term>apoplexy: a historical term for a stroke or sudden loss of consciousness.</term> and scotoma <term>scotoma: a condition where the vision is obscured by dark spots or dizziness.</term>; for these, give two ounces. It quickly cures those bitten by a snake; it also expels the fetus, though it disturbs the stomach and the senses. When applied as a suppository, it draws out the afterbirth <note>original: "secundinam," the placenta and membranes expelled after birth.</note>, and when drunk, it stimulates the flow of urine. Three obols <note>obol: a small unit of weight, roughly 0.5 to 0.7 grams.</note> of this herb drunk for thirty days will also dissolve the spleen. It is also effectively applied as a plaster with figs for the same purpose. It may be boiled for use in warm sitz baths to cleanse the womb. It also moves the bowels moderately. Its fruit, when rubbed on or applied as a poultice, is effective against dandruff and leprosy. The juice of the whole herb, when drunk with medicinal broths, promotes the production of milk. It is also beneficial for those with liver complaints when drunk or applied with oil. Furthermore, its leaves, when applied as a poultice with wine, are good for all types of ulcers. They are also suitable when applied over areas affected by rheums <note>rheums: an old medical term for the "flow" of excess watery fluids or inflammation in the body.</note>. And let us say further: *It is useful for all things to one who uses it with caution.*

There are, however, two species of it: the first is called the *White Vine* <note>original: "Vitis alba," the Latin name for white bryony.</note>, which some call *bryony* <note>original: "bryoniam."</note>. Others call it *snake's grape*, others *celandine* <note>original: "cœlidoniam."</note>, while some call it *miletos* <note>original: "μίλητον" (miletos), a Greek term sometimes used for red pigments or specific plants in ancient botanical lists.</note>, and others...

<summary>This page details the varied medicinal applications of White Bryony, ranging from treating epilepsy and strokes to its use as an abortifacient and a remedy for skin diseases. It emphasizes the plant's potency, noting that it must be used with caution due to its strong effects on the senses and the stomach.</summary>

...however, [it is called] *philotheon* <note>original: "φιλωθεον," meaning "God-loving."</note>; others call it *archisopike* <note>original: "ἀρχισωπικὴν," likely a variant of a Greek botanical name.</note>; others *Kethedron* (meaning *kethon* or "the helper" <note>original: "κηθον auxiliantem."</note>). Its shoots, leaves, and tendrils are similar to the domestic grapevine, but they are thicker and entwine themselves around neighboring fruits, grasping them with their tendrils. It has red, grape-like fruit, which is used for depilating <term>depilating: the removal of hair from the skin.</term> or preparing hides.

The second species of this plant is called the *Black Vine*, which they also call *bryony* <note>original: "bryoniam."</note>, while others call it *chaireneion* <note>original: "χαιρενειον."</note>. It has leaves similar to *smilax* <note>original: "σμιλακι," a climbing plant like bindweed or sarsaparilla.</note>, but larger, and it also attaches itself to trees with its tendrils. Its fruits are like clusters of grapes; they are green at the start, but when ripe they are black on the outside and resemble boxwood <note>original: "buxo simile," likely referring to the color or texture of the interior flesh.</note> on the inside.

Its stalks, when they first sprout, may be cooked as a vegetable. They are diuretic <term>diuretic: a substance that promotes the production of urine.</term> and stimulate menstrual flow; they also heal the spleen. They are especially beneficial for those with spleen ailments when the root is used, and the whole herb is suitable for those suffering from epilepsy <term>epilepsy: a neurological disorder characterized by seizures or loss of consciousness.</term> and scotoma <term>scotoma: a condition where the vision is blurred or darkened by dizzy spells.</term>. Its leaf, when applied as a poultice <term>poultice: a soft, moist mass of material, typically of plant material, applied to the body to relieve soreness and inflammation.</term> with wine, also helps to remove overgrown flesh <note>original: "supereminenti carnes abscisas," referring to "proud flesh" or excessive tissue growth in a healing wound.</note> and treats dislocated or strained limbs. Furthermore, it has a similar power to the previously mentioned species.

...exultation: The juice of the cluster <note>original: *succus botri*; referring to the grape or berry cluster</note> when drunk induces great joy. Plant it in every place. For neither the solemnities of the Gods nor of mortals, who have a set limit in life, nor those things which come into life, or those which now depart, or any of those things which occur in life, can be performed without this plant. Since he does not see, nor hear; for he is without a head. Therefore, take a grape having four seeds; extract the grains with your nails, and not with the mouth, and put them in a coarse cloth, and hang it around the neck, and it will restore health. And the stone which is found in the head of a fish, tied around the neck, takes away the quartan fever.

Take therefore an eagle-stone <term>aetite<gloss>A hollow geode containing a smaller stone that rattles inside, historically believed to be found in eagles' nests and possessed of magical birthing and protective properties.</gloss></term>, and engrave an eagle upon it as well, and place under the stone the seeds of a grape and the tip of the feathers from the wing of an eagle—and if you do not have an eagle, those of a hawk—and enclosing it, carry it! For it will preserve you from all the aforementioned occasions of evil; moreover, it will provide the power of speech and friendly conversation with great men, and it contributes to many other things which I do not wish to recount in full.

## (ELEMENT II. B.)

**Borsoites** <note>original: Βορσοίτης; likely a Persian or localized name for the crow</note> is a bird, which is called a **crow** <note>original: *korōnē*, *cornix*</note>, a common animal known to all, living up to five hundred years.

**Besilla** <note>The symbol following this word is a circle with a dot, an alchemical symbol for the Sun or Gold.</note> is a precious stone, which is also called **all-life** <note>original: πάνζωον (*panzōon*), *omnivita*</note>. **Bissas** is a marine crab, which is called a **crayfish** <term>carabus<gloss>A type of spiny lobster or large crustacean.</gloss></term>; it is called *bissas* because of its likeness and color to **red tiles** <note>original: βησάλων (*bēsalōn*); referring to "bessales," a type of Roman brick or tile</note>.

**Sinthi**, that is, the herb **Savin** <term>Sabina<gloss>*Juniperus sabina*, a species of juniper used in ancient medicine, often with caution due to its potency.</gloss></term>, is a herb known to all.

*For those suffering from difficulty breathing, those who cannot breathe while lying down, and asthmatics:*
Make this electuary <term>electuarium<gloss>A medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup.</gloss></term>: 1 ounce <note>original symbol: ℥ i.</note> of Savin, 4 ounces of butter, 3 ounces of honey. Mix, and give to the patient on an empty stomach.

*For every infirmity of the eye:* Crayfish hung from the neck heal infirmities. * * * * * * *

The bird, moreover, has this nature: for if the female dies, the male does not join with another, and the female does the same. *For the love of a husband and his wife:* If the man carries the heart of the male [crow], and the wife the heart of the female crow, they will agree with each other for the whole time of their life, and this miracle is certain. *For those with difficulty breathing, epileptics, and those with kidney ailments:* Take, I say, a **beryl** stone, and carve upon it a crow, and beneath its feet a crayfish; enclose also a little Savin, and a little of the heart of the bird...

### (ELEMENT III. G.)

**Glykysidē** <note>Greek: Γλυκυσίδη</note>, *a certain herb, that is, the Peony.*
**Glaux** <note>Greek: Γλαὺξ</note>, *a bird.* **Gnanthius**, *a stone.*
*The **Glaucus**, a sea fish known to all.*

**Glykiside** is the herb **Peony**, so named after its discoverer **Paeon** <note>In Greek mythology, Paeon was the physician of the gods who healed Hades and Ares; the peony was believed to have divine healing origins.</note>. It has fruit like the resinous pits <term>styrax<gloss>A fragrant resin; here used to describe the appearance or texture of the seed pods.</gloss></term> of an almond. Some of its seeds are open, while others are closed. 

The **Glaux** <note>Often identified as the Little Owl (*Athene noctua*).</note> is a bird attributed to **Pallas** <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom, whose symbol was the owl.</note>; it has a feathered crown upon its head and large eyes like a **night-raven** <term>nycticorax<gloss>Literally "night-raven," a term used in ancient texts for various nocturnal birds, including owls.</gloss></term>, and it lives in the fields. 

The **Gnathius** stone is hard like a **millstone** <note>original: μυλίας (*mylias*)</note>, and is similar to a garnet. 

The **Glaucus** is a sea fish known to all. 

There are two species of this herb: the female and the male. *In order that a woman might conceive:* If a woman does not retain the male seed and wishes to conceive, let her be girded with the closed seeds of this divine herb, binding them in a linen cloth fashioned with seven colors...

## ( ELEMENTUM IV. Δ. )

*The **Dragonwort** herb.*
*The **Woodpecker** bird <note>original: *Dendrocolaptes*, from the Greek for "tree-pecker."</note>, that is, the tree-cutter or Woodpecker.*
*The **Dragon** fish. The **Dendritis** stone.*

**Dragonwort** <note>original: *Draconthea*; likely the Dragon Arum (*Dracunculus vulgaris*), known for its serpent-like stalks.</note> is an herb that has seeds like the eyes of a dragon; its leaves are broad, and it is entirely harmful. The **Woodpecker** is a bird known to everyone; it is about the size of a quail <note>original: *ortyx* and *coturnix*, both words for quail.</note> or a rail. It drills into oaks, olives, and nut trees so that it may make its nest in the middle of the wood. The **Sea Dragon** <note>original: *Draco marinus*; often identifying the venomous Weever fish.</note> is a fish without scales. After it has grown quite large and wishes to commit excessive violence, the clouds of heaven seize it into the air and cast it onto the mountains, tearing it limb from limb so that it exists no longer.

*To ensure no one is harmed:* It is forked, like a horse's tail, and two fingers in length. Store this by placing it in oil. When worn as an amulet, it protects everyone from fascination <term>fascinatione<gloss>The "evil eye" or a malevolent glance believed to cause harm or bad luck.</gloss></term> and sick-

[That power] which is in the heavens will calm the terrible waves of the sea, drive away all demons, and make the bearer appear good to all people. We shall now turn to another counsel. ### ( ELEMENT 5. E. )

* **Rocket** <note>original: *Εὔζωμον* (Euzomon) / *Eruca*; known today as arugula.</note> is a herb.*
* **Nightingale** <note>original: *Ευlui* (likely *Eluph* or *Aëdon*) / *philomela*; a bird famous for its night song.</note> is a bird.*
* **Sea Urchin** <note>original: *Ἐχῖνος* (Echinos) / *ericius marinus*; literally "sea-hedgehog."</note> is a fish.*
* **Oenanthus** <note>original: *Enanthus*; a stone named for the color of vine-blossoms.</note> is a stone.*

**Rocket**, that is, the herb *Eruca*, is eaten among garden vegetables and is known to everyone. The **Sea Urchin**, that is, the "sea-hedgehog," is likewise known to everyone. The **Nightingale** bird is the *aëdon* or *philomela*, also called the little nightingale, and is well known. The **Oenanthus** stone is of every color; however, it is dedicated to **Venus** <note>The Roman goddess of love, beauty, and fertility.</note> because it is multi-colored. **Rocket** therefore produces heat, since there is an error among many [people], and they do not [re]cognize...

It is even better if the fat of the Moray eel is placed beneath it. For this is divine.

# THE KIRANIDES

When worn, it grants the grace of prudence and virtue. 

**Saturn** <note>original: *Saturnius*; referring to the god or the planetary power of Saturn.</note> himself wore it, and **Phaos** <note>original: *Phaos*; the Greek word for "light," here personified as a celestial being.</note> in the heavens and on the earth possesses gold and silver; it destroys infirmity and cruel poverty, and the evil bonds of necessity. It is not the good **Oraphos** <note>original: *Oraphos*; likely a corruption of the name Horapollon or Horus, figures of ancient wisdom in this tradition.</note> who lets go, but he is one who compels all the earth and the starry heaven, striking men with the step of fortune, binding them in their paths. 

A certain Saturnian King, **Kiranis**, in a single center with incorruptible and unfailing changes, is the **Roller** <note>original: *Κυλινδράτης* (Kylindratēs); one who revolves or turns, likely referring to the movement of the celestial spheres.</note>, revolving in a circle, turning all things from the **east** <note>original: *eoo*.</note> to the west, holding and moving all foreign things within the seven **arctic**—that is, **northern**—regions. 

O **soul** <note>original: *Anima*.</note>, be content and sufficient in those things you do as time runs out; and although you are in the state after **conception** <note>original: *prægnationem*; in this mystical context, it refers to the soul's descent into a physical body.</note>, you will soon recognize God in the air and the clouds, even though you suffer infirmity upon the earth, where you are tormented as an inhabitant. O blessed immortal soul, existing from that moment on, cease from bodily labors; although you are fastened to these things, seek instead the heavenly realm—or from whence comes the water? If you wish to learn, I will explain all things you command. **Heaven** is water and fire; the stars are visible—

### THE KIRANIDES

...[ineffab]le, and there is darkness over those divine and inexpressible things themselves. And there is a sphere revolving from the east to the west. It sets indeed within the Sphere while the winds keep watch, moving all things from the sea. It brings back the lightning and dissolves the air; it strikes with thunder and flashes forth sparkling fire; it gives sweet rains, draws roots out of the earth, and provides all food useful for the belly. 

And the divine power, which brings this forth, also hides all things which are among those born of the earth: the roots of herbs which are found outside among the diverse generations of men, and for the sake of the quadrupeds, reptiles, and all winged creatures that make their homes in nests and dens. Beyond all things, these were given for the sake of men, all well-colored and green. 

Let what I have foretold suffice for you; I speak in prose and show you the soul’s divinations <note>original: *divinationes*; here referring to the mystical insights or prophetic qualities of the soul.</note> concerning all virtues. When these are revealed, I shall speak of others. For I have spoken of things to come in meter or verse concerning foreknowledge, and likewise of the remaining animals. 

*Regarding the foreknowledge of those things which are in heaven and on earth, and what he who possesses them has in his soul, and whatever happens in regions and cities, and all future events for men:*

There is an animal flying in the air which is called the **epops** <note>original: *ἔποψ*; the Greek name for the hoopoe bird.</note>, that is, the **seven-colored hoopoe**, which has a crown <note>original: *regnum*; literally "kingdom" or "royalty," referring to the bird's distinctive crest of feathers.</note> on its head two fingers high, which it opens and contracts.

### KIRANIDES

It itself has four colors, so that it is aligned with the four seasons of the year. This bird is called the **cucubas** <note>original: *cucubas*; a Latinized name for the hoopoe bird.</note>, just as it is written about in the earlier book, which is called the **Archaicus** <note>original: *Archaicus*; likely referring to a "primitive" or "ancient" source text of lore.</note>. Moreover, this animal is sacred. 

Take this bird, therefore, and tear it apart, and while it is still palpitating [beating], swallow its heart, facing the Sun, at the start of the first or eighth hour of Saturday <note>original: *diei Saturni*; literally "the day of Saturn."</note>, while the Moon is in the east, and drink after it the milk of a black cow with a little honey as a potion. 

See that you swallow the heart healthy and whole, and you will have foreknowledge of what is in heaven and what is on earth, and whatever anyone has in their soul, or whatever happens in various regions and cities; and of things to come for all people. 

The composition of the honey shall be this: 1 cup <note>original: *cotyla*; an ancient Greek measure of volume, approximately half a pint.</note> of honey, 2 ounces <note>The symbol ℥ stands for ounce (*uncia*).</note> of crushed living lodestone <note>original: *magnetis viventis*; a naturally magnetic stone.</note>, and 7 ounces of the sprouts of the Sea Holly herb <note>original: *Eryngii*; Eryngium, often used in ancient medicine for various ailments.</note>. Mix these together with the honey. 

You should also have another lodestone, on which a **cucubas**—that is, a **hoopoe** <note>original: *upupa*; the common Latin name for the bird.</note>—is carved; dip the whole of it into the honey mixture. Whenever you wish to foreknow something, taste it [the mixture] with one finger, and place the carved lodestone around your neck, and you will foreknow whatever you wish. 

If, however, you also add the liver and heart of the hoopoe <note>original: *cucufæ*; a variation of *cucubas*.</note> to the preparation, it will be better, because it will make your memory more powerful. So that the heart of the one eating does not—

*If you therefore have foreknowledge, tell me: is the soul mortal or immortal? He, answering, said: Listen, my son! Many false sophists err in their understanding of the immortal soul, but she discerns herself by her own nature. How is it that the body rests in bed while the soul moves in its own place—that is, in the air, from whence we first received that soul—and sees things that are happening in other regions <note>original: *climatibus*; literally "climates," referring to geographical zones or latitudes.</note>? Often, just as a mother feels for her son, the soul is anxious for the body in which she dwells; she foresees in advance and divines future things, whether good or bad, which is called a dream <note>original: *somnium*.</note>. Then she returns to the body and her own dwelling, and upon waking <note>original: *surgens*; literally "rising."</note>, the person examines these things. From this it should be clear to you that the soul is immortal and incorporeal.*

## THE EIGHTH ELEMENT: Θ (THETA)

Thyrsos <note>original: Θύρσος</note>, the herb of Dionysus. The Thyr bird. 
The Thyrsites stone. The Thynnus <note>original: Θύννος</note>, a fish.

The Thyrsitis or Thyrsos is a Dionysian herb <note>Dionysus was the Greek god of wine, fertility, and ritual madness</note> that is fruitful and useful in all things. The Thyr is a bird similar to a sea-hawk, possessing a divinely granted efficacy. The Thyrsites is a stone similar to coral. The Thynnus <term>thynnus: the Atlantic Bluefin tuna</term> is a sea fish and is eaten; it is similar to the bonito <note>original: *pelamydi*; a smaller fish in the mackerel family</note>, which is indeed very well known. 
Balkera <note>likely a corrupted or local name for a specific plant associated with the Thyrsus</note> is the plant of Dionysus. They place the thyrsus <term>thyrsus: a staff or spear tipped with a pine cone and entwined with ivy, carried by Dionysus and his followers</term> itself in the winepresses during the festivals of Bacchus <note>the Roman name for Dionysus</note>; the plant was given to the earth for the joy of mankind.

### KIRANIDES

...whose virtues I shall now describe. It is useful in wine for whichever mortals it has been granted to pour into the winepresses. This I shall also say in plain speech <note>original: *pedestri sermone*; literally "on-foot speech," meaning prose or straightforward, non-mystical language</note>: To see or to know this belongs to the earth. 

*For joy and gladness in drinking:* 
If you grind one ounce <note>original: *℥ i.*; the symbol for an ounce</note> of this plant and one ounce of the stone, and speak the name of Dionysus over it, and put it into a flask of wine from which everyone drinks, those drinking only a single cup <term>cyathum: a small measure or ladle-cup used for serving wine</term> will be dissolved <note>original: *dissolventur*; suggests a loosening of the limbs or spirit, being overwhelmed by relaxation or mirth</note> as if drunk, and giving thanks they will say: "You have made us glad, O Lord." 

*How may friends drink and depart rejoicing and glad?*
If, however, you place the right eye of a tuna <note>original: *thirmæ (L. thynni)*; the text corrects the word to thynnus, the Atlantic Bluefin tuna</note> or an *anthimia* <note>possibly a type of fish or a floral preparation</note> into the wine, the friends reclining at the table are dissolved into joy without even drinking, and they will give thanks like those rejoicing.

*For discord at a banquet:* 
If, however, you cut the wing or the tip of a feather of the Thyr bird with a sword made entirely of iron, and put it into an amphora <term>amphora: a large two-handled jar used for storage, especially of wine</term> of wine, speaking the Dionysian name over it, you can cause those reclining to be scattered whenever you wish, so that they shed blood and beat one another; and so it happens. 

*That you may be without drunkenness, and for all favor and health:* 
On a Thyrsites stone, carve the Thyr bird and Dionysus holding the bird; then enclose the root of the herb beneath the stone and carry it. You will be without drunkenness, and you will have favor from everyone.

## (ELEMENTUM IX. I.)
<note>Element 9 corresponds to the Greek letter Iota (Ι)</note>

*Itea* <note>original: *Ἰτέα*</note> that is, *Willow, a fruitless tree*.
*Jasper, a green stone*.
*Iktin* <note>original: *Ἰκτὶν*</note>, *a bird, that is, a kite*. *Ioulis* <note>original: *Ἰουλὶς*</note> *a fish*.

The Willow is a fruitless tree. Jasper is a well-known stone. The Kite is a bird known to everyone. The Julis <note>likely the Rainbow Wrasse (Coris julis)</note> is a small, variegated sea fish that is easily found.

I say, grind the green leaves of the Willow with a little salt, and place them over a painful spleen, and the pain will cease immediately. If, however, you wish for the spleen to be "melted" <note>likely meaning to reduce an enlarged spleen, which was common in malarial regions</note>, boil the bark with oxymel <term>oxymel: a medicinal mixture of honey and wine vinegar</term>, and give two spoonfuls to the patient while they are fasting; or else: boil it until reduced to a third [of its volume], and you shall give it to each according to their own strength. 

In the jasper, however, you should carve a kite tearing a serpent apart, and beneath the stone, place a [small] stone found in the head of a kite, and seal it up <note>this refers to setting the stone in a ring or locket so the "head-stone" is enclosed underneath</note>. Give it to be worn on the chest, for it will drive away all pain of the stomach, and it will provide an appetite for eating and good digestion. For it has other virtues as well; let it simply be worn on the chest.

## ( ELEMENTUM X. K. )
<note>Element 10 corresponds to the Greek letter Kappa (Κ)</note>

*The Kynædius <note>original: *Κυναίδιος*; a name likely derived from the Greek for "lewd" or "lustful," reflecting the items' use in love magic</note> herb, stone, bird, and fish.*

The Kynædius is a herb which is also called *peristereon* <note>original: *περιστερεὼν*; usually identified as Vervain</note> or "the hundred heads of the courtesan Aphrodite" <note>original: *meretricis centum capita ἀφροδίτης*</note>. The Kynædius bird is that which is called the Iynx <note>the Wryneck bird (*Jynx torquilla*), which can twist its neck 180 degrees</note>, just as Kiranus says in a verse from Theocritus, for the sake of an example:

> "Iynx, draw that man of mine to my house!"
> (*Per te, Iynx, meus ille demum vir quæso trahatur!*) <note>This is a famous refrain from Theocritus' *Idyll 2*, where a woman named Simaetha performs a love spell</note>

Some, however, call it the "yoke-bird" <note>original: *jugium alitem*</note>. The Kynædius is a winged creature that eats like a quail, twisting its neck back, as Harpocration says. This bird has three stones around its neck and a long tongue; it is frequently associated with Venus <note>the goddess of love</note>. The Kynædius is also a sea fish, about six fingers in length. It has a broad head like a "uletinum" <note>possibly referring to a small owl or a specific vessel shape</note>. It is a small, round fish with a translucent body, so that its spine appears through the body as if through a mirror. It is abundant in the coastal waters of Syria, Palestine, and Libyan Syria. This fish has two stones in its head which have their own specific powers, as we shall describe below. There is also another stone in the third vertebra <term>spondyli: the joints or vertebrae of the spine</term> or node of the back toward the tail, which is extremely powerful and is much sought after
