# KIRANIDES

The herb **Nekia** is the *florinus*, that is, **mullein** <note>original: tapsus barbatus; commonly known as Great Mullein, a plant with velvety leaves</note>. There are seven species of this herb; it differs in its leaves, which rise one cubit <note>original: brachium; literally "one arm's length"</note> above the ground. They burn the leaves of this plant in lamps as wicks. They do this because these leaves are used in **necromancies** <note>original: nekyikais manteiais; divination involving the spirits of the dead</note> performed in a basin, a practice that continues to this day. For this reason, the herb is called *nekya*, for *nekys* means a "dead person" <note>original: νέκυς</note>.

The **Duck** <note>original: Νῆσσα (Nēssa) / anas</note> is a bird that swims in the waves and is about the size of a hen.

The **Naukrátēs** <note>original: Ναυκράτης; literally "ship-ruler"</note> is a sea fish known as the **remora** <term>remora: also called the suckerfish; in antiquity, it was believed to have the supernatural power to stop ships in their tracks by clinging to the hull</term>, which means "holding a ship." If this fish clings to a moving ship, it will not allow it to move unless the fish is broken apart, or the mast <note>original: arbor; literally "tree," referring to the ship's mast</note> is forced out of its place.

**For those suffering from gout:** This fish, boiled whole in oil until it reaches the thickness of wax, and the oil then strained and boiled again to the consistency of a wax-salve, cures those with gout when applied as a plaster.

**Nemesitis** is a stone taken from the altar of the Goddess Nemesis of the Athenians. It is an excellent stone. Let the image of **Nemesis** be engraved upon this stone, depicted with feet and standing upon a wheel. Her appearance should be like that of a Virgin, holding in her left hand a *pechys* <note>original: pirhin; likely a corruption of the Greek 'pechys', a cubit or measuring rod</note>—that is, a forearm or measure—and in her right hand a Rod, while with her foot she tramples an ox. Beneath the stone, enclose an **acronian stone** <note>possibly a type of dark agate or a "top-stone"</note> and a small piece of **mulberry** <note>original: morex; referencing the mulberry tree discussed on the previous page</note>.

<summary>This page describes the magical and medicinal properties of mullein (associated with necromancy), the remora fish (used to cure gout), and a protective amulet featuring the goddess Nemesis.</summary>
<keywords>Mullein, Necromancy, Duck, Remora, Gout, Nemesis, Amulet, Ancient Medicine, Iconography</keywords>

Therefore, if you show this finger <note>referring to the finger wearing the consecrated ring described previously</note> to one possessed by a demon, the demon will immediately confess its presence and flee. It also works against demonic apparitions and dreams, as well as the terror of infants and fears in the night. However, the wearer must abstain from all contaminated and impure things. For this ring, when worn, indicates the length of one's years of life, and the quality and location of one's death.

If you sew a small piece of the bones of the **Echeneis** <term>Echeneis: also known as the remora or suckerfish; ancient lore claimed its name meant "ship-holder" because it could stop a vessel's progress</term> into horse leather and keep it with you, and then board a ship, the ship will not move in the water despite the sails, unless that which was placed is removed, or you leave the ship. For the foreknowledge of life and death, as I said a little before, you must use the "pre-tasting" or "fore-knowledge" of a **hawk** <note>original Latin: hieracis / accipitris</note>.

**Xiphion** the herb. **Xiphion** the stone. **Xiphion** the bird.
The **Xiphias** <note>Greek: Ξιφίας</note> is a fish known to everyone; that is, the **swordfish** <note>original Latin: spatha or gladius; both words for "sword"</note>.

**Xiphion**—that is, the **gladiolus** <note>literally "little sword"</note>—is a herb that is abundant in every land. It has leaves similar to grain, but longer. It rises upright from the earth about one cubit <note>original: brachium; literally "one arm's length"</note> high. It consists of a single stalk and has a wax-colored flower, somewhat bluish—

### ( ELEMENTUM XV. o )

**Onothyrsis** is the herb. **Ortyx** is the bird.
**Orphos** is the fish. **Onychites** is the stone known to all.

**Onothyris** or **onothyrsis** is the herb; some call it *Onomalache* <note>original: "Ὀνομαλάχην"; literally "donkey's mallow"</note>, that is, donkey's mallow. This is the rose from which they weave crowns; it has leaves similar to domestic mallow. This is called by the Greeks <gloss>althea</gloss> <note>original: "ἀλθαία"</note>, that is, marshmallow. **Ortyx**, that is, the quail, is a bird known to all; however, its nature is not easily known, which is why even one thing about its origin is unfamiliar. For when there have been great winters in the parts of the Libyan desert, the sea casts large tunny-fish <note>tuna</note> onto the shore; these, producing worms for fourteen days, grow and become like flies, then like locusts, which, when increased in size, become the birds called quails. After these things, when the South Wind <note>original: "noto"</note> blows—that is, the southern wind—or when the Southwest Wind <note>original: "libonoto"</note> strengthens, they cross the seas into Pamphylia or Cilicia and Hibernia <note>original: "Hiberniam"; typically referring to Ireland, though here it may be a scribal error for Iberia given the Mediterranean context</note>, and consequently, when the North Wind <note>original: "borea"</note> blows, they proceed to the maritime places of the Syrian region and the rest of the land of Melanitis <note>often identified as Egypt, "The Black Land"</note>.

## ELEMENT 17 (THE LETTER P) <note>P corresponds to the Greek letter Rho (Ρ)</note>

**Raphy** <note>original: "Rhaphy"; likely a wild radish or a similar cruciferous plant</note> is a herb.
**Romphaea** is a bird; it is the **bat** <note>original Greek: "nykteris"; the Latin text glosses this as "noctua" (owl), but "nykteris" specifically refers to the bat, which fits the subsequent folk-medical uses</note>, known to all.
**Raphis** <note>original: "Rhaphis"</note> is a sea fish, having a needle-like shape in its mouth <note>The Needlefish or Garfish</note>.
**Rhinoceros** <note>original: "Rhinokerōs"</note> is a variegated stone, a horned stone from the tip of the nose of a Rhinoceros. For it is like a horn.

If you place a branch of the herb in a house, all demons will flee. Moreover, the juice of the herb and its fruit, when smeared on the eye with honey, sharpens the vision. If, however, it is applied alone, it wipes away whiteness <note>original: "albugo"; a white opacity or scar on the cornea of the eye</note>. Let it be boiled, however, until it reaches a thick consistency. Furthermore, the blood of the bat <note>original: "nycteris"</note>, if you smear it on with the juice of the herb and honey, will sharpen the vision and remove cataracts <note>original: "suffusio"; literally an "over-pouring" of fluid, historically used to describe cataracts</note>. 

Let a bat be engraved on the stone, and at its feet the **Raphis** fish (that is, the needlefish), and with the root of the herb placed under the stone: whoever carries this will drive away demons. And if you place it at someone's head without them knowing, they will not sleep. Similarly, if you cut off the head of a living bat and bind it in black skin <note>or leather</note>, and place it on the left arm <meta>The text continues on the next page, describing the effects of binding a bat's head to the arm.</meta>

## ELEMENT 18 (THE LETTER S) <note>S corresponds to the Greek letter Sigma (Σ)</note>

->**Satyrion** <note>original: "Σατύριον"</note> is a *herb*. The **Ostrich** <note>original: "Στρουθοκάμηλος"</note> is a *bird*.<-
->*Commonly known as the Ostrich.* <note>original: "Struthio gambarum vulgo"</note><-

The Satyrion herb is like a thorny plant and rises from the earth with its whole stalk <term>thyrso: a wand-like or flowering stem</term>; it is about two palms high, full of seeds, and has a pale, waxen color when in seed. The Ostrich is a well-known bird. The Salpe is a sea fish, brightly colored and well-known, and is edible. The Sapphire is dedicated to Aphrodite and is unspotted. A preparation of this herb is made as follows: *For weakened and sterile women, for sexual intercourse, for those who have watery and thin seed <note>original: "sperma"</note>, for those who cannot conceive, and those who are parched and dry. For this herb is dry and delightful, and it aids in conception:* If, before intercourse, you sprinkle it upon the male member which has been anointed with honey, the woman is weakened <note>likely meaning "overcome with desire" or "rendered receptive"</note> beyond measure. If, however, the woman herself is anointed in this way, the intensity of the act will be greatly increased: for it dries out her nature <note>referring to the "humors" or fluids thought to prevent conception</note>, brings about conception, and allows sterile women to conceive. It is prepared thus: two ounces <note>original: "℥ ij"</note> of the seeds of the female Satyrion, one ounce <note>original: "℥ I"</note> of pepper, and one ounce <note>original: "℥ I"</note> of split alum <term>Aluminis scissi: a naturally occurring astringent mineral used in ancient medicine</term>. *According to another book*, use two ounces <note>original: "℥ ij"</note> of round Styptyria <term>Styptyriæ: a Greek-derived term for alum or an astringent earth</term>.

## (ELEMENT XVIII. Σ.)

Satyrion <term>Σατύριον: an orchid species historically associated with satyrs and used as a potent aphrodisiac</term> *herb*. Ostrich <term>Στρουθοκάμηλος: literally "sparrow-camel"</term> *bird, commonly known as the Ostrich.*
Salpe *fish*. Sapphire *stone*.

The Satyrion herb is somewhat thorny and its whole stalk rises from the earth; it is two palms high, full of seed, and has a pale waxy color <note>original: "ceravinum", likely *cerinum* (wax-colored)</note> along with the seed. The Ostrich is a well-known bird. The Salpe is a sea fish, beautifully colored and well-known, and it is edible. The flawless Sapphire is attributed to Aphrodite <note>The goddess of love; stones were often "assigned" to deities to indicate their planetary or magical associations</note>. Therefore, a preparation of this herb is made in this manner: *For weak and sterile women, and for sexual intercourse, and for those who have watery and thin seed, and those who cannot conceive and are dry and parched: for this is drying and pleasant and grants conception.*

If, before intercourse, you sprinkle [this herb] over the male member which has been smeared with honey, the woman is weakened beyond measure <note>In this context, this implies being overwhelmed by the intensity of the aphrodisiac</note>. If, however, the woman is anointed in this way, there will be great intensity in the act: for it dries out the nature <note>The "nature" here refers to the environment of the womb</note> and causes conception, and enables sterile women to conceive. 

It is prepared thus: 2 ounces <note>original: "℥ ij"</note> of Satyrion seed, 1 ounce <note>original: "℥ i"</note> of pepper, and 1 ounce of split alum <term>Aluminis scissi: a fibrous form of the mineral alum, used as an astringent</term>. *According to another book, however,* use 2 ounces of round Styptyria <term>Styptyriæ rotundæ: another name for a type of alum or astringent earth</term>.

2 ounces <term>℥: the symbol for an ounce</term>: of dry aromatics, 2 ounces. Store it in a vessel and hand it over to no one. The preparation or composition of dry aromatics is done thus: 4 ounces of Costus <term>a ginger-like root used in ancient perfumes</term>; 1/2 ounce <note>original: "℥ s.", where "s." stands for "semis" or half</note> of Spikenard (in another book, 3 ounces); 3 ounces of Carpobalsamum <term>the fruit of the balsam tree</term>; 1/2 ounce of Amomum (elsewhere 5 ounces); 2 ounces of Cloves; 2 and a half ounces of Cassia; 5 ounces of Storax <term>a fragrant resin</term>; 1 and a half ounces of Opobalsamum <term>the sap or "juice" of the balsam tree</term>; 4 ounces of "de-clawed" Roses <note>original: "Rosæ exungulatæ", referring to rose petals with the white, bitter base removed</note>; 1/2 ounce and 2 <unclear>grains</unclear> of Musk. Grind the dry ingredients very finely and sieve them diligently. Then, grind the storax separately for a longer time; from here, with the balsam and with the ground roses, mix everything together and make into lozenges <term>trochiscos: small medicinal discs or tablets</term>. Afterward, fumigate and dry them in a censer <note>original: "θυίῳ" (thyiō), a Greek term for an incense burner</note> of musk wood, storax, mastic, ostrich claws, and costus. Once these are dried, store them in a glass vial, and when needed for conception, grind 2 lozenges. And this is the composition of the dry aromatic.

The Ostrich <term>Struthiocamelus</term> bird has a substance <note>original: "echidirum"</note> which is called "dry incense"; this, ground and given in a drink secretly, *prepares love and lust. A stone from the stomach <note>original: "echino .i. ventriculo"</note> of the ostrich <note>original: "struthi gambari", likely a linguistic corruption or specific anatomical reference to the bird</note> ground with one grain of Satyrion and given in food or drink secretly, causes great intensity <note>referring to sexual vigor or erection</note>, especially for those who are unable to lie with a partner. Furthermore, the stone itself, when merely hung from the neck, creates good digestion and an intensity for intercourse.*

The Salpe <note>A species of sea bream</note> fish, however: a stone from its right side, when carried, [causes] fear...

### (ELEMENT 19. T.)

*Clover is a well-known herb.*
**Peacock** <note>original Greek: "Ταώς" (Taōs)</note>, that is, the peacock bird, is delightful and acceptable, and known to everyone.
**Sea-Turtle** <note>original Greek: "Τρυχών" (Trychōn)</note>, that is, the turtle, is a sea fish known to all, whose good and bad qualities are known.
The **Taitis** stone is most beautiful, just like the peacock, and is similar to it.
Engrave, therefore, upon the stone a peacock holding a sea-turtle fish, and beneath the stone place the voice of the peacock—that is, the word "**ajo**" <note>the phonetic representation of the peacock's cry, used here as a magical word</note>—and place the small roots <note>referring to the roots of the Clover mentioned at the start of the section</note> underneath. *For this is a great marvel when worn, bringing victory, peace, stability, and every kind of profit; so that the Divine itself may rejoice in it.*

## (ELEMENT 20. Y.)

**Hypericum** <note>original: "Υπέρικον" (Hyperikon); commonly known as St. John’s Wort</note> is a stupendously good herb; some call it "Dionysian"—that is, belonging to Bacchus and Jupiter—and it is also called *hyperbatotylon* <note>original: "ὑπερβατότυλον"; likely referring to its growth habit or a variant name for the plant</note>. It is a plant that grows in the summer.
**Hyperion** <note>original: "Υπερίων"</note> is a bird; it is the female eagle, which is similar to the male eagle.
**Hymos** <note>original: "Υμος"</note> is a well-known sea fish; it is edible but of poor quality.
**Hyenos** <note>original: "Υενος"</note> is a stone found in rivers; it is a small stone possessing a deep, blood-red color.

Engrave, therefore, upon the stone an **Eagle** tearing a **fish** apart. Beneath the stone, place a small root of the herb <term>Hypericum</term> and the tip of a feather from the eagle’s wings; if you do not have that, use a hawk’s feather. By enclosing it, you will have a great **phylactery** <note>a protective amulet or charm</note> *against all attacks of the womb <note>original: "occursus matricis"; referring to the ancient medical concept of "suffocation of the womb" or hysteria</note>, and for blackouts, the flow of blood, inflammations, and furthermore for all uterine sufferings—except for the prolapse of the womb.* Give this, therefore, to suffering women as if it were a great sacred mystery.

## (ELEMENT 21. PHI.)

**Phrynion** <note>Greek: *Φρύvιον*; likely a species of buttercup</note> is an herb which is called **batrachion** <note>Greek: *βατράχιον*; literally "frog-herb"</note>, that is, the "frog-plant," and it is a harmful plant. Its appearance is like "frog-parsley" <note>original: *apium raninum*; likely the Celery-leaved Buttercup</note>: it grows in water and possesses a burning quality. The **Phryne** <note>Greek: *Φρύvη*; usually meaning "toad," but here described as a bird</note> is a bird; some call it the **icterus** <note>original: *icterem*; the Golden Oriole or a yellow bird believed to cure jaundice</note>, others the "green bird"; it is the size of a sparrow. The **Phoke** <note>Greek: *Φώκη*</note>, that is, the **sea-calf** <note>a seal</note>, is a sea fish—or rather, it is a most excellent sea animal—having hands like a human and a face like a calf or an ox. The **Phrynos** <note>Greek: *Φρύvος*</note> is a stone; some call it the **batrachite** <note>original: *βάτραχον*; "toad-stone"</note>.

The power of this herb is as strong as iron, namely for cutting through abscesses, swollen glands, scrofula <note>a form of tuberculosis affecting the lymph nodes of the neck</note>, and whatever contains pus. You must only apply the plaster or wax-salve for three hours; then remove it, and you will find the body marked and wounded. On tough bodies, however, leave it for a longer time. After the incision has been made, use those things which cleanse and draw out—or wipe away—and then use healing agents. This is the **rosarium** <note>a rose-colored or rose-scented medicinal preparation</note>:

| :--- | :--- |
| Batrachian herb | 4 ounces |

| Cedar, Rose, or Costus oil | as much as suffices |
| French Soap | 4 ounces |

Grind these things diligently and make a plaster, and store it in a glass vessel. Use it, but take care not to place it over a nerve or a muscle, and it will do—

The <term>apozema</term> <note>a medicinal decoction or herbal infusion</note> or the cup of the phylactery is as follows: Take a new flask or a basin in which the aforementioned water is kept where the bird was killed; take 2 ounces of that water, 1 ounce of honey, and 4 ounces of the root of the herb *libanotis* <note>rosemary, often called "frankincense-herb" in antiquity</note>, and twenty-eight grains of barley that have been previously soaked in rainwater until they germinate—so much so that the sprout is already visible—and 4 ounces of the herb *batrachia* <note>likely "frog-wort" or a species of Ranunculus</note>, 4 ounces of frankincense, and the herb *chameleon* <note>likely the carline thistle</note>...

If indeed you tie its heart <note>the seal's heart</note> to the mast of a ship, the ship will never suffer shipwreck; and if you place its skin on the yardarm or the mast of a ship, it will not be struck by lightning. Its claws, when hung [around the neck], heal the chills of daily, tertian, and quartan fevers <note>ancient classifications of fevers based on whether they recurred every day, every third day, or every fourth day</note>.

Its heart, tied in a cloth and worn on the arms, drives away tertian and quartan fevers, as well as same-day fevers <note>original: *αὐθημερινον* (authemerinon); a fever that begins and peaks on the same day</note>. Therefore, on a frog-stone <term>batrachite: a legendary stone believed to be found in the heads of frogs, or a stone resembling a frog in color</term>, carve a hawk, and by its feet a frog-fish <note>original: *batrachum piscem*; likely referring to a monkfish or anglerfish</note>, and enclose within [the setting] the tongue of a frog, the small root of the herb, and the tip of the bird’s tongue, and give it to whomever you wish to carry.

For it stops the flow of blood and *aslepiadas* <note>original: *aslepiadas*; an obscure medical term likely referring to specific types of internal pains or swellings</note>, and heals the sufferings of jaundice. It also works for those with hemoptysis <term>hemoptoics: people who cough up blood</term> and those who spit blood, and for the menstrual flow of women; it also acts against fits of fury and the wrath of enemies, especially if the hairs of a seal are enclosed therein.

Furthermore, it protects against poisonous stings. Whatever you wish to know about the life of a man and a woman—their actions, lineages, and lots or fortunes—and about thieves and fugitives, where and how they are, you will know every single thing like a Wise Man, and you will recognize and discern them.

# KIRANI

*The **Chrysopteros** <note>original: Χρυσέωτερος</note>, that is, the **golden wing**, is a bird the size of a quail.*
*The **Chrysophrys** <note>original: Χρύσοφος</note> is an edible sea fish, known to all <term>Chrysophrys: the gilt-head bream, named for the golden stripe between its eyes</term>.*
*The **Chrysites** <note>original: Χρυσίτης</note> is a variegated stone, and seemingly golden <term>Chrysites: likely a type of litharge or a mineral with a metallic, golden luster</term>.*

The flower of the **Chrysanthemum** <term>Chrysanthemum: literally "golden flower"; in this context, likely a marigold or a similar bright yellow herb</term> is therefore golden, and the calyx in the middle of the flower contains things like small black ants with tiny wings. This is called **human blood**. This, therefore, should be collected before the rising of the Sun, while the Sun is in Aries <term>Aries: the first sign of the zodiac, associated with springtime, renewal, and the exaltation of the Sun</term>, and it should be placed in a glass vessel with oil of roses and the flower of the herb. Then, coming in the morning, purely <note>original: castè; implying a state of ritual or moral purity</note> take a little of it and anoint your face, and go forth with confidence; for it will make you **lovable, and accepted, and successful with everyone and in all things.** If, however, you do this as the Sun is rising, it will be even more effective. It will also provide **protection against unfortunate degrees** <note>original: gradus infortunatos; a reference to unfavorable astrological positions or "steps" of bad luck</note> and such things.

Furthermore, the root of the herb, tied in a cloth with a stone taken from the head of the fish, helps with the pain of **erupting teeth in small children.** The eyes of the **Chrysepteris** bird, when hung [on the body], heal **tertian fevers** <term>tertianarios: a fever that recurs every third day, often associated with malaria</term>. Its heart, likewise hung around the neck, cures those suffering from fevers. The stone from its head, hung at the neck, heals those with **consumption** <term>phtisicos: people suffering from phthisis, a historical term for pulmonary tuberculosis or wasting diseases</term>. On a **Chrysites** stone, carve the bird having a round crown, and at its feet the fish, and enclose the root of the herb beneath it. Give it to be carried...

### (ELEMENT 23. $\psi$.)

**Psyllion** <note>original: $\psi\acute{\upsilon}\lambda\lambda\iota o\nu$; likely Fleawort (Plantago psyllium), named for its seeds which look like fleas</note> is a herb known to everyone.
The **Sea-Psyllos** <note>original: $\psi\acute{u}\lambda\lambda$ [marinus]; likely a sand-hopper or sea-flea</note> is a very small animal which fishermen use, and which leaps along the shore.
The **Psar** <note>original: $\psi\grave{\alpha}\rho$</note> is a very well-known bird, that is, the **Starling** <note>original: *Sturnellus*</note>.
The **Psorites** stone <note>original: *Psorites lapis*</note>, which is also called **Psorites** <note>original: $\psi\omega\rho\grave{i}\tau$</note>, that is, a "ford" or "shoal" <note>original: *vadum*; perhaps suggesting the stone is found in shallow waters</note>.

Take 3 ounces of the seed of the herb, 2 cups <term>cotyla: an ancient measure of volume, approximately half a pint or 0.27 liters</term> of water; boil until it becomes viscous, and strain it through a cloth, and throw away the dregs. In that water which you have strained, put 3 ounces of wax and 6 ounces of oil. Then boil it until the wax melts, and grind it for a long time in a mortar. *For this is a divine plaster for those with gout, and for hardened and insensitive flesh* <note>original: *lapidosos & infensatas carnes*; refers to callouses, tumors, or areas of the body that have become "stony" or lost sensation</note>. If, however, you also place the stone in the blood of the bird and anoint the face, you will cure tertian and quartan fevers <term>tertian and quartan fevers: types of malaria characterized by paroxysms occurring every third or fourth day</term>. If you dip the stone three times into the aforementioned juice of the herb and anoint those suffering or those with headaches <term>cephalalgicos: those suffering from cephalalgia, the medical term for a headache</term>...

### (ELEMENT 24. Ω.)

**Basil** <note>original: *Ωκιμον* (Okimon); Ocimum basilicum</note> is known to everyone; it is an edible plant.

It is smooth to the touch.

**Aiz**, that is, the female goat known to all, which is also called an "oracle" <note>original: *oraculum*; here suggesting the animal possesses a natural prophetic or divinatory power</note>: so that if someone wraps a goat skin around an **epileptic**, and leads them beside a river or the sea, they will soon be seized by falling, trembling, and suffering. 85. That is, the **Cow** <note>original: *Bos foemina*; literally "female ox"</note>, known to all. **The Frog** <note>original Greek: *Batrachos*; Latin: *Rana*</note>, a most well-known animal.

If someone wishes to remove hair from the whole body, let him burn the skin of a frog, put it in bathwater, and it will cast the hair off. For **patchy baldness** <term>alopecia</term>, apply the ashes of small frogs with soft pitch, and you will cure the condition.

It also heals large **scrofulas** <note>swelling of the lymph nodes in the neck</note>, as well as ailments of the breasts, the testicles, and **abscesses** <term>apostemata</term> prepared for surgery, and **buboes** <note>painful swellings in the groin or armpit</note>.

Its **testicles** are used for both aiding conception and preventing it. However, the left testicle, when tied in the skin of a **mule** and worn, acts as a **contraceptive** <term>inconceptivum</term>. One must write upon the mule-skin these names: *in ora . i . idacvia . i . joa . Q . 4 . v VIII . irayo og . j . voi koox Ζ . 1 . ceochi .* <note>These are "voces magicae" or magical words/symbols common in ancient spells; they are intended for their sound or mystical power rather than literal meaning.</note> 

If you do not believe this, test it on a hen that lays eggs, and she will cease to lay. You must cut off the weasel's testicles during the **waning of the Moon** <note>original: *deminutione Lunae*; the period after the full moon, traditionally associated with "decreasing" or stopping things</note>, and then let the animal go alive. If the testicles are then carried in a mule’s hide, it is an invincible contraceptive and an **aphrodisiac** <term>amaterium</term>. 

Furthermore, preserve the weasel's **blood** in vinegar and give it secretly to an **epileptic** until it is all consumed; it heals the condition perfectly.

The **doe** <gloss>original: *dorcas*, also known as the roe deer</gloss> is a four-legged animal that possesses a power for **conception**. If, therefore, you wish to ensure a great and "unfailing" <note>original: *intransgressibilem*, meaning a conception that cannot be thwarted</note> conception, do as follows: Take four ounces of **Satyrion** seed <term>satyrion</term> <note>a plant, typically an orchid, believed in antiquity to be a powerful aphrodisiac</note>, all the liquid gall of a doe, and three ounces of honey. Dissolve these and store them in a glass vessel. When it is needed, give some of it to the woman <note>original: *merechidi*, likely a corrupted term referring to the womb or the female recipient</note> to be taken with raisins, and let intercourse take place. If you wish to conceive a **male**, take the gall of a male; if a **female**, take the gall of a female. Indeed, this is a very delightful remedy. If it has become dried out, add enough honey to compensate.

The **viper** <gloss>original: *exidna*</gloss> is a twisting serpent known to everyone. Some people take it alive and place it in a raw clay pot with **salt**, and leave it in a furnace for a day and a night until it is "fried" <note>meaning thoroughly dried or calcined by heat</note>. By mixing in aromatic spices, they make a preparation to resist every infirmity. This **medicated salt** treats **elephantiasis** <term>elephantiosis</term>, **leprosy**, **gout** <term>podagra</term>, **epilepsy**, **paralysis**, and all desperate ailments <term>passiones</term>. Furthermore, the **fat** of the viper sharpens the vision and removes all dimness of the eyes. The **eyes**, when hung around the neck, cure all **inflammations of the eye** <term>ophthalmia</term>. The **teeth** assist with **toothaches** <term>odontalgia</term> and allow the teeth of small children to grow without pain. The smoke of a **jet stone** <term>gagates</term> <note>a type of black lignite gemstone</note> puts this serpent to flight, and the stone, when drunk with **deer marrow**, heals its bites.

Anointed with lily oil, it settles fever; ground with mustard and dry vinegar, it thickens [hair in] **hair loss** <term>alopecia</term> <note>the clinical term for patchy baldness</note>.

**Deer** <gloss>original: *Elaphon* from the Greek Ἐλάφων; Latin: *cervorum*</gloss>. There are three species of deer. It is a well-known animal. One species is called the **broad-antlered deer** <gloss>original: *platykeros* or πλατύκερως, meaning "having wide and high horns"</gloss>; the second has **round horns**; the third is the **female**. To this female, the male cannot be joined except near a spring. For when she is thirsty, she seeks a spring, and while she drinks with burning thirst and great appetite, then the male mounts her; for, compelled by the desire to drink, she cannot flee. At other times, she does not permit mating. Thus, she conceives immediately. This animal would live five hundred years if it were not captured. Of the "true" deer—I mean those with round horns—give one spoonful of **horn filings** with **honey-water** <term>hydromel</term> <note>a mixture of honey and water, often fermented or used as a vehicle for medicine</note> for seven days to one suffering from **colic** <term>colico</term>, and they will be perfectly healed of the suffering. Moreover, the **gall** <note>the bile or gallbladder</note> taken with honey aids in conception.

There are three types of **Lizard** <gloss>original: *Zavra*, from the Greek σαύρα</gloss>: one is called **Solar** <gloss>original: *heliakē* or ἡλιακὴ</gloss>; another **Bronze** <gloss>original: *chalke* or χαλκὴ</gloss>; the third is **Green** <gloss>original: *chlōra* or χλωρὰ</gloss>. The solar lizard is known to everyone. If anyone wears its right [front] foot around the neck in a small golden **pendant case** <term>capsella</term>, having also this name written [within]: *ha.va.ba.Ga.b.i.auluſare* <note>this is a magical "word of power" or *voces magicae* used to charge the talisman with efficacy</note>.

The **Solar lizard** can do the same things as the **Green** lizard. In another [instance], take a **Jasper** <note>a variety of quartz, often green, highly valued in antiquity for protective amulets</note>, having an engraved **Lizard** <gloss>original: *Saura*</gloss> curled back toward its belly, and the inscription: *zibem Q. a. y. i. pyram*. If someone has a **cold catarrh** <note>a "runny nose" or excessive mucus discharge believed in humor theory to be a "cold" ailment</note> and kisses the nostrils of a **hinny** <term>burdonis</term> <note>a *burdo* is the offspring of a stallion and a female donkey; a hinny</note>, he will be healed, even if he has a **heavy head cold** <gloss>original Greek: *κόρυζαν* (*koryzan*)</gloss>. Furthermore, if one takes the **urine of a mule** secretly and cooks it with **wax**, **oil**, and **litharge** <term>lithargyro</term> <note>lead monoxide, commonly used in ancient ointments and plasters</note>, and applies it as a plaster to the foot of a person with **gout** <term>podagrici</term>; the human will indeed become healthy, but the hinny’s foot will suffer the pain instead <note>this describes a "transference" spell, where the disease is moved from a human to an animal</note>. For a woman, perform this using a female mule.

When applied as a plaster, as has been said, it evacuates <term>dropsy</term> <note>edema; an excess of watery fluid in the body's tissues</note> through the urine. Its **dried and ground dung**, sprinkled over water and drunk, heals those with dysentery. Used as a fumigant, it also casts off adversities. Its **brain**, however, when anointed with rose oil on the front and back of the head and the whole body, heals <term>epilepsy</term>. This is wonderful and most excellent.

Castoreum, if eaten during the onset of a **quartan fever** <note>a fever that recurs every four days, often associated with malaria</note>—specifically if one <term>drachma</term> <note>an ancient unit of weight; indicated by the symbol ʒ</note> is given—heals those suffering from that fever.

The **Land Crocodile** <note>Greek: *Krokodeilos*</note> is known to everyone. For it is a four-footed animal with a wide head and a long tail. One is known to everyone: others, however, have long heads and are large, black in color: others are thin, slender, and red, which are called Zinyphes* <note>original: *Zinyphes*; likely a term for stinging gnats or very small biting ants</note>: *others are large and broad: others are field ants and medium-sized: others are also* **of the good path** <note>original Greek: εὐόδιαι (*euodiai*); literally "well-traveling" or "easy-road" ants</note>, *and they are small: others, however, are* **ant-lions** <note>original Greek: μυρμηκολέοντες (*myrmēkoleontes*)</note>, *large and variegated and winged, and they eat flesh, but die quickly. All of them, however, beheaded with forceps and ground up, and placed on the eyelids, heal sties* <term>hordeolos</term> <note>original: *hordeolos*; literally "little barley-corns," referring to small, painful boils on the edge of the eyelid</note>. *Likewise, the field ants, which eat grain, do the same. If anyone, however, [takes] 40 ants with the juice of asphodel* <note>a hardy lily-like plant associated in antiquity with the meadows of the Underworld</note>

The **Sheep** <note>Greek: *Probaton*; Latin: *Ovis*</note> is known to everyone. # KIRANI. 91

...the **liver** <note>original: *hepatem*</note> of a sheep, eaten on an empty stomach, prevents drunkenness, and no matter how much one drinks, they will not feel its effects. **Scingus** <note>Greek: *Skingos*; likely referring to the skink, a type of lizard</note> is a land animal, similar to a crocodile, found in the **Melanitida** <note>likely referring to a region in Egypt or Ethiopia</note> region. # KIRANIDES

**Salamander** <note>original: *Salamandra*</note> is a four-footed animal, larger than a lizard or the **green lizard** <note>Greek: *Saura viridis*</note>; it lives among brambles and thickets. *Its heart, when carried, makes the bearer fearless against fire, bold against burning, and unburnt. Therefore, if the animal is cast into a furnace or fire, it extinguishes every flame; and if you hang its heart from the neck of someone suffering from a fever, the fever is immediately extinguished. And if a woman wears it around her knees, she will by no means conceive, nor will she feel her monthly periods; indeed, they are extinguished. If, however, you bind it in a black skin <note>likely black leather or parchment</note> and hang it from the elbow, it removes tertian, semitertian, and quartan fevers, and every type of feverish cycle <term>typum</term>.* <note>These terms refer to fevers that recur every third or fourth day, common in malaria-prone regions.</note> *Truly, its burnt ashes, when sprinkled on **warts** <note>Greek: *myrmēkias*</note>, exterminate these eruptions <note>original: *formicationes*</note>, and it eradicates foot corns and draws them out swiftly.*

**Bull** <note>Greek: *Tauros*</note>, known to all and bold. *Its bile, dissolved with an eggshell and rubbed on, makes a scar the same color [as the skin]. Washed with vinegar, it heals black leprosy, and casts out dandruff from the head, and cleanses freckles and the spots* <note>original: *nebulas* or *naevulos*</note> *of women, for the sake of the children who are outside.* <note>This phrase is obscure; it may refer to the health of children already born or perhaps to the external appearance of the mother.</note> *For not only is the animal itself strong, but the power placed around it reaches deep within. And if a man carries it <note>likely referring to an amulet made from the bull</note>, he shall know all things. Similarly, the horns of its head do not...*

# KIRANIDES 93

By itself, applied to the anus or the navel, the gall evacuates the bowels more effectively than an enema; applied as a plaster to the navel, it draws out **intestinal worms** <term>lumbricos</term>. Furthermore, **bull’s dung** <term>fimus tauri</term> stops the flow of blood from the nose, thickens hair lost to **alopecia** <note>original: *alopeciam*; a condition of patchy baldness</note>, and halts the falling of hair due to illness. 

His dried blood, mixed and rubbed on with **omphacine oil** <note>oil made from unripe olives</note>, and used as an ointment, blackens grey hair—provided the bull was black. When the bull has died, after seven days it produces worms, which on the thirty-first day become **honey-bees** <term>apes mellificæ</term>; collect these in vessels. <note>This refers to the ancient "Bugonia" belief that bees were spontaneously generated from the carcasses of oxen.</note>

The common **hawk** <term>accipiter</term> also performs this; however, keep this strictly hidden.

Moreover, the **tallow** <term>sevum</term> from its kidneys, boiled with **emmer** <term>farre</term> or **spelt juice**, is administered for the same [ailments].

The **resin** <term>laudanum</term> from its beard—that is, **mummy** <note>original: *mumia*; here referring to a medicinal resinous substance</note>, or **grease** <note>original: *ysopos* or *aesypos*; known today as lanolin</note>, or collected **unwashed sweat**—acts against **alopecia** <note>patchy baldness</note> and the falling out of hair when mixed with wine and **unripe olive oil** <term>oleo omphacino</term>; when smeared on with vinegar, it heals a **headache** <term>cephalalgiam</term>. The **bile** <note>original: *Fel*</note> of this animal <note>the hyena</note> is sweet and possesses great power for making illusions. A great **spectacle** <term>ostentaculum</term> is prepared from it.

The composition is as follows: take the eyes of the **Glaucus fish** <note>a silvery-blue fish mentioned by ancient writers, possibly a blue shark or a species of sea-bass</note> and the right eyes of the **hyena**, along with all the fluid from the hyena. Dissolve all of these together and store them in a glass vessel, sealing it very well. 

If you then wish to create a great illusion, do so using a **lamp** <term>lucerna</term>. Mix the **fat** <term>adipem</term> of any reptile or four-legged animal you choose with a small amount of the aforementioned mixture. If you then smear it onto a **wick** <term>inularion</term> or **reed-wick** <term>chinariton</term> <note>these are Latinized Greek terms for specific types of lamp wicks or the plants used to make them</note> and show it to those present late in the evening with the lamp lit, they will believe the lamp itself is that wild beast whose fat was used—whether it be a **lion**, a **bull**, a **serpent**, or any other creature.

If you wish to create a display or a **phantasm** <term>phantasiam</term>, place the fat of any animal or wild beast you choose, along with a tiny portion of the preparation, upon **burning coals** in the middle of the house. The animal whose fat you mixed in will then appear. You may do the same with **birds** <term>volucribus</term>.

If you mix a little **sea-water** <note>original: *undâ maris*</note> into the preparation and sprinkle it among dinner guests, they will all flee, believing the sea has appeared in their very midst.

It is an extremely useful and very beneficial animal.

# KIRANIDES

The power of this animal <note>referring to the Mole, *aspalax*</note> is divine and effective; we shall not remain silent regarding it, and this is a taste of its properties: For if someone tastes one ounce <note>original: *℥ i.*</note> of it at sunrise, he will know all future things until the sun sets. The preparation for this tasting is as follows: Take a **mole** and suffocate it in three cotylas <note>original: *cotyl. iij.*; a cotyla is an ancient liquid measure roughly equal to half a pint</note> of rainwater and boil it until it is dissolved;

then strain the water into a bronze vessel, adding the following: four ounces of **Theogonos root** <note>original: *Theogoni radicum*; likely referring to the Peony or a plant named "Birth of the Gods"</note>, four ounces of **mugwort** <note>original: *artemisia*</note>—specifically that which has only a single branch—four ounces of **storax** <note>a fragrant tree resin</note>, and four ounces each of **Troglodyte myrrh** <note>a high-quality resin from the Red Sea coast</note>, **bdellium** <note>a semi-transparent oleo-gum resin</note>, and **sphaeritis** <note>likely a rounded type of incense or the plant *Sphaeritis*</note>, and eight ounces of **male frankincense**. Grind and sift these, and add one cotyla of the best honey; cook it to the thickness of honey, store it, and use it as described. Truly, bury the **bones of the mole** under the earth in your house. For the mole is useful both living and dead, just as is the "head of a woman" <note>this may refer to the root of a specific plant or a symbolic magical correspondence</note>. 

If, however, someone has **parotids** <term>swellings of the salivary glands behind the ears</term>, **scrofula** <term>a form of tuberculosis affecting the lymph nodes of the neck</term>, or any **abscess** <note>original: *apostema*</note>, and takes a living mole and rubs it in his hands for a long time until it dies, it will cure all such ailments; and he shall not suffer from issues with the **uvula** thereafter, nor will he ever have **cankers** <note>original: *cantharidas*</note>, **buboes** <term>inflamed swelling of a lymph node</term>, **scrofula**, or any **abscess**. Its melted **fat** excellently heals **ophthalmia** <term>severe inflammation of the eye</term>. Therefore, bury a mole in your house. 

If anyone should swallow its **heart** while it is still crawling or palpitating, he will receive **foreknowledge** of future things and of those things which shall happen in the world.

<meta>This page serves as a critical commentary and list of corrections for the preceding text of the Kiranides. It focuses on philological details—correcting scribal errors, clarifying Greek-to-Latin translations, and debating specific word choices based on manuscript evidence and the opinions of other scholars like Caspar Barthius.</meta>

### For the better correction of those preceding [works] of
# KIRANI KIRANIDES,
# RHYAKINI KORONIDES
## Some Notes and Various Readings.

Page 1, line 14. **He does not disdain to look down upon earthly things.** <note>original: *Terrena despicere non dedignatur*</note>] Here, a certain foolish scribe <note>original: *Sciolus*; a "smatterer" or someone with superficial knowledge</note> in the manuscript ineptly substituted—or rather, superimposed—the word **to look at** <note>original: *conspicere*</note>. In this context, **to look down upon** <note>original: *despicere*</note> properly signifies to look from above or to look downward. It corresponds to the Greek **to look down/oversee** <note>original: *καθορᾶν (kathoran)*</note>, not **to overlook/neglect** <note>original: *παρορᾶν (paroran)*</note> or **to look over/scorn** <note>original: *ὑπερορᾶν (hyperoran)*</note>; just as a **view** <note>original: *καθόρημα (kathorema)*</note>, or a prospect from a higher place, is called a "despectus" by Caesar.

Same page, line 21. **The translations from the Agarenic language are diverse.**] In the manuscript, it was written as the "Angarenic" language, but it is more correctly read as **Agarenic**—that is, Arabic, named after Hagar. However, this slip is easy to make in the Greek language, because a double Gamma <note>the Greek letter Γ</note> might have been used, where the first is then pronounced as an "N." We see this same thing happen in the words **forced service** and **compulsory labor** <note>original: *ἀγγαρείᾳ (angareia)* and *ἀγγαρεία*</note>, which signify the same thing: namely, forced servitude.

Page 2, line 21. **The Book of Physical virtues, compassions, and cures.**] The Illustrious Caspar Barthius, Knight of the Holy Roman Empire, in his *Adversaria* <note>a collection of miscellaneous notes or commentaries</note> reads this as: **Of Contests** <note>original: *Certationum*</note>, which I do not fully understand. For otherwise, this word **of cures** <note>original: *curationum*</note> appears everywhere in the manuscript in this contracted form.

Same page, line 27. **Hermes Trismegistus was the receiver of this book...** <note>original: *Accipiens fuit Hermes T. D. h. o. n. librum hunc*; the abbreviations likely stand for *Ter Maximus* (Thrice Great) and perhaps *huius operis nostri* (of this our work)</note>] We recognize a transposition here <note>a reversal of word order</note> which plainly obscures the sense; it should therefore be read: Re-

...receiving this book was Hermes Trismegistus, a god known to all men.

Page 3, line 20. **Their reciprocal mixings.** <note>original: *Vicarias eorum permixtiones*</note>] So it is indeed expressed in the codex, by an error of the scribe <term>librarius</term><note>a professional copyist or secretary</note>, who should have written not "reciprocal" <note>original: *vicarias*</note> but "various" <note>original: *varias*</note>.

Same page. **And the properties (we hold): if indeed etc.**] I have added that word **we hold** <note>original: *tenemus*</note> on my own authority, so that the sense might remain consistent, just as I substituted **if indeed** <note>original: *siquidem*</note> for **if any** <note>original: *si qua*</note>, as it was in the text. Indeed, just as in this place, I have often observed in others that whatever appeared clearly corrupt in the manuscript should be corrected, enclosed in parentheses, and printed in different characters. Likewise, if anything required explanation, as in line 25 of the same page: **element** <note>original: *elementum*</note> is explained as the "Order of letters" or the "Alphabet"; on page 11, **copper-stone** <term>chalcitis</term> <note>a copper-bearing mineral often used in ancient medicine</note> is explained by **Misy** <note>original: *Misy*; a yellow mineral, likely oxidized copper ore or iron vitriol</note>; and on page 42, **Valorus** is explained as a "wolf" <note>original: *lupum*</note>.

Page 4, line 4. **The therapeutic Book of Syria.**] The manuscript had **of the therapeutic** <note>original: *therapeutici*</note>, following the Greek text, **the therapeutic book** <note>original: ἡ βίβλος θεραπευτική (*hē biblos therapeutikē*)</note>.

Same page, line 8. **Of which I have written the whole history.**] The manuscript said **I have taken up** <note>original: *sumpsi*</note>.

Page 5, line 2. **For the health and cure of those men of the city.**] I write: **For the health and treatment of the men of that city.**

Page 6, line 1. **Things which I do not wish to bring forth to everyone.**] The manuscript says: **things which it is not proper to bring forth.** And likewise also on the second to last line of page 16: **things which it is not proper to say.**

Same page, line 15. **The second book concerning the small Archaic [work]**] The manuscript had **archiac**, from which I would have preferred **archiatric** <note>original: *archiatricâ*; relating to an "Archiater" or chief physician</note>, unless

<meta>This page continues the philological corrections of the *Kiranides* manuscript. The translator/editor is specifically addressing errors made by previous scribes or the earlier Latin translator, focusing on botanical terms and medical applications. The catchword from the previous page was "visis," and this page begins with a discussion of the "Archaic Book."</meta>

On page 30, the "Archaic Book" <note>original: *Archaicus liber*</note> is explicitly cited, of which mention is also made on page 7. For *archaikos* <note>original: ἀρχαϊκὸς</note> means ancient, because this first book is considered older and prior to the others which are called the *Kiranides* <note>a collection of ancient texts on the magical and medicinal properties of plants, animals, and stones</note>. By which name perhaps it should read "concerning the first archaic [book]"; for in the manuscript, at least the letters "pa" <note>likely an abbreviation for *prima archaica*</note> were present.

Page 8, line 1. Read: "for those suffering from dysentery and those coughing up blood" <note>original: *ad dys. & hæmoptoicos*</note>.

Page 9, line 11. **with forty thymes of the herb and *ruboso de spicu*.**] So the manuscript has it, but erroneously. Read *Cymis* <note>meaning young shoots or sprouts</note>. Our author quite often uses this word to mean the young shoots of first germination (which among the Greeks are *kymata* <note>original: κύματα</note>, as if *kyemata* <note>original: κυήματα</note>, and among our own people are also called **sprouts** <note>original: **Keymen**</note>, while by physicians they are otherwise called asparagus or shoots <term>turiones</term> <note>the tender, edible part of a plant budding from the underground stem</note>; and they are explained in this way a little later on page 12, lines 7 and 8). What "ruboso de spicu" might be, however, I do not understand, unless it should be read as "of the oak" <note>original: *quercu*</note>, or *kekidi* <note>original: κηκίδι; a gall-nut or oak-apple</note>, or rather "of the red grape-like fruit" <note>original: *rubeo uvoso fructu*</note>, as it appears on page 14, line 7. I think this is certainly the case, for it is added there that hides <term>coria</term> are depilated <note>hair is removed</note> or prepared with it. And hence the "white vine" is called *psilothron* <note>original: ψίλωθρον; a depilatory</note>, for the "depilation of hair" <note>original: πρὸς ψίλωσιν τῶν τριχῶν</note>, from the depilating of hides, a use which its berries and root possess. See Dioscorides <note>an ancient Greek physician and botanist</note>, book 4, chapter 280, and Theophrastus <note>a Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle in the Peripatetic school</note>, *History of Plants*, book 9, chapter 22.

Page 11, line 18. **Juice of the river-neighbor** <note>original: *Succū ποταμοῦ γείτονος*</note>] Read *potamogeitonos* <note>original: ποταμογείτονος; pondweed</note>. The translator in this place was quite ridiculous, who, since he did not know that a genus of herb was named from its proximity to rivers because it grows in watery places (as found in Dioscorides book 4, chapter 101), divided it into two words...

<meta>This page continues a list of textual corrections to a printed edition of the Kiranides, comparing it against an original manuscript. The previous page ended with a discussion of botanical terms and possible errors in the transcription of Greek words like "kekidi."</meta>

*...visited, and also translated it into Latin, as if not the juice of some herb, but the water of the nearest river should be mixed in.*

Page 12, line 26. **the small hollows of the hooves: the root broken with wine.**] The manuscript <note>abbreviated as *Msc.*</note> reads: **I have seen the small hollows of the hooves prepared with wine.** <note>The Latin *anxillas* is likely a corruption of *axillas*, meaning the "armpits" or hollow joints of the hooves.</note>

Page 13, line 14. **For it also moderately purges the belly: line 18. it is also beneficial for paralytics.**] Correct both passages according to the authority of the manuscript.

Page 14, line 11. **The manuscript: having leaves similar to ivy, but large ones similar to bindweed.** <note>original: *smilaci*</note>] This passage was corrupted by the typesetter <term>typotheta</term> <note>the person who manually set the lead type for the printing press</note> because of the repeated word: "similar."

In the same place, the last line. **Secular and delightful, as applied.**] This passage must also be corrected according to the manuscript copy as follows: **The white vine, however, also has other secular and delightful powers, such that in drinks it provides not only sobriety, etc.** What he calls "secular powers" <note>original: *efficacias sæculares*</note> in this place, he calls "worldly" <note>original: *mundanas*</note> on the following page.

Page 15, line 14. Read: **all things which are done by writing, or medicine, or incantation, or the sword.**

Page 17, line 2. **The *Borosites* is a bird, which is called a crow.** <note>original: *Corone*</note>] Ulysses Aldrovandi <note>1522–1605, a renowned Italian naturalist and collector</note> correctly read this passage of the *Kiranides* in book 12 of his *Ornithology*, page 369, although in the manuscript it was *Uorositis*. However, he did not explain the word itself but only included it among the synonyms for the crow. This name seems to designate not so much a broad genus as a specific species of crow—namely, the "fruit-eater" which Aristotle called *spermologon* <note>original: σπερμολόγον</note>, that is, a "seed-collector" <term>frugilega</term> <note>from the Latin *frux* (fruit/grain) and *legere* (to gather)</note>.

# KIRANIDES
<meta>catchword: Thus [Latin: *Sic*]</meta>

The **Eagle** <note>original Greek: *Aëtos*; Latin: *Aquila*</note> is the king of all birds, blackish in color; when this bird flies through the air, every other bird trembles. Moreover, it possesses great <term>actions<span>the medicinal or magical properties of a substance in ancient science</span></term>. Therefore, take an eagle and allow it to live for one day and night, and collect the dung which it produces during that time. Then, binding its feet and blocking its mouth, speak into the ear of the eagle:

->“O eagle, friend of man, now I sacrifice you for the cure of every illness; I adjure you by the God of heaven and earth and by the four elements, that you may have efficacy for each and every treatment for which you shall be offered!”<-

After this, take a sword made entirely of iron, fumigating it with <term>storax<span>a fragrant resin used in incense and medicine</span></term> and honey. Then, place a bowl beneath the bird and cut off its head over the bowl so that all the blood flows down into it. Immediately, while the body is still twitching, cut it open and extract its heart together with all the internal organs, and place them in a vessel containing fragrant wine for drinking. As for the head, pack it away with a great deal of salt; however, extract the eyes and the brain separately. Then, remove all the internal organs from the wine, <gloss>treat them with myrrh</gloss> <note>original: *smyrniza*</note> along with salt, and dry them in the sun. Dry the blood as well and place it in a vessel. Pluck the feathers from the eagle, and keep the large feathers from the thighs. You shall also take the <term>sinews<span>the original term *nervos* refers to tendons or sinews, believed to hold physical power</span></term> from the thighs, marking which are from the right side and which are from the left.

<summary>This page describes the ritual preparation of an eagle for alchemical and medicinal purposes, including a specific incantation and the systematic preservation of its blood, organs, and feathers.</summary>
<keywords>eagle, ritual sacrifice, ancient medicine, alchemy, storax, myrrh, sinews, sympathetic magic</keywords>

# KIRANIDES

You should also do the same with the feathers that are from the wings. 
Treat all the flesh with many aromatics so that it is fragrant, <gloss>rubbing it with myrrh</gloss> <note>original Latin: *smyrnizans*</note>; just as you did with the internal organs, and dry it. Keep all these parts separate and labeled. *For headache, a certain great miracle:* The bone from the crown of the eagle's head, bound to the patient in deerskin, heals the crown of the head. The temples of the eagle cure <term>migraines<span>Latin: *hemicrania*; a severe pain affecting one side of the head</span></term>. Its brain also, anointed with oil and a little <term>cedar oil<span>Latin: *cedria*; a resinous, medicinal oil derived from cedar trees</span></term>, heals those suffering from <term>dizziness<span>Latin: *scotomaticos*; referring to vertigo or dark spots in the vision</span></term> and every ailment of the head. *For favor and fortune:* its right eye, bound in a clean cloth and held in the left hand during conversations, will procure the favor and friendship of great men. Its left eye, however, worn around the neck in deerskin, never allows the wearer to suffer from <term>eye inflammation<span>Latin: *ophthalmia*</span></term>. Above the eyes of the eagle, in the eyebrows, two stones are found, one in each eyebrow; these, bound in the skin or hide of a wolf, a seal, a hyena, or an eagle, and worn at the neck, preserve the wearer from all injustice and harm from wild beasts, and from the snares of demons and men, and bring every good thing. Its tongue, hung in a linen cloth, magnificently heals those with <term>joint pain<span>Latin: *arthriticos*; gout or arthritis</span></term>, and <term>swelling of the uvula<span>Greek: *κιονίδας* (kionidas); the uvula is the fleshy hang-down at the back of the throat</span></term>, and those with a cough or <term>difficulty breathing<span>Greek: *δυσπνοιαν* (dyspnoian)</span></term>. <meta>This page continues the pharmacological and talismanic uses of the eagle's body parts, moving from the head and eyes (discussed on the previous page) to the internal organs, feathers, and extremities. # KIRANIDES

...drunk with <term>oxymel<span>A medicinal mixture of honey and vinegar</span></term> <note>original: *oxymelle*</note> for ten days heals <term>epileptics<span>Those suffering from seizures</span></term>. Its gall, however, applied as an ointment with the juice of <term>horehound<span>Latin: *prasii*; white horehound was a common remedy for respiratory and ocular ailments</span></term>, and also with myrrh and honey without dregs, removes all cloudiness and dullness of vision, *and does not allow disease to develop in the eyes.*

*For every prosperity:* Its heart, as has been said, given in wine and honey and <term>scented with myrrh<span>Latin: *smyrnizatum*; treated or infused with myrrh aromatics</span></term> for seven days, then sewn into the skin of a wolf and carried, ensures that the wearer will fall into no evil, infirmity, or encounter with a wild beast, *but will become prosperous, blessed, and wealthy.*

*For love:* Dried kidneys and testicles, infused and sprinkled over spiced wine, and given in drink or food, lead the one who drinks it into great lust and love, whether it be a male or a female. Furthermore, a feather kept from the right wing will make the wearer wealthy and lovable. But if one takes a feather from the left wing, dips it in oil, and anoints a woman laboring in childbirth from the loins down to the <term>sacrum<span>Latin: *sacram spinam*; literally the "sacred spine," referring to the base of the spinal column</span></term>, she will give birth immediately. Its dried dung, used as a fumigant <note>The practice of burning a substance so the patient inhales or is exposed to the smoke</note>, also aids women in labor, *brings out miscarried fetuses, and extracts the <term>afterbirth<span>Latin: *secundinam*; the placenta and membranes expelled after delivery</span></term>.*

The nerves of its neck and back, tied to the neck and the <term>vertebrae<span>Latin: *spondylos*</span></term>—the right to the right side and the left to the left side—are beneficial to those with <term>gout in the hands<span>Latin: *chiragricis*</span></term>. Similarly, the nerves of the feet *heal* <term>gout in the feet<span>Latin: *podagram*</span></term> and pain in the knees. Its talons, [worn] by children and adults...

# KIRANIDES

...[having been] carried, they free the wearer from every evil and wicked phantom, and from every harm. Its skin, however, cured and carefully kept with its feathers, if placed as a bandage upon the belly and stomach, magnificently aids those with <term>colic<span>Severe, fluctuating pain in the abdomen</span></term>, and frees both those with stomach ailments and those who suffer from stomachic colic; it aids digestion and even provides an oracle in dreams regarding these types of future events. If someone going to sleep places the bone or the beak of an eagle at their head, they will see in dreams whatever they desire.

This is the sum of what we have said concerning the eagle. If, however, you cannot find an eagle, take a vulture and act in a similar manner: for it can do the same things, though not as intensely. The vulture has its own specific virtues and efficacies. But if you do not have a vulture, a hawk fulfills the utility, though even less effectively. Each bird has its own specific virtues. But the eagle, being greater than all of them, is capable of greater things, and this power is unique to it. 

If anyone places an eagle's feather or wing under the feet of a woman laboring in childbirth, she will soon give birth; however, as soon as she has given birth, remove the feather. 

The <term>Aetites stone<span>Also known as the "Eagle Stone," these are traditionally geodes containing loose sediment or a smaller stone that rattles when shaken</span></term> <note>original: *Ætites*</note>, which rattles from within when touched, is red in color and like <note>The text notes an alternative reading: "in"</note> wax; when worn, it preserves the fetus in the womb and does not allow miscarriages to occur, for it brings about a swift birth.

# KIRANIDES

**The Falcon** <note>original: *Ἅρπη* (Harpē)</note> is a bird of prey, similar to a vulture, though indeed smaller and more ruddy <note>original: *rubicundior*</note>. Its belly, dried and ground, when taken with barley meal or a grain porridge, heals <term>dysuria<span>Difficulty or pain during urination</span></term>, breaks stones in the bladder, and heals <term>ileus and chordapsus<span>Two forms of severe intestinal obstruction and painful abdominal cramping</span></term> <note>original: *εἰλεὸν & χόρδαξον*</note> of the loins: for the belly of this bird, whether eaten or carried as an amulet, produces the highest state of digestion.

**The Rooster** <note>original: *Ἀλέκτωρ* (Alektōr)</note>, that is, the Cock known to everyone. The broth of the **Rooster's** belly, when drunk, loosens the bowels. Its blood, however, heals <term>erysipelas<span>A bacterial skin infection characterized by large, red, inflamed patches</span></term> and <term>chilblains<span>Painful inflammation of small blood vessels in the skin in response to cold</span></term> <note>original: *χίμετλα*</note>, and treats those who have eaten a **sea hare** <note>A type of marine mollusk, *Aplysia*, which was considered highly poisonous in antiquity</note>. If anyone tastes garlic beforehand and then drinks its warm blood with wine, he will have no fear of any reptile. Truly, if the blood is sprinkled with porridge and given in a portion the size of a nut to be eaten for about ten days, it profits those who cough up blood from their chest. Also, its bile, smeared on together with the juice of the **celandine herb** <note>original: *chelidoniæ*</note> and honey, perfectly sharpens the eyesight. Its dung, moreover, when applied as a poultice with vinegar, heals the bites and ulcers caused by rabid wild beasts; and when applied with vinegar, it also ripens <term>boils<span>Localized skin infections or abscesses</span></term> <note>original: *δοθιῆνας*</note>. Its brain, moreover, drunk with vinegar—or rather, not just with vinegar, but drunk with a spiced wine preparation for snakes <note>original: *condito serpentis*</note>—aids those suffering from snake bites. Its testicles, drunk with wine, excite the <term>libido<span>The text uses "Venus" (Venerem) to refer to sexual desire and reproductive health</span></term> and provide a good physical constitution. If anyone binds the **eyes** of this bird in a cloth and places them at the head of someone sleeping too deeply, it will drive sleep away from them. If anyone carries its eyes while sailing on the sea, he will not fear a storm, or winter, or any kind of hardship. Furthermore, if a **pilot** <note>original: *Gubernator*</note> carries them, he will steer the boat secretly and without the affliction of the winter weather.

Its **heart**, however, if carried, will make the bearer handsome, beloved, and dear, and peaceful toward all; even if he falls into the midst of enemies, they will not harm him, and he will be hurt neither by winter nor by lightning, but will be gracious and quiet in all things. It is necessary, however, to place the heart in the bird's own skin, sew it up, and place it in a **golden reed** <note>original: *aureo calamo*; likely a small golden tube or amulet case</note>.

If a fisherman carries the belly, or the head, or the feathers, he will never be cheated of a catch. The whole bird, roasted with its feathers and eaten, calms <term>demoniacs<span>Historically, those believed to be possessed by spirits, often used to describe people with epilepsy or mental illness</span></term>. If kept in a house, it averts seditions and quarrels.

<note>original Greek: *Γύψ* (Gyps); Latin: *Vultur*</note>, a large and most useful bird, known to everyone. When you cut off its toe, say: "I take this toe as a medicine for sciatica, gout, and for all the joints," and after anointing the bird, release it.

It is a bird of varied colors.*

The **Blackbird** <note>original: *Κόσσυφος / Kossyphos*</note> is a *sweet-sounding bird, singing much in summer; and it is entirely black, having only a golden beak. This bird, cooked in old oil until it dissolves, when the oil is applied,* *relieves all pain* <note>original: *ὁ πᾶς ὁ πόνος*</note> *and cures sciatica.*

# KIRANIDES

### KIRANI

The **Aerops** <note>original: *Aἀρℊ* (likely *Aerops* or *Aërops*); a bird often identified with the bee-eater or a type of sea-gull in ancient lore</note> is a sea bird, and it has the same powers as the **Halcyon** <note>The Kingfisher</note>. If a woman laboring in childbirth holds the heart of this bird, she will give birth immediately; however, as soon as she has given birth, take the heart away and carry it outside, lest her intestines follow <note>A common folk-medical warning that the "drawing" power of the charm might cause a prolapse if not removed quickly</note>. Also, its dried stomach, when drunk and worn upon the person, perfectly aids digestion.

The **Firefly** <note>original: *Λαμπουρὶς* (Lampyris)</note> is a small winged bird (or rather, a small winged worm <note>original: *vermiculus*</note>) that flies at night and shines like a star; for it has light in its hind parts, within the lining of its belly. If you tie one in a womb-cloth <note>original: *matrice vulvæ*; likely a small pouch or linen cloth worn near the womb</note> and hang it upon a woman, it acts as a total contraceptive. If anyone places a Firefly on the floor of a house where there are fleas, they will all flee.

The **Bee-eater** <note>original: *Mέροψ* (Merops)</note> <term>i.<span>Abbreviation for *id est*, meaning "that is."</span></term> the **Parra** <note>An ancient bird of omen, sometimes associated with the lapwing or owl</note>, is a bird that is entirely green, which some call the **Gangraena**. It is a clever bird, useful for many things, just like the Halcyon. When it raises its young (its chicks), it moves them from place to place so that no one may catch them. When it sees someone looking for its chicks, it frequently shifts its own position from here to there, lest it be discovered where it is feeding them. Its heart is suitable for inciting excessive love; when eaten, it also helps those with heart trouble <term>cardiacos<span>Those suffering from ailments of the heart or stomach-pit.</span></term>, jaundice <term>ictericos<span>A condition characterized by yellowing of the skin and eyes.</span></term>, and stomach complaints. It is called the **Merops** because it quickly wins a person over to love <note>The author suggests a folk etymology connecting the name to human affection</note>. Its gall, moreover, mixed with honey or the juice of rue, heals cataracts <term>suffusionem oculorum<span>A clouding of the vision or "suffusion" of the eyes, known today as cataracts.</span></term>.

# KIRANIDES

The **Melikos** <note>original: *Μελικός*; a name likely derived from "honey" or "song," probably referring to a warbler or a blackcap</note> is a bird that sings sweetly in the summer. This bird, when burned, mixed with honey, and applied as an ointment <gloss>to dark spots</gloss> <note>original: *μελανηρίῳ* (melanerio); likely referring to skin discolorations, melasma, or "black" marks on the skin</note>, heals fatness or thickness <note>potentially referring to thickened skin, callouses, or swelling</note>; however, when it is roasted and eaten, it heals those with dysentery.

The **Nessa**, that is, the **duck** <note>original: *anas*</note>, is a river and marsh bird known to everyone. Therefore, the blood of a duck, whether warm or dried and drunk with wine, protects the drinker from all poison, and heals those who have been bitten by a viper <note>original: *echidnâ*</note>; it also provides health, strength, and a good constitution.

The **Xutheos** <note>A Greek name often used for yellowish birds or the willow warbler</note>, called by others the **sparrow** <note>original: *στροῦθος* (strouthos) or *passer*</note> (which some call the **tower-dweller** <note>original: *πυργίτην* (pyrgites) or *turritam*</note> and others the **hole-dweller** <note>original: *τρωγλίτην* (troglodytes)</note>). The dung of this bird, drunk with wine, causes a great erection; when applied as an ointment with pig fat, it heals and thickens hair loss <note>original: *alopeciam*</note> and breaks up carbuncles <note>boils or skin abscesses</note>. The bird, when roasted and eaten, stirs Venereal pleasure <note>sexual desire</note>.

The **Ornis**, that is, the **domestic hen** <note>original: *gallina domestica*</note>: its brain, when rubbed [on the gums], causes children’s teeth to grow without pain. When drunk with wine, it heals those who have been bitten by a scorpion. Its heart, moreover, if tied to the hip while it is still throbbing, hastens childbirth most effectively. Its liver, ground up and applied as a plaster with barley flour and water, benefits those suffering from gout <note>original: *podagricis*</note>. Its fat <note>original: *axungia*</note>, melted with nard <note>an aromatic oil from the spikenard plant</note>, is useful for ear pain.

**Rhaphios** <note>original: *Ῥάμφιος*</note>, is a bird along the Nile river, which is called the **Pelican**, and it dwells in the marshes of Egypt. This bird loves its offspring intensely. It also makes tarnished silver bright, and heals and wipes away all darkness. Their blood, when drunk, cures epilepsy.

The head-shaking sparrow is a bird found near water streams and torrents; its tail is always moving, which is why it is called the **wagtail** <note>original: *caudatremula*, literally "trembling-tail"</note>. If someone shuts it up alive with its feathers in a pot, burns it to ash, and gives it to a woman in a drink, it will cause her to dissolve and melt with love. For this drink is irresistible, and there is nothing else like it.

The **Peacock** <note>original: *Ταώς* (Greek), *Pavo* (Latin)</note> is a most sacred bird, striking for its various colors and very beautiful, possessing a delight in its tail. When it mates, it cries out, and after copulation, it retreats backward; it only mates in the springtime. Its eggs are useful for creating a golden color, as are goose eggs. If a peacock dies, its flesh does not rot or give off a foul odor, but remains as if it were preserved with spices <note>This ancient belief in the incorruptibility of peacock flesh led to the bird becoming a symbol of immortality and the Resurrection in early Christian art</note>. The brain of this bird serves as a love potion; its heart, when carried upon the person, grants goodwill and happiness. Its blood, when drunk, drives out demons. Its entrails and dung, when used for <term>suffumigation<gloss>the practice of burning a substance so the smoke can be used for medicinal or magical purposes</gloss></term>, cast out every evil thing. Eating the bird heals those suffering from dysentery. Its dung, when drunk, cures epilepsy.

The **Turtledove** <note>original: *Τρυγών* (Greek), *turtur* (Latin)</note> is a bird known to everyone, being content with only one mate <note>The turtledove was a famous symbol of marital fidelity because it was believed never to take another mate if its partner died</note>. Its fat is beneficial for those suffering from tetanus and for ailments of the womb. Its blood serves as an antidote against poison, while its gall sharpens the eyesight.

The **Phalakros** <note>original: *Φαλακρὸς* (Greek), literally "bald-head," likely referring to the Coot</note> is a bird called the "white-front"; it is entirely black, having a white forehead only. It is found in rivers and ponds. Its brain, mixed with aged oil and applied as an ointment, heals all ailments of the anus. If the bird is eaten, it serves as a medicine against poison.

# KIRANI

The **Chelidon** <note>original: *Xελιδὼν* (Greek)</note>, that is, the **Swallow**, which in springtime awakens everyone with its singing. It possesses the following powers: If someone takes its chicks, puts them in a pot, seals it with clay, and roasts them, then upon opening the pot and inspecting it, they will find two chicks kissing each other and two turning away from each other. Therefore, if you take the ones that are kissing and dissolve them in rose oil, then anoint a woman, she will follow you immediately. If, however, you give her some of their ash in a drink, she will become mad with love. You may undo this if you give her the ash of those turning away, either as an application or in a drink, and she will return to her normal state.

The ash of these chicks and their parents, when applied as an ointment with honey, *cures those suffering from <term>cynanche<gloss>a severe, choking throat inflammation</gloss></term> <note>original: *cynanchicos*</note>, and heals hoarseness when drunk with <term>melicrate<gloss>a mixture of honey and water</gloss></term>.* The swallow itself, when eaten frequently, *cures the <term>sacred disease<gloss>an ancient name for epilepsy</gloss></term> and the <term>falling sickness<gloss>another term for epilepsy, so called because it affected people during public assemblies, or "comitia"</gloss></term>.*

Furthermore, the small stones <note>original: *lapilli*</note> found in the stomachs of these chicks, when hung from the right arm, perfectly heal those with <term>hepatic<gloss>liver-related</gloss></term> <note>original: *epaticos*</note> ailments. They also serve as a preventative against coughs, <term>coryza<gloss>the common cold or nasal congestion</gloss></term>, phlegm, ailments of the uvula in the throat, and even all types of <term>ophthalmia<gloss>inflammation of the eye</gloss></term>. Indeed, the eyes [of the swallow], when bound to the forehead, heal ophthalmia and take away all the shivering of fevers. Swallows, when eaten, also heal epilepsy and speed up childbirth. Their ash, applied as an ointment with honey, sharpens the eyesight; and similarly applied, it treats ulcers in the throat or...

The **Charadrius** <note>original: *Χαράδριος* (Greek), usually identified as a Plover</note> is a royal bird, foreknowing the future. If someone is ill and this bird is brought before them, should the bird turn its face away from the sufferer, the sick person will die. But if it gazes into the face of the invalid, it draws all the infirmity out of them, flies toward the Sun, and casts the sickness away; thus both the sufferer and the bird are saved. Carrying the heart and head of this bird makes the bearer safe and unharmed by any infirmity for all the days of their life.

If someone cuts the tongue from a **living goose** <note>original: *χηνὸς ζωῆς* (Greek)</note>, and places it upon the breast of a sleeping man or woman, that person will confess everything they have ever done. Its brain, when applied with its own fat and sweet clover or fenugreek, along with a decoction or broth, cures <term>rhagades<gloss>painful cracks or fissures in the skin</gloss></term>, <term>hemorrhoids</term>, and every kind of tumor of the anus. It is effective against <term>phlegmon<gloss>acute, spreading inflammation of the tissues</gloss></term> <note>original: *phlegmonem*</note>, conditions of the anus, and ruptures, and perfectly aids every pain. ...[burnt] <note>The text begins mid-word, likely "combustus" (burnt), referring to the eggshells mentioned on the previous page.</note> is blown in for a nosebleed; when washed, it also cleanses the teeth.
