Goose and peacock egg membranes work in a similar way.

Moreover, a roasted egg of the **Ibis** expels the fetus <note>The Ibis was a sacred bird in Egypt; its eggs were here used as an abortifacient.</note>. **Crow’s** eggs, when smeared on the testicles, induce pleasure, and when drunk, they create love. In the same way, the eggs of a **Swallow** both blacken the hair and [clear] white spots in the eyes <note>original: *albugines*</note>.

Eating **Pigeon** eggs provides vigor <note>likely referring to sexual potency or stamina; original: *intensionem*</note>. **Partridge** eggs dissolved with honey sharpen the vision and also accelerate childbirth. The eggs of an **Ostrich** <note>original: *Struthio cameli*</note>, when smeared on, benefit those suffering from <term>gout<gloss>a form of inflammatory arthritis</gloss></term> <note>original: *podagricis*</note>.

**Spider** eggs, when drunk—three for a <term>tertian fever<gloss>a fever that returns every third day, often malaria</gloss></term>, four for a <term>quartan fever<gloss>a fever that returns every fourth day</gloss></term>—also cast out a <term>quotidian fever<gloss>a daily recurring fever</gloss></term> <note>original: *amphimerinam*</note> and every type of fever. An odd number of **Chicken** eggs boiled in the urine of a donkey and eaten cure those with kidney ailments and <term>colic<gloss>severe pain in the abdomen caused by wind or obstruction</gloss></term>.

# KIRANI. 141

The Eagle-ray <note>original: "Aeros," likely a transcription error for "Aetos"</note> is a fish without scales, similar to the hawk-fish, yet darker; it is somewhat similar to the stingray <note>Greek: τρυγόνι (*trygoni*)</note> because of its sting <note>Greek: κέντρov (*kentron*)</note>. Therefore, the stones of this fish, which are found in its head, cure quartan fevers <note>a form of malaria where fever recurs every four days</note> when worn as an amulet. Its bile, moreover, sharpens the sight when applied as an ointment. Its spines, when burned over vine branches, cast out demons. Furthermore, eating the fish perfectly cures epilepsy. **THE ANTHIAS** <note>likely the sea-perch or barberfish</note> is a large fish. Its bile, applied as an ointment with honey, cures skin eruptions and restores a blooming complexion. Its fat, however, when mixed with a wax salve, heals carbuncles, <term>fatty tumors<gloss>original: stearemata; sebaceous cysts or fatty swellings</gloss></term>, abscesses, ailments of the breasts and <term>scrofula<gloss>a swelling of the lymph nodes, often associated with tuberculosis</gloss></term>, as well as <term>boils<gloss>original: dothienas</gloss></term>. The stones from its head, when hung from the neck, cure headaches and all ailments of the head and neck. **THE AMIA** <note>likely the Atlantic bonito</note> is a bold fish that even hunts other fish. Its teeth, when worn, cause children's teeth to emerge without pain, and eating the fish cures <term>dysuria<gloss>painful or difficult urination</gloss></term>.

**THE ACHARNI** <note>small fish, possibly a type of sea-bream</note> are small fish; their essence softens the belly and is beneficial to the kidneys.

**THE LOBSTER** <note>original: "Astacus"</note> is known to everyone. Give its shell, burned and dissolved in rice water, to those suffering from dysentery or <term>abdominal distress<gloss>original: coeliacos; those suffering from chronic digestive or bowel complaints</gloss></term>, and it will heal them.

The Skate or Ray <note>original: "Batos" or "Vatos"</note> is a fish without scales, which the Romans call the *culpelna*. Eating this fish frequently stirs up sexual desire <note>original: "Venerem," referring to Venus</note>. **THE SOLE** <note>original: "Buglossa," literally "ox-tongue"</note> is a marine fish which is called *Skitopmia*. This fish, when placed over the spleen and bandaged, [reduces] the spleen

<meta>The catchword "minuit" (reduces/diminishes) completes the sentence on the following page, indicating that the sole fish treatment reduces the size of an enlarged spleen.</meta>

# KIRANIDES

*it diminishes [the ailment] in a natural way; it is necessary, however, to hang it in the smoke after three days.*

**LEECHES** <note>original: *Bdellarum*, that is, *sangvisugarum*</note>. Dissolve the ash of burnt leeches in vinegar; if you pluck out the hairs of the eyelids and smear this on, they will not grow back again. However, when applied alive to a place where <term>matter<gloss>the "humors" or fluids thought to cause disease in pre-modern medicine</gloss></term> is overabundant, they evacuate it and make the person healthy. They are therefore suitable for those with **spleen disorders, dropsy** <note>swelling caused by excess fluid</note>, and **rheumatic eyes** <note>watery or inflamed eyes</note> when applied to the forehead. When used as a <term>fumigant<gloss>burned so the smoke reaches the affected area</gloss></term>, they kill <note>Greek: *koridas*</note> **bedbugs**; conversely, bedbugs used as a fumigant drive out swallowed leeches, for they cannot tolerate one another.

**THE BLANIUS** <note>perhaps read as the Greek *blaneias*</note> *is a river fish.* Its head, when burned and dissolved with honey and smeared on, provides the sharpest vision; its bile also does the same.

**THE BOBES**, though others call them *Buscos*, are fish similar to small **mullets** <note>original: *cephalis*</note>. These are beneficial for the kidneys. Their bile sharpens the sight. Their spines, when burned and made into a **dry powder** <note>original: *Xerion*</note>, heal ulcers.

**EARTH-GUTS** <note>original: *Gisentera*</note>, that is, the intestines of the earth, or **EARTHWORMS**, when applied, perfectly heal twisted nerves. They are also effective for abscesses of the breasts and they dissolve gangrene; when crushed with honey and applied, they heal scorpion bites and the sting of the <term>moray eel<gloss>original: muraena</gloss></term>, a sea fish. When applied to **toothaches**, they are a powerful remedy. If cooked with oil until they dissolve, the resulting oil, when rubbed on, heals earaches. **THE CONGER** <note>original: *Gongrus*</note>, is a sea fish similar to an eel. **THE CATFISH** <note>original: *Glaneus*</note> is a river fish. Its burnt bones put demons to flight; its bile, when smeared on, cures white spots in the eyes <note>original: *albulas*, referring to cataracts or corneal scarring</note>; its liver, when eaten, cures **epilepsy**. The whole fish is <term>easy to digest<gloss>original Greek: *eusymachos*</gloss></term>. 

**THE GLAUCUS** <note>a name used for various gray-colored sea fish</note> is a very large sea fish. When cooked with vegetables and fennel and eaten, and its broth is drunk, it produces much milk; its bile darkens the <term>blue-gray cloudiness<gloss>original Greek: *glaukophthalmian*, likely referring to a specific type of cataract or eye discoloration</gloss></term> in children and cures white spots. Both its eyes, and those of the **sea-perch** <note>original: *orphi*</note> and the **tuna** <note>original: *thynniae*</note> similarly, as well as the **starfish** and the bile of a **hyena**, crushed and mixed...

# KIRANIDES

...when [mixed] with the fat of any kind of animal or bird and lit in a lamp, those looking will think they see that wild animal; for example, if the fat is from a river creature, they will see a river creature, and if from a feathered creature, they will see a feathered one. Moreover, take a whole **starfish** <note>original: *Astera*</note> and crush it.

*The fat of the Glaucus fish is useful for many things, and especially for ailments of the anus and the womb.*

**THE DOLPHIN** gives birth to live young <note>an accurate biological observation for a medieval text</note> and is a marine animal. It is very abundant around the **Black Sea** <note>original: *Euxinum Pontum*</note>, from which **dolphin-oil** <note>original: *delphinelaion* (δελφινέλαιον)</note> and **fish-glue** <note>original: *ichthyokolla* (ἰχθυοκόλλα)</note> are made. If its womb is anointed (or, as others say, inflated and turned) and placed toward the **North** <note>original: *itibarcton*, a corruption of the Greek *aparktian* (ἀπαρκτίαν)</note>, it causes the **North Wind** <note>original: *Boreas*</note> to blow; if placed toward the south, it similarly brings the **South Wind** <note>original: *Auster*</note>, and it works the same way for other winds. Its teeth, when hung from the neck, are beneficial for children who are teething. Its stomach, when dried, ground, and drunk, heals those with spleen disorders. Its liver, *roasted and eaten, perfectly cures **tertian** and **quartan** fevers <note>periodic fevers occurring every three or four days, often associated with malaria</note>, as well as nightly fevers (or perhaps daily or <term>quotidian fevers<gloss>original: *amphimerinum*</gloss></term>) and every kind of fever-cycle.*

**THE DRAGON FISH** <note>original: *Draco*, likely the Greater Weever, a fish known for its venomous dorsal spines</note> is a very poisonous fish. When split open and applied to its own sting, it heals the wound. Its spine <note>original: *centrum* or *aculeus*; some sources say "brain"</note>, when burned and placed upon a tooth with the juice of **spurge** <note>original: *tithymalli*, a plant with a caustic milky sap</note>, pulls the tooth out by the roots. The stones found in its head, when drunk, [cure] the passing of stones <note>kidney or bladder stones</note>...

...they cure. If, however, it is reduced to ash and sprinkled upon them, it cures ulcers resulting from its wound; it also cures when dissolved with sulfur <note>original: *sulphure*; some versions read *furfure*, meaning "bran"</note>.

**THE EEL** <note>original: *Enchelys*</note> is a fish similar to a serpent, without scales; that is, **THE EEL** <note>original: *Angvilla*</note>. If you take its liver along with the gall, dissolve it in wine, and secretly give it to someone to drink, they will never be able to drink wine again <note>a common folk remedy for alcoholism in antiquity</note>. Furthermore, this eel, if killed in wine, creates a drink that prevents drunkenness, provided you give the person a little of the wine in which it was killed. When roasted and eaten, it heals those suffering from stomach ailments and <term>dysentery<gloss>a severe form of diarrhea</gloss></term>.

**THE ELOPS** <note>original: *Elops*; a large fish mentioned by ancient writers, possibly a sturgeon or a type of wrasse</note> is a large fish, similar to the Glaucus. its fat and gall cure all dimness of the eyes. Its liver, when roasted and eaten, cures those with liver diseases. In its head, moreover, are stones <note>likely referring to otoliths, or "ear stones," found in fish heads</note> which, when hung around the neck, cure every headache and <term>migraine<gloss>original: *hemicraniam*, meaning pain on one side of the head</gloss></term>. The stone from the right side of the head should be used for the right side of the patient, and the left for the left. Its eyes, when worn as an amulet, cure all eye inflammations and migraines.

**SEA URCHINS** <note>original: *Echini*</note>, that is, "sea-hedgehogs"; their meat, when eaten, softens the bowels and is an excellent cure for the <term>passing of stones<gloss>urinary or bladder stones</gloss></term> when taken with <term>spiced wine<gloss>original: *condito*</gloss></term>. Furthermore, the urchin itself, when burnt and reduced to a fine ash and applied as an ointment, heals leprosy and restores every ulcer to a healthy scar and firmness. It also heals <term>alopecia<gloss>hair loss or thinning</gloss></term> and thickens the hair when applied with the fat of a bear or a dolphin—or perhaps <term>piglet fat<gloss>original: *delphacio* (δελφακίῳ)</gloss></term>—or pork fat.

**THE REMORA** <note>original: *Echeneis*</note> is a most powerful fish. If this fish should adhere to a sailing ship...

# KIRANIDES

...[sailing] with a favorable wind; it stops the ship. **Take, therefore**, a living Remora <note>original: *Echeneidem*; a fish famous in antiquity for its supposed ability to stop ships by clinging to the hull</note> and put it into <term>smoke-oil<gloss>original: *capnelæum*; identified later in the text as naphtha or petroleum</gloss></term> so that it is suffocated. When you wish to cook it, weigh the fish; for if it weighs one pound, add one <term>sextarius<gloss>an ancient Roman measure, roughly equivalent to a pint</gloss></term> of smoke-oil for every pound of fish, and cook it over a gentle fire. 

When you see that the fish is dissolved and the mixture is dry, strain the juice with the oil and mix three ounces <note>original: *℥ iij*</note> of the finest butter into the juice of the fish and into the <term>Xestes<gloss>the Greek term for a sextarius or pint</gloss></term> of smoke-oil. When it has been well cooked, store it in a glass vessel and use it. You shall anoint the joints of the hands and feet with this; for even if the suffering of <term>gout<gloss>original: *podagra*, specifically gout of the foot</gloss></term> has lasted ten years, it will cure it entirely, as well as <term>hand-gout<gloss>original: *chiragram*</gloss></term> and similarly <term>knee-gout<gloss>original: *genuagram*</gloss></term>. Use it while in bed and after a bath. 

When you cook this, however, take care that it does not boil over too much and spill the smoke-oil, for you will cause a fire. Therefore, cook the prescribed amount in the open air <note>original: *sub divo*</note>, as for example if the fish weighs nine pounds and the butter four ounces. "Smoke-oil," moreover, is that which is called <term>naphtha<gloss>a naturally occurring flammable liquid hydrocarbon</gloss></term>.

It is venomous and aggressive toward humans. The Murena is an adversary to the <term>eight-foot<gloss>original: *Octopedi*, referring to the octopus</gloss></term>, that is, the Polyp, and it kills it; yet the <term>spiny lobster<gloss>original: *Carabus*; a large crustacean</gloss></term> kills the Murena. Thus, if a Murena and a lobster are both cooked together, the Murena vanishes, and does not*

...will appear. The **Octopus** again kills the **Sea-crayfish** <note>original: *Carabum*; likely a spiny lobster or large crustacean</note>. Therefore, the suspended teeth of the **Moray Eel** <note>original: *Zmyrænæ*; the Mediterranean moray</note> are suitable for children who are cutting their teeth <note>used as a protective amulet or teething aid</note>; moreover, if eaten in a peppery broth, it heals <term>kidney-sufferers<gloss>original: *nephriticos*</gloss></term>, and it perfectly cures leprosy and <term>scabrous conditions<gloss>original: *ſcabioſas paſſiones*; itchy skin diseases like scabies</gloss></term>.

The **Liver-fish** <note>original: *Hepar*; likely the monkfish or anglerfish, known for its oversized liver</note> is a soft and sluggish fish, having a large liver. Its gall, drunk with <term>honey-water<gloss>original: *melicrato*; a mixture of honey and water, sometimes fermented</gloss></term>, heals <term>liver-sufferers<gloss>original: *epaticos*</gloss></term>; however, its liver, when ground and applied as a poultice, heals all swelling and <term>gout<gloss>original: *podagram*</gloss></term>. Its head, burnt and ground, and its ashes sprinkled on, heals old ulcers and flesh-eating sores.

If anyone grinds the eyes and lungs of a **Tuna** <note>original: *Thynni*</note> and scatters them in a house late in the evening, those who are in the house will think they see stars. If, however, you anoint a staff and walk about late, when the moon is not shining, they will think a light is flashing from the staff. Truly, if you paint a wild beast or some image on a wall or on paper with it, those seeing it will marvel <note>this refers to bioluminescence; the glowing effect of certain decaying fish tissues</note>. However, the gall of a **Female Tuna** <note>original: *Thynniæ*</note> and its liver, ground and mixed, and smeared on the eyelids after the hairs have been plucked out, do not allow them to grow back.

**SHAD** <note>original: *THRISSA*</note>—some call it *copla*, while others say it is the **Hair-fish** <note>original: *Trichias*, from the Greek word for hair, *thrix*</note>. This **Shad**, when salted and eaten, heals <term>difficulty in urinating<gloss>original: *dyſuriam*</gloss></term>. Also, when burnt and smeared on with an ointment of iris, it makes the hair thick and woolly and stops it from falling out. Roasted and eaten, it is beneficial for those suffering from <term>colic<gloss>abdominal pain</gloss></term> and stomach ailments.

# KIRANIDES

The **Horse-tail** <note>original: *Hippurus*; likely the mahi-mahi or dorado, known for its vibrant colors</note> is a fish which some call the **Prince** <note>original: *Coryphæum*; literally "the Head" or "the Chief"</note> or the Crested-one. Its gall, smeared on with <term>smokeless honey<gloss>original: *melle sine fumo*; honey clarified or harvested without using smoke to drive away the bees</gloss></term>, heals every dullness, darkness, or cloudiness of the eyes. It possesses the exact same medicinal power as the gall of a hyena <note>hyena gall was a prized ancient remedy for cataracts and vision loss</note>. The **Sea Seahorse** <note>original: *Hippocampus marinus*</note>, known to everyone, is very suitable and sweet to eat. This creature, when burned and crushed into pig fat, excellently cures <term>hair loss<gloss>original: *alopeciam*</gloss></term>. When dissolved with the leaves of a wild black fig or the ashes of butcher's broom <note>original: *ruscæ*</note> in vinegar, it cures white-spots <note>likely vitiligo or skin depigmentation</note> and leprosy. Furthermore, when tied around the body as an amulet, it calms <term>sciatica<gloss>original: *ischiadicam*</gloss></term> and benefits those suffering from tremors.

The **Rainbow Wrasse** <note>original: *Julis piscis*; a colorful Mediterranean fish</note> is a variegated fish known to all. Its teeth, when carried on one's person, drive away demons and <term>evil-eye spells<gloss>original: *fascinationes*</gloss></term>. If eaten frequently, it heals <term>epilepsy<gloss>original: *epilepsiam*</gloss></term> and keeps the person unharmed.

The burnt head of the **Mullet** <note>original: *Kephali*; likely from the Greek *kephalos* meaning "head"</note>, when smeared on with honey, cures <term>hemorrhoids<gloss>original: *ficus de sede* (literally "figs of the seat") and *exochadas* (external piles)</gloss></term> as well as similar sores elsewhere on the body. The head of a **Young Tuna** <note>original: *pelamydiæ*</note> has the same effect; it is best to use a mixture of both. The gall of the **Raven-fish** <note>original: *Korasci*; likely the *Coracinus* or brown meager</note>, cooked with honey, heals dullness of vision. A mixture of the gall of this fish, a raven, a partridge, a **Parrotfish** <note>original: *scari*</note>, and a **Scorpionfish**, applied equally with honey and <term>balsam oil<gloss>original: *opobalsamo*</gloss></term>, is a supreme remedy for the failing vision of the elderly, as is the gall of a vulture.

# KIRANIDES

The burnt teeth of a **Sea-dog** <note>original: *Canis marini*; likely a dogfish or a small shark</note>, when dissolved with honey, cleanse the gums. However, its skin, when worn, puts land dogs to flight. The **Carp** <note>original: *Kyprinus*; a common freshwater fish</note>: the fat and liver of this fish, when used as a fumigant <note>the practice of burning a substance so the patient inhales or sits in the smoke</note>, drive away demons. Its gall, used as an ointment, heals all darkness of the eyes. Furthermore, its fat is a stimulant for **sexual desire** <note>original: *Veneris*; referring to Venus, the goddess of love</note>. If someone melts the fat and anoints their private parts in a bath, it provides a healthy complexion and ensures immediate conception after intercourse. The **Komos**-fish <note>original: *Komos*; likely a species of barbel or similar river fish</note>, when cooked with water and salt until it dissolves, and the broth is drunk (especially with milk), acts as a powerful laxative for the bowels. The broth of **Thrush-fish** <note>original: *Kichlae*; colorful wrasses, so named because their spotted patterns resemble the bird</note> softens the belly, promotes good digestion, incites **sexual desire**, and draws out milk for nursing mothers.

The **Sea-shrimp** <note>original: *Karis marina*; also known as the **prawn** <gloss>original: *gammarus*</gloss></note>, when tied around a wound, heals those stung by a scorpion. Similarly, if you write the word "**Shrimp**" <note>original: *Karis*</note> above the afflicted spot, it removes the pain more quickly; it possesses an efficacy of this kind. Indeed, if anyone carves a **Shrimp** <note>original: *Karida*</note> into an **Agate** stone and wears it in a ring, he will never be stung by a scorpion.

**Crabs** <note>original: *Karkini*; specifically freshwater or river crabs</note>, when ground up and drunk with goat's milk, heal those stung by a scorpion, or bitten by a spider, a deadly serpent, or a **horned viper** <note>original: *cerasto*; the Cerastes snake</note>. When given in a drink of dark wine to a woman in difficult labor, they accelerate the birth. When ground up and bound over the puncture wounds of darts or needles, they [draw out] an iron sword, and—

->### KIRANI. 151<-

...they stop burns and cure scars. When smeared on with honey, they thin thick eyelids and [treat] cataracts <note>white opacities on the eye; original: *leucomata*</note>, and **Whelks** <note>original: *Kirykes*; a type of sea snail</note> have the same use; however, one ought to wash the ash with water.

The marine **Electric Ray** <note>original: *Narca*; known for its numbing sting, used here as a primitive anesthetic</note>, when applied to chronic ailments, soothes them and mitigates violent headaches, and when applied, it wonderfully reduces a prolapse of the anus.

**Xuthos** is a fish which some call the **Picarel** <note>original: *Smarida*</note>: its head, when dried and burnt, restrains overgrown flesh <note>likely "proud flesh" in wounds</note>, ulcers, and ailments, and consumes corns and tumors <note>original: *phymos*</note>. For the bite of a scorpion or a dog, it is suitable raw, just as it is suitable for all ailments when dried.

The **Sea-Ass** <note>original: *Onos*</note>, which some call an **Octopus** <note>original: *polypum*</note>, others an eight-foot. Place this into a new pot while it is still living and make it boil, and the dark fluid <gloss>otherwise: blackness</gloss> which comes out of it, give with old wine in a bath to those with kidney disease or those passing stones; they will indeed be healed and will pass the stones.

**Clams** <note>original: *Pelorides*; large bivalve mollusks</note> are small marine creatures; the broth of these and of **Crabs** <note>original: *pagurūm*</note> softens the belly <note>acts as a laxative</note> when taken with wine.

The **Sea-Lung** <note>original: *Pulmonarius* or *cinus marinus*; a type of jellyfish</note>, when placed upon the feet, heals gout <note>original: *podagricū*</note> and chilblains <note>original: *chimethla*</note>. Moreover, it frequently causes all the birds of the sky to fly around it, so that when it is eaten by them, it captures them <note>likely a reference to the stinging tentacles of the jellyfish or its iridescent appearance</note>. Indeed, its bones <note>likely referring to the hard parts of similar marine creatures or perhaps cuttlebone</note>, when used as a fumigant...

->### KIRANIDES 151<-

...they ward off all evils, just as the star-fish <note>original: *stella .i. aster*</note> [does].
The intestines of the **YOUNG TUNA** <note>original: *Pelamydis*; a small tuna or bonito</note> along with the head, when burnt and sprinkled over, heal sores <note>original: *pasturas*; likely skin eruptions or grazing wounds</note>. The ash of the **PERCH** <note>original: *Percae*</note>, when sprinkled over, heals the rot of ulcers and of the mouth. The broth of the **ROCKFISH** <note>original: *Petreos*; fish that frequent rocky shores</note>, eaten with pepper, benefits those with stomach ailments and cures difficulty in urinating <term>dysuria: painful or difficult urination</term>; for its broth is truly excellent.

The **Needlefish** <note>original: *Raphis*, literally "the seam" or "stitch"</note> is a fish which is called a *belonis* <note>Greek: βελονίς, meaning "needle"</note>, having a small, long mouth similar to the **Barracuda** <note>original: *sphyrænæ*</note>. Its mouth, when worn [as an amulet] or used in fumigation <note>the practice of burning a substance so the patient inhales the smoke</note>, casts out demons. Moreover, the whole fish, crushed with iris-scented ointment, powerfully thickens [hair lost to] mange <note>original: *alopeciam*; patch hair loss</note>.

The burnt and crushed skin of the **ANGELSHARK** <note>original: *Rinas*; though the text glosses this as *Ranæ pellis* (frog skin), in this context it refers to the rough-skinned Monkfish or Angelshark</note>, applied and smeared on with water, heals growths <note>original: *nascentias*; tumors or boils</note>.

Regarding the **SALEMA** <note>original: *Salpes*; a Mediterranean fish known in antiquity for causing vivid hallucinations when eaten</note>, the stones found in its head, when worn—the right one indeed creates persistence <note>or "urgency"; original: *instantiam*</note>, while the left one does the opposite; however, its fat, when rubbed on, brings great delight.

The bile of the **LIZARD-FISH** <note>original: *Sauri*</note>, smeared upon the breasts, contributes much to [the production of] milk.

The shells of **CUTTLEFISH** <note>original: *Sepiarum*; the internal bone of the cuttlefish, often used as an abrasive</note> cleanse the eyelids and thin the hairs of the body, just as pumice [does]. Therefore, take those which are found on the shore; when boiled and eaten, they heal the bladder, the kidneys, and difficulty in urinating.

The teeth of the **DENTEX** <note>original: *Synagridos*; a large-toothed sea bream</note> assist with the teething [of infants] and all toothaches; its bile also, with almond oil, soothes the pain of teeth.

->### KIRANIDES 155<-

**SARGO** <note>original: *Sargi*; the White Seabream</note> teeth, when worn [as an amulet], ward off all toothache.

Regarding the **RED MULLET** <note>original: *Triglæ*</note>: if anyone cuts off its "beard" <note>original: *barbam*; the sensory chin-barbels characteristic of the red mullet</note> while it is still alive, and releases the fish back into the sea alive, and gives that beard to a woman in a drink, it will ignite great love and delight in her. Furthermore, the beard, when worn, provides good fortune and every kind of benefit. If someone rubs its eyes and anoints the eyes of another person with them, that person will soon suffer from dimness of vision. The remedy <note>original: *solutio*</note> for this is the bile of the fish smeared on with honey, which sharpens the sight. The whole Red Mullet, when burnt and dissolved with honey and applied, eradicates "coals" <term>carbuncles: severe, inflamed skin boils or clusters of furuncles</term> and heals them perfectly. Its liver, when applied to the site, heals the wounds of the **STINGRAY**, the **WEEVER FISH** <note>original: *draconis*; specifically the venomous Greater Weever</note>, and the **MORAY EEL**. Truly, if the fish is killed in wine and that wine is drunk, it helps women in difficult labor. Its broth, when drunk, helps those who have swallowed harmful potions <note>likely referring to toxins or poisons</note>.

The spine or stinger of the **STINGRAY** <note>original: *Trigonis*</note>, if driven into a tree, withers it. If you fasten it into a house that is being built, or into a ship, it will become unlucky and will not be completed; and if it is already in a finished house or ship, the structure will suffer hindrance. For it is in every way a cause of malice and injury. But also, if you prick around aching teeth with it, it will heal them. <note>original: *NB. Hic in Ms. idem quod p. 144...*; the transcriber notes that the manuscript here erroneously repeated a passage about the Weever fish's spine that had already appeared on page 144.</note>

->### KIRANIDES 156<-

The **HYDROS** <note>original: *Ydros*; a water snake, though sometimes conflated with the mythical Hydra</note> is a serpent that lives mostly in the fields and frequently swims on top of the water in marshes, holding its chest upright; it is a clever animal. This creature has a stone in its head; if anyone catches it, they will find the stone, provided it is exorcised so that the snake vomits it out. I tell you, hang the serpent up alive and fumigate it with laurel, conjuring it thus:

> *By Him who created you, whom your forked tongue prays to as is fitting: if you give me the stone, I will not harm you; indeed, I will return you to your kin.*

And after it has vomited the stone, collect it in a clean silk cloth and keep it. The power of this stone is proven in this way: fill a bronze vessel with water and tie the stone to the vessel; you will find that the water decreases every day by two **cotylae** <term>cotyla: an ancient unit of liquid volume, roughly half a pint or 0.27 liters</term>, which is equal to one **xestes** <note>original: *ξέσης*; a Greek measure equivalent to the Roman sextarius, approximately 0.5 liters</note>. 

For I once tied the stone around a woman suffering from **dropsy** <term>hydropicæ: an old medical term for edema, the painful swelling of soft tissues due to excess fluid</term>, and she was cured. I measured her belly with a rush <note>a stalk of a marsh plant used as a flexible measuring tape</note>, and every day the swelling decreased by more than four fingers, until her belly returned to its proper place, and then I removed it. For if I had left the stone on any longer, I would have dried out even her natural internal moisture. 

I tell you, the stone is effective when worn according to measure. Indeed, when carried, it is beneficial not only for those with dropsy, but also for those with **rheumatism of the feet** <note>original: *rhewmatipedum*; likely referring to gout or inflammatory joint pain</note>, for a constant flow of tears, for the head, and furthermore for every limb suffering from **rheumatism**... <note>the text breaks off here at the word *rhewmat...*</note>

<summary>This page describes the ritual extraction of a magical stone from the head of a water snake (Hydros) and its potent ability to absorb or "dry out" excess fluids from the body. It highlights a clinical case where the stone was used to treat a woman's dropsy (edema) through controlled contact.</summary>

<keywords>Hydros, water snake, magical stones, dropsy, edema, sympathetic medicine, ancient measurements, rheumatism, exorcism</keywords>

...suffers with the one being treated, and by its natural properties cures excessive moisture.

**THE SEAL** <note>original: *Phoca*</note> is a marine animal of which much has often been said. The fat of this creature, when used as a fumigant <note>the practice of inhaling or being exposed to medicinal smoke</note>, continuously aids those in a lethargic state and those suffering from constriction of the womb. A portion of seal rennet <term>coagulum: a substance used to curdle milk, often taken from the stomach of an animal and used in ancient medicine</term>, about the size of a bitter vetch seed <term>orobus: the bitter vetch, used as a standard for small measurements</term>, heals those suffering from **quartan fever** <term>a form of malaria where fever recurs every fourth day</term> when taken as a drink; it similarly cures **opisthotonos** <term>a severe muscle spasm that causes the back to arch and the head to bend backward</term> when drunk. Likewise, if anyone carries the rennet of a seal, they will overcome all their adversaries in a legal dispute. If you tie a strap made of its skin around a bowl and give a drink from it to those who have been bitten by a rabid dog or a person afflicted with "water-madness" <note>original: *lymphatico*; refers to a state of frenzy or hydrophobia</note>, they will be healed immediately. Furthermore, wherever its skin is placed—whether in a house or on a ship—or if it is worn, no evil will happen; for it wards off lightning, storms, dangers, the **evil eye** <term>fascinum: a curse or spell cast by a malevolent glare</term>, demons, wild beasts, thieves, and attacks in the night. It is also necessary to have a stone of sea coral along with it. It is also beneficial for those suffering from **gout** <term>podagricis: people afflicted with podagra, or gout of the foot</term> to wear shoes made from its skin. Its nostril hairs, when carried along with its heart, bring great fortune and favor.

**THE GILT-HEAD BREAM** <note>original: *Chrysophri*; from the Greek for "golden brow," referring to the *Sparus aurata* fish</note>, also known as the Golden-Stones, has stones found in its head that heal those suffering from **consumption** <term>phthisicos: people suffering from phthisis, a wasting disease usually identified as pulmonary tuberculosis</term> when tied around them. Its gall <note>bile</note>, when smeared on the member with an ointment, provides lust and delight during sexual intercourse. Its eyes, however, heal **tertian fever** <term>a fever that recurs every third day</term> and all forms of **ophthalmia** <term>severe inflammation of the eye</term>;

-># THE KIRANIDES<-

**THE TURTLE** <note>original: *Chelones*; here referring to the sea turtle</note>, that is, the blood of the sea turtle, is a medicine <note>original: *pharmacum*, meaning a drug or remedy</note> against all wild beasts, and it magnificently heals the bites of asps and every kind of wild animal. Its eggs, when eaten, cure **lunatics** <term>epileptics: in ancient medicine, epilepsy was often associated with the phases of the moon</term>. Also, its urine, when drunk, heals those bitten by an asp or a viper <note>original: *echidna*</note>. The blood of the **TERRESTRIAL TURTLE** <note>the land tortoise</note>, when drunk, cures **epileptics** and is of the greatest help to those struck by a viper or a scorpion. Indeed, when smeared upon the head, it heals **alopecia** <term>patchy hair loss</term> and washes away **scurf** <term>furfures: dandruff or scales on the skin</term>. When dissolved with the slough of a snake <note>the shed skin of a serpent</note> and vinegar, it heals earaches and burns. Its gall, combined with honey, is of the highest benefit for scars and **white spots on the eye** <term>leucomata: white opacities or cataracts on the cornea</term>. Truly, the whole creature, when burnt and taken with honey, continuously cleanses very old white spots on the eye; and when applied as an ointment, it heals pain and **cloudy vision** <note>original: *nubeculas*, literally "little clouds"</note>. Furthermore, when dissolved with old butter, it cures **carbuncles** <term>painful, pus-filled skin boils</term>.

The **MARSH TURTLE**, which is called the **Emydus** <note>original Greek: *emys heleios*, the European pond turtle</note>, has blood that heals **migraines** <note>original: *hemicraniam*</note> and every pain of the head when smeared upon the forehead. Its ash, when applied with **rose cerate** <term>ceroto ro.: a wax-based ointment made with rose oil</term>, is highly effective at healing burns, **erysipelas** <term>a bacterial skin infection characterized by large red patches</term>, and "hot" **gout** <term>podagra: painful inflammation of the foot, specifically the big toe</term>. Indeed, the ash, when sprinkled over, stops every eruption of blood, whether from the nose or from wounds. Moreover, the blood of the marsh turtle, when smeared upon the head, greatly heals chronic **headaches** <note>original: *cephalalgias*</note>. The blood of the sea turtle also aids those struck by a viper <note>original: *echea* or *echidna*</note> both when drunk and when smeared upon the ulcers; and when seasoned with porridge and drunk...

-># THE END OF THE KIRANIDES. 159<-

It heals **shortness of breath** <term>orthopnea: a condition where one can only breathe comfortably when sitting or standing upright</term> and those who have consumed something harmful. **Tortoise-stones** <note>original: *Chelonitides lapides*; mythical stones said to be found in the heads of tortoises</note>, when worn with peony root, make a person perfectly self-possessed. Its gall <note>bile</note>, when smeared on, casts away all darkness from the eyes. Its liver, when drunk, heals those with **jaundice** <term>icterus: a yellowing of the skin and eyes caused by liver or gallbladder issues</term>. The fat of the **CHILO** fish, dissolved with the juice of fenugreek and applied to the lips and the **soles of the feet** <note>original Greek: *pelmasin*; Latin gloss: *plantis*</note>, heals cracks and chapping most effectively. The **CHANNUS** <note>a species of sea perch or comber</note>, when roasted and eaten very frequently, provides sharp eyesight. The **CHELIDON** <note>meaning "swallow"; referring to the flying fish</note> is a small fish that flies over the waves during storms; when many of these emerge together and then dive back down, sailors know they are predicting a coming wind and storm. If anyone catches this fish and carries it dried upon a ship, the vessel will travel swiftly, and the person will be successful in all matters.

**PSARI** <note>original Greek term for fish; here glossed as "excellent fish"</note>, which, when eaten frequently, create beauty and provide a healthy stomach.

The **PSYLLUS** <note>the "sea-flea," likely a type of small shrimp or sand hopper</note>; if you boil them in seawater with the herb **fleabane** <note>original: *coniza*</note> and sprinkle it in a house that has fleas, they will no longer exist there. If a fisherman carries the sea-flea with him, he will obtain a great catch of fish.

Fish eggs, preserved and dried, when eaten, cure all weariness and disease, and every **aversion to food** <term>fastidium: a loss of appetite or a feeling of loathing toward food</term>, especially the eggs of the **mullet** <note>original: *cephalorum*</note>, **sea bass** <note>original: *lauracum*</note>, **barracuda** <note>original: *sphyraenarum*</note>, and similar fish; whether fresh or dried, they heal all nausea. And indeed, divine nature has bestowed these things for the benefit of mankind through all aerial, terrestrial, and aquatic animals, so that nothing in life might be left without the reception of a gift.
->**OF LIFE.**<-

<summary>This final page of the Kiranides details the medicinal uses of tortoise parts, various fish, and sea-fleas for conditions ranging from jaundice to cracked skin, ending with a philosophical note on nature's providence.</summary>

<meta>Following the conclusion of the previous section on aquatic creatures and turtles, this page introduces a scholarly commentary or "Coronis" (a finishing touch) by the editor Rhyakinus. It marks the transition into the third book of the *Kiranides*, shifting the focus from the sea to the sky.</meta>

->**  **  **  **  **  **<-

# Regarding the Third Book of the KIRANIDES OF KIRANIDES

->Take this, the **CROWNING FINISH BY RHYAKINUS**<-
->of Notes and Various Readings, and<-
->Emendations and Explanations!<-

***NB.** Note: Since few things were noted in Book I due to the lack of paper, the birds of that same book—since many of them recur here in Book III, which is specifically dedicated to birds—will be touched upon in passing. Furthermore, in the finishing section of Book IV, the fishes that were left out will be particularly addressed, and thus many passages from across the work will be clarified here and there.*

*P. 112. The Eagle is the King of all birds.] In the same way, above on page 8, the author began Book I with this same aquiline majesty, just as other writers begin their own treatises on birds <term>ornithologies: the study of birds</term>. This title was also attributed to this bird by Dares Phrygius <note>a legendary historian of the Trojan War</note> in Book 6 of his *History of the Fall of Troy*, and by Josephus <note>a 1st-century Jewish historian</note> in Book 3, Chapter 5 of *The Jewish War*, and others. Similarly, in Homer she is called the "most perfect of winged creatures" <note>original Greek: *teleiotatē peteinōn*</note>, which is to say the "most royal" <note>original Greek: *basilikōtatē*</note>; in Pindar’s Pythian Ode 6, she is the "leader of birds" <note>original Greek: *archos oiōnōn*</note>; and elsewhere she is known as the "Queen of birds" proclaimed by God. She is even called a "Tyrant" <note>original Latin: *Tyranna*</note> by some, because she does not follow a true manner of ruling, but rather oppresses and devours everyone with violent dominion, being feared by all birds and many other animals as an enemy of all. It is strange, therefore, that our author attributes this preeminence to the male eagle alone, unless a nearly different species—the female eagle, named Hyperion—is being imagined.*

<vocab>Kirani Kiranida, Rhyakini Koronida, Aquila, Ornithologias, Dares Phrygius, Josephus, Homerus, Pindarus, Hyperion</vocab>

<summary>This page serves as a scholarly preface to the third book of the Kiranides, providing a commentary on the eagle's status as the "King of Birds" through references to classical authors like Homer, Pindar, and Josephus. The editor, Rhyakinus, explains his plan to consolidate notes on birds and fish to compensate for previous omissions.</summary>
<keywords>Eagle, King of Birds, Kiranides, Rhyakinus, classical literature, ornithology, Hyperion, natural magic</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the editorial commentary by Rhyakinus on the third book of the *Kiranides*. The previous page introduced the Eagle as the "King of Birds," and here the editor critiques the superstitious and cruel rituals associated with it in ancient texts, while citing other Renaissance and Classical authorities like Aldrovandi and Galen.</meta>

16. I sacrifice you for the cure of every infirmity ] <note>original Latin: *Macto te ad omnis infirmitatis curationem*. This appears to be the conclusion of a ritual formula for slaughtering a bird for medicinal use.</note>
I would have preferred to mark this incantation of the Eagle—as well as that of the Water-Snake on page 156, and no less than that of the Donkey above on page 90—with an asterisk or a black theta <note>The Greek letter Theta ($\theta$) was used by ancient scribes as a symbol for death (*thanatos*), often used here to mark passages that should be "killed" or rejected</note> and to have deliberately passed over them; I would have done so unless the entire text in both places would have had to be utterly ruined. It is hoped that no one would be so foolish as to slaughter that bird—which is otherwise quite rare—out of their godless credulity and cruelty for the sake of such highly uncertain cures. Truly, one or two passages had to be left intact for that very reason: so that those superstitious follies of the ancients might be exposed. For in this same way, Alexander the Great—or rather the one who published him, Albertus Magnus <note>a 13th-century Dominican friar and influential scholar</note> in his work *On the Virtues of the Seven Plants*, regarding the influence of the seven planets—writes on page 248: That the person uprooting these herbs ought to name the suffering or the specific matter for which they are being gathered.

P. 113. l. 11. It heals every condition of the head. ] Aldrovandi <note>Ulisse Aldrovandi, a 16th-century Italian naturalist</note> in Book 1, folio 44, reads or translates this as: "Every disorder of the head." In that passage, he transcribed all the sections of the *Kiranides* concerning the brain, wings that speed childbirth, the nerves of the legs that heal knee-gout <term>gonagra: gout specifically affecting the knee joint</term> and foot-gout <term>podagra: gout in the foot, typically the big toe</term>, the bones of the crown of the head that cure one-sided headaches <term>hemicrania: a migraine or intense pain affecting one side of the head</term>, and finally the skin, which is a wonderful help for those with colic or those suffering from stomach ailments, as well as the heart of the eagle preserved with honey and spices.

In the same place. Coughing and diffiniam. ] Read: "cough and shortness of breath" <term>dyspnoea: labored or difficult breathing</term>. For Galen <note>a prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note> also taught the same in *Easy Remedies*, Book 3, Chapter 37: That the tongue of an eagle suspended from the neck in a linen cloth is of the greatest benefit for the windpipe <note>original Latin: *arteria aspera*—the catchword "aspera" on the next page completes this phrase</note>

<summary>The editor Rhyakinus critiques the "superstitious follies" and cruelty of ancient medical rituals involving the sacrifice of eagles, though he retains the text to preserve the historical record. He cross-references these eagle-based cures with the works of noted scholars like Aldrovandi, Albertus Magnus, and Galen.</summary>

<keywords>eagle, medical superstition, ritual sacrifice, Aldrovandi, Albertus Magnus, Galen, gout, migraines, respiratory ailments</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the editorial commentary on Book 3 of the *Kiranides*. Having previously discussed the Eagle, the text now transitions to the medical properties of the "Eagle-stone" (Aetites) and introduces the Vulture and the Harpe bird, comparing their virtues and appearances based on classical and Renaissance authorities.</meta>

*...rough, and it may cause defects of the uvula <note>original: "columellae"; refers to the small fleshy piece hanging at the back of the throat</note>, as well as difficulty of breathing and a cough.*

P. 115. *As many as we said concerning the Eagle.* ]
*This seems to be an interrupted passage, and looks toward that which is said a little later regarding the Vulture:* **As many things as the eagle does, so many also does the vulture.**

*Ibid. line 25* A stone which sounds from within when touched. ] *As cited on page 8, he previously described it: The Aetites <note>The Eagle-stone, a hollow geode with a smaller stone loose inside that rattles when shaken</note>, that is, the eagle-stone, is "pregnant" and resounds. Where perhaps it should be read: it is "heavy" <note>original: "gravis"</note>, or "it is suitable for pregnant women" <note>original: "gravidis aptus est"</note>.* Lucan, in Book 6 of *The Civil War*, likewise depicted the eagle-stone thus:

->And the stones that resound with offspring, warmed beneath the bird.<-
<note>original Latin: *Quaeq; sonant foetû tepefacta sub alite saxa.* Lucan refers to the myth that eagles placed these stones in their nests to protect their eggs.</note>

*The power of this stone for drawing out the offspring is marvelous; for which reason, even today, it is applied to women in labor to accelerate the birth. See Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician</note> Book 5 and Aldrovandi <note>A 16th-century naturalist</note> folio 21, where he adds: that this noble stone, which earned its name from eagles, barely exceeds an egg in size, and is pomegranate-colored, or red, or ash-gray, just as our author also described it.*

P. 116. **The Harpe is a predatory bird, similar to the Vulture, though smaller and more reddish.** ] *Elsewhere on page 25, namely as the Zaucon or Zeucon (the Manuscript says Zenon), which others call the* <term>harpe <note>original Greek: ἄρπην (*arpēn*)</note></term>, *and he named it a vulture that devours white carcasses. Here, Aldrovandi reads "white vulture" carelessly <note>original: "supine"</note>, and therefore accuses our author of contradiction more than is fair.*

<summary>This page discusses the Aetites or "Eagle-stone," detailing its physical properties and its use in traditional medicine to assist in childbirth, while also beginning a description of the Harpe bird and the Vulture.</summary>
<keywords>Aetites, Eagle-stone, midwifery, childbirth, Lucan, Dioscorides, Aldrovandi, Harpe bird, Vulture, ornithology, lapidary medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of bird species in the *Kiranides* and their representation in ancient texts, moving from the eagle to the *harpe* (a bird of prey), the nightingale, and various types of owls.</meta>

...as if he had described the *harpe* <note>A legendary bird of prey, often identified with a hawk or falcon</note> as appearing sometimes like a white vulture and sometimes like a more reddish one. For he did not say: "It is white like a vulture," but rather that it devours white carcasses (where, of course, the bones are most visible). For this reason, some call it the *Ossifraga* <note>Literally the "Bone-breaker," known today as the Bearded Vulture or Lammergeier</note>, as does the commentator on the *Iliad*, Book 3; others call it a species of eagle. From this, I believe it can be reconciled with what *Pliny* recorded in Book 37, Chapter 9: *The vanity of the Magi promised that if eagles were carved into emeralds, they would turn away hail, etc.* Our author, on page 26, determines that this power against every disturbance holds true if the *harpe* bird is carved. But let us leave this to those who, as *Pliny* says, wrote such things not without mocking the human race.

P. 117. *Aëdon* <note>Greek for "nightingale"</note> singing day and night.]
*Hesiod*, as cited by *Aelian* <note>A 3rd-century Roman author of natural history</note>, testifies that the nightingale is the only bird that lacks sleep and is constant in its vigils. It is for this reason, therefore, that our author, in this place and on page 24, asserts that its eyes prevent all sleep, an opinion shared by *Psellus* <note>An 11th-century Byzantine philosopher</note>, *Pliny* (Book 32, Chapter 10), and *Aelian* in *On Animals* (Book 1, Chapter 43).

P. 119. *Buffus*.] In the *Kiranides* <note>An ancient magico-medical text</note>, this is a nocturnal bird; as the translation by *Gerard of Cremona* <note>A prolific 12th-century translator of scientific works</note> has it, it is the *Bubo* <note>The Eurasian eagle-owl</note>. I suspect, however, that the original Greek was written as *byzan* <note>original: βύζαν</note>. For in the works of *Simeon Seth* <note>A 11th-century Byzantine-Jewish physician</note>, *byzai* <note>original: βύζαι</note> are owls; and in certain parts of Italy, the *Bubo* is called *lo Bufo* in the common tongue. See *Aldrovandi* <note>Ulisse Aldrovandi, a famous 16th-century naturalist</note>, Book 8 of *Ornithology*, folio 258.

In the same place. *Gyps* <note>Greek for "vulture"</note>, that is, a vulture: its gall with honey and f. <note>The "f." likely abbreviates a medicinal ingredient like "fimus" (dung) or "felle" (bile), common in these ancient recipes</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of medicinal and symbolic properties of birds, transitioning from owls to the vulture and the woodpecker. ...leek-green medicine <note>original: "prassii"</note>, etc.] This eye-salve <term>collyrium</term> <note>A liquid or semi-liquid medicinal preparation for the eyes</note> for cataracts <note>original: "suffusionem"</note> and blurring of the eyes, which he repeats twice in this passage—made both from chicks and from the vulture itself—he already prescribed on page 42 for all dullness and other ailments of the eyes. This is the same way *Quintus Serenus* <note>Quintus Serenus Sammonicus, a 3rd-century Roman physician and author of a medical poem</note> taught to prepare the medicine:

> If sluggish old age draws shadows over the eyes,
> Drops squeezed from fennel <note>original: "marathro"</note> with liquid honey
> Can wipe away the affliction; or the gall of a black vulture,
> With which the herbs of celandine have been
->mixed.<-

Regarding the vulture's brain as a cure for headache—if the temples are anointed or the nostrils touched with it along with cedar oil—*Galen* <note>The most influential physician of the Roman Empire</note> (Book 9 of *Easily Procured Remedies*, Chapter 91), *Pliny* <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the encyclopedic *Natural History*</note>, and *Marcellus* <note>Marcellus Empiricus, a 4th-century medical writer</note> (Book 1) all confirm this. Indeed, if you read the volumes of the Physicians, you will see there are as many cures in the vulture as there are parts of its body, as *Saint Jerome* <note>A prominent early Christian scholar and translator of the Bible</note> also testifies in Book 2 of *Against Jovinian*.

P. 121. Tree-pecker <note>original: "Δενδρηκολαπτης"</note>] otherwise Woodpecker <note>original: "Δρυοκολαπτης"</note>. I would read it this way because of the added interpretation, if the former name had not already appeared on page 20, element 4, assigned to this bird.

Ibid. *Erodius* <note>A heron</note> nesting and dwelling.] Read: "having."

P. 122. Its "beak" <note>original: "ramfum"</note>, that is, its "claw" <note>original: "ungulam"</note>.] *Aldrovandus* <note>Ulisse Aldrovandi, a famous 16th-century naturalist</note> (Volume 3, page 157) judged that "beak" <note>original Greek: "ramphos"; Latin: "rostrum"</note> should be read instead, and he adds that this was repeated from *Pliny* (Book 30, Chapter 15).

<meta>This page continues the philological and natural history commentary on the Kiranides, moving from the medicinal uses of the vulture and woodpecker to the symbolic and biological characteristics of the crow, the jay, and the blackbird. **P. 123. The heart of a crow carried promotes harmony.]** Same as on page 17, where this bird is said to live for 500 years; with which Aristophanes also agrees: "Do you not know that the chattering crow lives for five generations of men?" <note>original: "πέντε γενεὰς ἀνδρῶν"</note> Hesiod, however, according to Pliny (Book 7, Chapter 48), suggests it equals nine of our lifetimes.

**P. 124. *Kissa* <note>The Greek term for a jay or magpie</note>, that is, the Jay <note>original: "Gaja"</note> or Acorn-eater <note>original: "Glandaria"</note>.]** This is the variegated Magpie <term>Pica</term> <note>The genus including magpies and jays, known for their "pied" or multicolored feathers</note>, for on the sides of its wings it has transverse blue markings, such as you will perhaps see in no other kind of bird. *John Jonston* <note>A 17th-century Polish physician and natural historian</note> in *The Wonders of Birds* (Chapter 28). Hence it is added in the text that it is a bird of varied color; from which that varied appetite of pregnant women is called *Kissa* and *pica* <note>The medical condition "pica," involving cravings for non-food items, derives its name from the magpie's reputation for indiscriminate eating</note>. On this, see *Aldrovandus* <note>Ulysses Aldrovandus, a celebrated 16th-century Italian naturalist</note> (Book 12, *Ornithology*, Chapter 12, page 392). There he says that the Italians call this bird *Gaza* and *Gazzola*, but by Pliny (Book 10, Chapter 42) it was named *Glandaria*. Wherefore the same Aldrovandus (page 389) correctly amended this passage: he says that the interpreter of the *Kiranides*, Cremonensis, translated *kitta* as *Gaja*, or *Gaza*, and *Glandaria*; but *Gaja* and *Glandaria* belong to the second species, while *Gaza* is indeed a common name for both types of magpie.

Same place. *Kossyphos* <note>Greek for blackbird</note>.] and *kottyphos*, or *kossybos* and *kottybos*: a species of bird in Aristotle (Book 9, *History of Animals*, Chapter 19). *Theodorus Gaza* <note>A 15th-century Greek scholar who translated Aristotle into Latin</note> translates it as Blackbird <note>original: "Merula"</note>. On which see also *Aelian* and *Oppian* regarding fowling, and the *Epigrams*.

**P. 125. Bee-eater <note>original: "Merops"</note>.]** Otherwise called the Bank-dweller <note>original: "Riparia"</note> or the Bee-bird <note>original: "Apiaria"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on bird names and medicinal properties found in the Kiranides and the works of Albertus Magnus, specifically focusing on the green woodpecker, quail, and the internal anatomy of storks.</meta>

from which perhaps the corrupted name of the Translator, **Parra** <note>A name for a bird of ill omen, often identified as a lapwing or a barn owl</note>, descends, if you prefer to relate it more to "parum" <note>meaning "little" or "insufficiently"</note> with others. However, just as Albertus Magnus <note>A 13th-century Dominican friar and influential naturalist</note> did, so also before him, the *Kiranides* <note>An ancient magico-medical text on the properties of animals, plants, and stones</note> seems to confuse the Bee-eater <note>original: "Meropem"</note> with the **green woodpecker** <note>original: "pico viridi"</note>. See Aldrovandus <note>Ulysses Aldrovandus, the 16th-century "father of natural history"</note> (Book 12, Chapter 45). From this, I think, comes the fact that for some (Germans, I believe), according to our Author, this bird was called entirely green, **Gangræna**, that is, *gant-grüne* <note>German for "entirely green"</note>.

**P. 127. l. 2. Pulcini**] This is what the Italians call young chickens <note>original: "pulli gallinacei"</note>, or *pullicini*; a word which Lampridius <note>A Roman historian, one of the authors of the Augustan History</note> also used in his life of Alexander Severus, as Aldrovandus reads *pullicinis* (Book 14, folio 89, *Ornithology*).

**ib. Eyes of the Quail.** <note>original: "Ortygis oculi"</note>] page 50. These, along with the root of a herb (Evening Primrose <term>Aenothera</term> <note>A flowering plant often used in traditional medicine</note> or Marshmallow <term>Althea</term> <note>A plant whose roots contain mucilage, used to soothe inflammation</note>; for so Aldrovandus reads instead of "herb" in Book 13, folio 81) heal quartan fever <note>A form of malaria characterized by paroxysms that occur every fourth day</note>.

**P. 127. last line. The Sea-urchin or Hedgehog, which is in the belly of the stork.** <note>original: "Echinum .i. ericium, quod est in ventre pelargi"</note>] Elsewhere he calls this a *suffusium* <note>A term sometimes used for a cataract or an internal lining/membrane</note>, and you should correct page 55 accordingly. Ulysses Aldrovandus (Volume 3, *Ornithology*, folio 125) says regarding this passage: *Pliny recommends the stomach of a stork against all poisons; the Kiranides ascribes this power to the "Echinus," that is, the inner membrane of the stomach.*

**P. 130. l. 15. There is also a tree in India, which is called peridexion.**] Albertus, who also translated this passage among his own works, has *hyperdexion*; the Ornithologist perhaps thinks it should read *peristereon* <note>The Greek name for the herb Vervain, or "pigeon-plant"</note>, but in the judgment of Aldrovandus (Book 15, folio 188), this is an error: since *peristereon* is a herb to Pliny, whereas *peri-*

...but that tree is called **Dexion** <note>original: "dexion," from the Greek for "right-handed"</note> or **Hyperdexion** <note>original: "hyperdexion," meaning "superior to the right"</note>. Therefore, I believe he intended to speak of the **Peridexion** <note>original: "περιδέξιον," a mythical tree from the *Physiologus* that protects doves from dragons</note> tree as if calling it the "ambidextrous tree," because its shadow stretches now toward the East and now toward the West, driving away serpents and serving as a defense for doves. As for how he might have rendered this entire passage differently, look for it in **Aldrovandus** <note>Ulisse Aldrovandi, the 16th-century naturalist</note> in the place mentioned.

**P. 131. And with saffron ointment** <note>original: "crocomaginate"</note>.] So it is in the manuscript, but read as **Krokomagmati** <note>original: "κροκομάγματι"</note> or *crocomagmate*; regarding this, see **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and author of *De materia medica*</note> Book 1, Chapter 26. For **Magma** <note>original: "μάγμα"</note> is the dregs and thick residue which remains from some expressed material.

**P. 132. Ramphios** <note>original: "Ραμφίος"</note>.] **Aldrovandus**, Book 13, folio 40, reads **Ramphos** <note>original: "Ῥάμφος," Greek for "beak"</note>. Indeed, the **Pelican**, he says, is called by another name in the *Kiranides*, specifically *Ramphos*, evidently because of its beak, which is remarkable and the widest of all birds.

**Ib. Seisopygis** <note>original: "Σεισοπυγίς," Greek for "shaking-rump" or wagtail</note>, that is, **Eropa**.] Perhaps it should be read as **Aeropa**, as some call it. For according to **Hesychius** and **Varinus** <note>Ancient Greek lexicographers</note>, "aerones" are also birds. Because it is indeed found near streams of water and torrents, someone had written the German name **Bachsteltze** <note>Modern German: "Bachstelze," literally "brook-stilt," referring to the wagtail</note> in the margin of that passage in our Author; however, **Aldrovandus** (Book 17, Chapter 28) changed this to **Beistertz** <note>A German dialect variant for a bird with a moving tail</note>, seeing as it is called a "motacilla" <note>Latin for "wagtail," from *motare* (to move) and *cilla* (tail)</note> because of its trembling tail. **Tzetzes** <note>A 12th-century Byzantine poet and grammarian</note>, however, called the bird the **Seisopygion** and **Cinclis**, which **Galen** and **Suidas** called the **Kinaidion** <note>Ancient terms for the wagtail or similar small, tail-bobbing birds</note>, just as our author also stated on page 36. **Gaza** <note>Theodorus Gaza, a 15th-century translator of Aristotle</note> calls it the **Torqvilla** <note>The "wryneck" bird, named for its ability to twist its neck</note>, and among Romans today it is the **Ver-**

...ticilla <note>Likely part of the word *Iuncticilla*, a diminutive for the Wryneck bird</note>, which by Caelius <note>Ludovicus Caelius Rhodiginus, a 15th-century philologist</note> is called the *collitorquis* <note>original: "collitorquis," meaning "neck-twister"</note>, and in our native tongue **Wendhals** and **Drehhals** <note>German names for the Wryneck (*Jynx torquilla*), known for its ability to twist its neck nearly 180 degrees</note>. Also see Aldrovandi, Book 17, folio 314, who wrote that this bird is identified by some in the *Kiranides* <note>An ancient magico-medical text</note> as the *Phylincopterus*, where the manuscript had *Phyracopterus*; I, on account of the added gloss, preferred **phoenicopterum** <note>the Flamingo</note>, namely: having crimson wings.

P. 133. Here the **Peacock** stars its wings with many a constellation,
> **And swelling with pompous pride, it balances**
> **Its fierce gait as it goes, and spreads its rays**
> **In a sounding train.** <note>original: "syrmate," referring to a long, trailing robe, here describing the peacock's tail</note>

**Du Bartas** <note>Guillaume de Salluste Du Bartas, a 16th-century poet</note>, Book 5, 177. Peacock dung taken as a drink, as Sextus Empiricus and the *Kiranides* report, wonderfully heals those with the **falling sickness** <note>original: "caducos," an old term for epilepsy</note>. But since the same author of the *Kiranides* is often accustomed to mix fables with truths, when he says that a fumigation of the same dung and entrails drives away every evil, I would think he exceeds the boundaries of truth. Aldrovandi, Book 13, folio 15.

p. 134, **Phēnēs** <note>original: "Φήνης," referring to a type of eagle or vulture, possibly the Ossifrage or Lammergeier</note>.) **Aldrovandi** (Book 2 on Ornithology, chapter 11) denies that this word can be found in the nominative case. He was, I believe, forgetful of his own words, for within a few lines in the same chapter he had previously stated: "This bird is called **Phēnēs** by the Greeks, and **Phēnē** by Aristotle." Correctly, therefore, both for our Author in this place and for Hermolaus Bar- <note>Hermolaus Barbarus, a Renaissance scholar known for his corrections to Pliny</note>

<meta>This page continues the philological and natural history commentary on the *Kiranides*, specifically concluding the section on birds and moving into the final summary or "Koronis" regarding aquatic creatures. It maintains the previous page's focus on identifying species through the works of Renaissance naturalists like Aldrovandi and Scaliger.</meta>

Barbaro <note>Hermolao Barbaro (1454–1493), a Venetian scholar known for his "Corollarium" or botanical/zoological additions to Dioscorides and Pliny</note> in his *Corollarium* on Pliny, as well as Caelius Rhodiginus <note>Ludovicus Caelius Rhodiginus, an Italian philologist</note> in Book 10, Chapter 6 of his *Ancient Readings*, states that **this bird is called the Phinis** <note>original: *Phinis*, likely referring to the *Phenis* or Bearded Vulture</note>, which is the same as what the Latins call the **Ossifragus** or **Ossifraga** <note>original: *Ossifragus*, literally "the bone-breaker," the Lammergeier or Bearded Vulture</note>. Furthermore, the same Aldrovandi <note>Ulisse Aldrovandi, the famous 16th-century naturalist</note> in the aforementioned place, folio 122, reads the word as **dung** <note>original: *stercus*</note> rather than **stomach** <note>original: *stomachus*</note> in our Author <note>The author of the *Kiranides*</note>, from whom he transferred all the medicinal uses of the **Phenes**.

In the same place, **the Bald One** <note>original: *Φαλακρὸς* (*Phalakros*), Greek for "bald-headed"</note>) The manuscript had *Phalanus*. However, Aldrovandi, looking at this passage, I believe, in Volume 3, folio 40, reads it as **Phalaris** <note>The Coot (*Fulica atra*)</note>; he says that this is the **coot** <note>original: *fulica*</note> mentioned in the *Kiranides*.

Page 137, third line from the bottom. **The Parrot is a bird that is entirely green.**] In the most scorching parts of Ethiopia and the furthest reaches of India, they are entirely **white**; in Brazil, they are **red**; in Calicut, they are **leek-green**, **checkered**, or **purple**. Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger, a 16th-century polymath</note>, *Exercise* 59, section 2. Among the ancients, they were primarily known for their green color. The head and the beak are extremely hard. Thus, when it is taught to speak, it does not feel anything unless it is struck with an **iron rod** <note>original: *radio ferreo*</note>; for this reason, it cannot be confined in wooden cages, and even iron ones are in danger from it. Johannes Jonston <note>A 17th-century Polish naturalist and physician</note> in *Wonders of Birds*, chapter 26.

## THE FINAL CURTAIN-CALL <note>original: *KORONIS postrema*, using the Greek word for a flourish at the end of a book</note> OF THE LAST PART OF THE KIRANIDES

Page 141. **The AETOS is a fish without scales.**] This same description appears above on page 8. Aldrovandi, in Book 1 of his *Ornithology*, chapter 1, folio 12, determined this to be a **cartilaginous fish** of the "Flat" genus <note>original: *ex genere Planorum*, referring to rays and skates</note>. It is as if it were not simply a second species of **stingray** <note>original: *pastinaca*</note> (as some would have it, though Rondeletius <note>Guillaume Rondelet, a 16th-century ichthyologist</note> objects in Book 12 *On Fish*, chapter 2, citing Galen's Book 3 *On the Properties of Foodstuff*), which even today is called the **Eagle** <note>original: *Aquila*</note> by the Romans and Neapolitans; but the stingray...

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological and zoological commentary on the *Kiranides*. While the previous page focused on identifying birds like the "Phinis" (Bearded Vulture) and the "Phalaris" (Coot), this page transitions into the identification and medicinal properties of various fish and sea creatures. The commentator continues to cite major Renaissance naturalists such as Guillaume Rondelet and classical authorities like Pliny, Galen, and Dioscorides to clarify the text.</meta>

...nevertheless, it is born with a similar appearance and is therefore called the **Eagle-fish** <note>original: *Aquila*; likely referring to the Common Eagle Ray, *Myliobatis aquila*</note>, because it imitates an eagle with its wings spread out. Among the people of Genoa, it is called the **Rat-fish** <note>original: *pisce Ratto*</note> due to its thin, long tail that resembles that of a mouse.

**In the same place. THE SEA BREAMS** <note>original: *Acharni*; likely referring to a species of sea perch or bream</note>] Read: the broth of these, not their toughness, softens the bowels; a point which is also repeated below regarding Snails.

**Page 124. THE BOGA** <note>original: *Bobes*, referring to *Boops boops*, a small Mediterranean fish</note>] The translator wrote this according to his own Italian inclination, for in Venice this fish is named the **Booba** (according to Rondelet, Book 5, Chapter 11). However, that name corresponds better to the Greek <term>Bome</term> <note>original: *βώμη*</note>.

**Page 143. THE GLAUCUS** <note>original: *Glaucus*; a name used in antiquity for various silvery or "gray" fish, often a species of sea bass or even a shark</note> **is a very large sea fish.**] The things he brings forward here to display as a marvel, he emphasized slightly differently in Book 1, page 19; let the reliability of these claims, like others, depend upon empirical experience. For what he says about it producing a great quantity of milk is perhaps derived from the similar-sounding Greek word <term>of milk</term> <note>original: *γάλακτος* (*galaktos*)</note>. However, Rondelet (Book 8, Chapter 16) is the authority for the fact that it lacks bile (which the author of the *Kiranides* <note>referred to here as *Kiranus*</note> recommended above). And Ovid <note>original: *Naso* (Publius Ovidius Naso)</note> in his *On Fishing* <note>original: *Halieuticon*; a poem on the nature of fish which the poet Jacopo Sannazaro, also known as Actius Sannazarius, was the first to bring as a manuscript from France to Italy</note> records: *That the Glaucus is never seen under the summer star.*

**Page 144. THE WEEVER-FISH** <note>original: *Draco*, literally "the Dragon"</note> **is a highly venomous fish.**] Galen (Book 11, *On the Properties of Simple Medicines*) and Dioscorides (Book 2, Chapter 15) agree with this. Dioscorides says that the Sea Dragon stings with a venomous spine, and that the sting is cured by the Dragon itself if it is cut open and applied to the wound. Pliny also (Book 32, Chapter 5) asserted that its flesh and brain have the same healing power. Regarding what Albertus Magnus <note>the 13th-century scholar and theologian</note> also wrote following our Author on page 20...

<meta>This page continues the commentary on marine life, specifically addressing misconceptions about the "Sea Dragon" and the legendary ship-stopping powers of the "Remora." It connects classical descriptions from Aelian and Aristotle with Renaissance naturalists like Rondelet and poets like Du Bartas. The catchword "frigido" (cold) at the end of the previous section likely referred to the "cold humor" mentioned in the closing lines of this page regarding the theories of Keckermann.</meta>

...[they] believe that this fish grows so much that it becomes a most violent sea monster. These people, not understanding the etymology of the name <note>The name "Dragon" in Greek (*drakōn*) is derived from *derkesthai*, "to see clearly," referring to the fish's large eyes.</note> (which, according to the witness of **Aelian**, Book 11, Chapter 42, arises from its head, its large eyes, and its dangerous spines, by which it in some way resembles a terrestrial Dragon), when they hear the name **Sea Dragon** <note>original: *Draconem marinum*; likely referring to the Greater Weever fish (*Trachinus draco*), known for its venomous spines</note>, they imagine in their minds something similar to those Dragons that painters depict according to their own whim. See **Rondelet** <note>Guillaume Rondelet (1507–1566), a French physician and naturalist famous for his work on marine life</note>, Book 10, Chapter 11.

**Page 145. THE REMORA** <note>original: *Echeneis*; from the Greek *echein* (to hold) and *naus* (ship)</note>] The holder-back of ships and, as it were, an anchor in mid-course, which above on page 46 was called the **Pilot-fish** <note>original: *Naucrates*</note>; it is likewise praised as a remedy for **gout** <note>original: *podagram*; a form of inflammatory arthritis</note>, just as it is commended here. **Guillaume de Salluste Du Bartas** <note>A Huguenot poet (1544–1590) known for his epic poem on creation, *La Sepmaine*</note>—or rather **Gabriel de Lerme** in twenty-one verses of Book 5, Week 1—described this fish excellently. **Ovid** also wrote in his *Halieutica* <note>A fragmentary Latin poem about fishing</note>:

> The small Remora is present, a wondrous and massive delay for ships.

He rightly said "for sterns" <note>original: *puppibus*</note>, for the Remora does not cling to the prow, nor to the sides, but to the rudder; and indeed, with its mouth fixed to the tiller (by which the whole ship is driven), and its tail or its whole body moving now to the left side, now to the right, it causes the ship itself to waver uncertainly and prevents it from holding a straight course. This is just as **Rondelet** in Book 16, Chapter 18, set out to demonstrate from **Aristotle’s** *Mechanics*. In that same place, he also presented the varying descriptions of this fish found in **Oppian**, **Pliny**, and **Aristotle**. Truly, **Keckermann** <note>Bartholomäus Keckermann (c. 1572–1609), a German writer and theologian</note> was ridiculous in his *Physical Disputations*, who—as cited also by **Jonston** <note>Jan Jonston (1603–1675), a Polish physician and natural historian</note> in *The Wonders of Fishes*, Chapter 17—attributed this effect to a <insert>cold</insert> humor...

<summary>This page clarifies that the "Sea Dragon" is a fish with sharp spines rather than a mythical beast and discusses the legendary ship-stopping ability of the Remora (Echeneis). The author cites various classical and Renaissance authorities to explain that the Remora halts vessels by attaching to the rudder and disrupting its movement.</summary>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on marine life and natural history, following the previous page's discussion of the Remora and the Sea Dragon. The catchword "frigido" (cold) transitions into the author's critique of Bartholomew Keckermann’s theory regarding the Remora's ability to stop ships.</meta>

[He] seems to attribute that effect to a cold humor poured out by the **Remora** <note>original: *Remora*; also known as the suckerfish, which was legendary in antiquity for its supposed ability to stop ships in their tracks by attaching to the hull</note>, which freezes the water around the rudder. If that were the cause, the ship could not have been moved immediately once the **Remora** <note>original: *Echeneide*</note> was removed, which is the opposite of what happened. See **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), Roman naturalist</note>, Book 32, Chapter 1, and that emphatic simile of **Oppian** <note>Greek poet of the 2nd century AD, author of the *Halieutica*, a poem on fishing</note>, Book 12 <note>Though the OCR says Book 12, Oppian’s *Halieutica* consists of only five books; this likely refers to a specific passage in Book 1 or 2 often cited by later scholars</note>, Chapter 45, where the **Remora** <note>original: *Echeneis*</note>, gripping the very edge of the stern with its teeth, restrains it as if with a strong bit on an unbroken horse, etc.

**Ibid. Capnelaeum** <note>Smoke-oil; from the Greek *kapnos* (smoke) and *elaion* (oil)</note>] The manuscript had *Capueleum*; but because it is explained shortly after as **Naphtha**, called such by the Medes, and known to the Greeks as **Medea’s oil** <note>original: *Medeias elaion*; a reference to the mythical sorceress who used combustible oils</note>, and to **Nicolaus Alexandrinus** <note>A 13th-century physician known for his pharmaceutical writings</note> as **rock-oil** <note>original: *petrelaion*</note>, and hence called **petroleum** in the apothecary shops; I therefore did not hesitate to read **Capnelaeum**, as it is known in Cilicia <note>A region on the southern coast of modern-day Turkey</note>, which in Sparta is called **spontaneous flow** <note>original: *protorrhytos*</note>, that is, a resin that flows of its own accord. See **Galen** <note>The most influential physician of the Roman Empire (c. 129–216 AD)</note>, Book 2 *On Places* and Book 3 *On Genres*.

**Page 146. Moray eel** <note>original: *Smyraina* (Greek: Σμύραινα)</note>] Thus on page 26. Regarding what our author brings forward in this place concerning the mutual hatred between the **Octopus** <note>original: *Polypi*</note> and the **Moray eel** <note>original: *muraenae*</note>, as well as the **Spiny Lobster** <note>original: *Carabi* or *locustae*</note>, **Oppian** has brilliantly described that battle in Book 2 of the *Halieutica*. Indeed, how bold and—as our author has it—how aggressive the Moray is toward men, has been recorded by **Nicander** <note>A Greek poet and physician (2nd century BC) who wrote about venomous animals</note>.

**Page 152. Sea Hare** <note>original: *Lagōos thalassios* (Greek: Λαγωὸς θαλάσσιος)</note>] We have marked this indistinct place with an asterisk: As for those who have drunk it (namely, the **Sea Hare**, for its poison was accustomed to be served in drink no less than in food, according to the testimony of **Pliny**, Book 31, Chapter 1), give them warm **goose blood**, etc. to drink. **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and pharmacologist (c. 40–90 AD) whose work was the primary source for medicines for 1,500 years</note> recommended the same antidote in Book 6, Chapter 30.
