<meta>This page continues a philological and scientific commentary on a manuscript regarding marine life. The previous page discussed the legendary ship-stopping powers of the Remora and the chemical properties of "smoke-oil" or petroleum. Here, the commentator corrects the original author's nomenclature and classification of various fish and sea creatures, drawing on classical authorities like Pliny, Dioscorides, and Aristotle, as well as Renaissance naturalists like Rondeletius.</meta>

## Ibid. MARIDES. ]
Just as elsewhere our author placed a *z* or *s* before **Muraena** <note>original: *Murænæ*; the moray eel</note>, so in this place he has omitted it. For he intended to say **Smarides** <note>original: *Smarides*; a type of small Mediterranean fish</note>, namely a species of the **Maena** <note>original: *mænæ*; the blotched picarel</note> called the **white-maena** <note>original: *λευκομαιώιδα* (*leukomainida*)</note>; which is why **Paulus Aegineta** <note>A 7th-century Byzantine Greek physician</note> also subjoined the **Smaris** immediately after the **Maena**. The **Smaris** possesses the same essence and flesh as the **Maena**, and **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist</note> (Book 32, Chapter 10) attributes to it the same qualities that **Dioscorides** <note>Greek physician and botanist</note> (Book 2, Chapter 30) attributes to the **Maena**; above all, both wrote that both fishes are effective for **warts of the seat** <note>original: *thymia sedis*; refers to excrescences or warts in the anal region</note>. From these passages, it was possible to correct our author regarding the **Xuthus** fish, which he says is called **Smirida** and "heals muzzles"; for it is clearly gathered that one should read **Smaris** and **warts** <note>original: *thymos*; the commentator is correcting a scribal error where *thymos* (warts) was likely mistaken for *phimos* (muzzles/bridles)</note>. See **Rondeletius** <note>Guillaume Rondelet (1507–1566), a famous French anatomy professor and naturalist noted for his work on fish</note>, Book 5 on Fish, Chapter 14. Or rather, see **Guillaume Pellicier** <note>A French bishop and diplomat who provided much data to Rondeletius</note>, whom he cited in that place. From him, **Rondeletius** is said to have transcribed almost everything (according to **Thuanus** <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou, a French historian</note>), where he also has some remarks on **Garum** <note>a fermented fish sauce prized in antiquity</note>, which is called **brine** <note>original: *muria*</note> and is prepared from these fish.

---

## P. 153. PULMONARIUS. ]
Thus in the manuscript, but read **Sea-lung** <note>original: *πλεύμων ἅλιος* (*pleumon halos*)</note>, which reflects the nature of a plant more than a fish; about which **Athenaeus** <note>Greek rhetorician and grammarian</note> writes in Book 8 and **Dioscorides** in Book 2, Chapter 38. <note>The "Sea-lung" usually refers to a species of large jellyfish, such as *Rhizostoma pulmo*, which looks like a breathing lung in the water</note>

---

## P. 154. RAPHIS .i. suture. ]
Read **Suture** [seam] or **Awl** <note>original: *subula*</note>. **Aristotle** and other Greeks always used the term **Needlefish** <note>original: *βελόνην* (*belonen*)</note>, but those who now inhabit Greece call it the **little needle** <note>original: *βελονίδα* (*belonida*)</note>. See **Rondeletius**, Book 8, Chapter 3. And so our author also delighted in this latter name, for which see also page 53.

<meta>This page continues a philological and scientific commentary on a manuscript regarding marine life. The previous page discussed the identification of the Smaris fish and corrected scribal errors regarding its medical uses for skin growths. This page continues with various fish species—the Cuttlefish, Synagris, Red Mullet, Stingray, and Gilt-head Bream—detailing their regional names, medical properties, and references in classical and Renaissance natural history.</meta>

*Ibid.* **RINAS** <note>original: *RINAS*; likely a transliteration of the Greek for "skin" or a specific fish name</note>. that is, Skin of a frog.] I am uncertain whether it should be written as **rhinos** <note>Greek: *ῥινὸς*; skin or hide</note> or **rhines** <note>Greek: *ῥίνης*; the Angelshark</note>. For the former denotes any kind of skin, while the latter could denote something belonging to the **Rhina** fish <note>The Angelshark, known for its rough skin used as an abrasive</note>. Thus, the first would refer to the whole thing (the frog's skin), while the second would regard only the subject or the **Rhina**.

*Ibid.* **The shells of CUTTLEFISH** <note>original: *SEPIARUM testæ*; cuttlebone</note> cleanse the eyelids.] **Rondelet** <note>Guillaume Rondelet (1507–1566), a French physician and naturalist famous for his work on fish</note> says the same (Book 17, Chapter 2, page 504): it is added to eye salves for roughness of the eyelids, and it clears away **skin spots** <note>original: *vitiligines*</note>, **scurf** <note>original: *furfures*; flaky skin or dandruff</note>, **teeth** <note>Cuttlebone was frequently used as a tooth-polishing powder</note>, **facial skin defects**, and **growths in the eyes** <note>original: *ungves oculorū*; literally "nails of the eyes," likely referring to pterygium</note>. See **Galen** <note>Claudius Galenus, the influential 2nd-century Greek physician</note> (Book 11, *On the Properties of Simple Medicines*).

*Ibid.* **Synagris** <note>original: *Συναγρίδος*; likely the Dentex fish</note>] which they also call the **synodonta** <note>original: *συνόδοντα*; "joined teeth"</note>. **Gaza** <note>Theodorus Gaza, a 15th-century translator of Aristotle</note> calls it the **dentex**, the Italians call it **dentale**, and the French call it **denté**. From this name, it is said to be beneficial for **toothache**.

**P. 155. TRIGLA.** <note>The Red Mullet</note>] Called **Triga** by those in Marseille <unclear>suinmoto</unclear>, **Stritha** by the people of Nice. Truly, because of the **beard** <note>original: *barbâ*</note> that they have, which **Kiranides** <note>The *Kyranides*, an ancient collection of magico-medical lore</note> extols, they are called **Barboni** by the Venetians and **Barbeau** in Bordeaux. Rondelet (Book 10, Chapter 3).

*Ibid.* **The sting of the Trygon** <note>original: *Τρυγόνος κέντρον*; the sting of a stingray</note>] that is, the **Stingray** <note>original: *Pastinaca*</note>, for so **Pliny**, **Cornelius Celsus**, and **Gaza** translate it. It is also called **bastonago** by the Sicilians and **pastenago** by others. It is called **bastango** in the Provençal dialect. According to **Pliny** (Book 32, Chapter 2) and **Aelian** (Book 13, Chapters 5 and 6), trees are killed by the sting of the **Stingray**. See Guillaume Rondelet (Book 12, *On Fish*, Chapter 1) and **Aldrovandi** <note>Ulisse Aldrovandi, a 16th-century Italian naturalist</note> (Book 15, *Ornithology*, Chapter 10, page 236).

**P. 157. CHRYSOPHI** <note>The Gilt-head Bream</note>] So it appears in the manuscript and on page 63, but it is called **chrysophrys** <note>original: *χρύσοφρυς*; "golden brow"</note>. See Rondelet (Book 5, Chapter 2), where he also says something about the **little stones in its head** <note>Otoliths, or ear-stones, used in early modern medicine</note> which cure **consumptives** <note>original: *Phtificos*; those suffering from wasting diseases like tuberculosis</note> mentioned above.

**P. ult. Oa** <note>original: *Ὠὰ*; eggs</note>] **salted fish eggs** <note>original: *ταρίχα* (*taricha*)</note>, commonly called **Botargues** <note>Bottarga, a Mediterranean delicacy of cured, salted fish roe</note>. They stimulate the appetite. See Rondelet.

A. P. ☉ <note>The symbol for the Sun, often used by this author as a signature</note>.

<meta>This page continues the philological and medical commentary on the "Cyranides," an ancient Greco-Roman text on the magical and medicinal properties of plants, animals, and stones. The previous page concluded an alphabetical index; this page provides specific scholarly notes on fish and aquatic substances mentioned in the main text, citing Renaissance naturalists like Rondelet and ancient authorities like Pliny and Galen.</meta>

A.   P.   Θ. <note>These initials likely refer to the commentator or a library shelf mark.</note>

*In the same place. RINAS, that is, Frog's skin.*] I am in doubt whether one should write *rhinos* <note>original: ῥινός; meaning skin or hide</note> or *rhinēs* <note>original: ῥίνης</note>: for the former denotes any skin whatsoever, while the latter could denote something of the Rhina fish <note>the Angelshark</note>. Therefore, the former would refer to the whole (the Frog's skin), while the latter would refer only to the subject at hand or the Rhina fish.

*In the same place. THE SHELLS OF CUTTLEFISH* cleanse the eyelids. ] Guillaume Rondelet <note>a 16th-century physician and naturalist</note> says the same in Book 17, Chapter 2, page 504. It is added to eye salves for roughness of the eyelids; it clears away skin spots, dandruff, cleans the teeth, treats facial skin defects, and "nails" <note>likely pterygium, a fleshy growth on the eye</note> of the eyes. See Galen, Book 11, *On the Faculties of Simple Medicines*.

*In the same place.* $\Sigma\upsilon\nu\alpha\gamma\rho\acute{\iota}\delta\alpha$ <note>original: Synagrida</note> ] which they also call the *synodont* <note>original: συνοδοντα; literally "joined teeth"</note>; Theodore Gaza <note>a translator of Aristotle</note> calls it the *dentex*, to the Italians it is the *dentale*, to the French *denté*. For this reason, it is said to be beneficial for toothaches.

**P. 155. THE MULLET.** <note>original: TRIGLA</note> ] To the people of Marseille it is called *suirmote* or *Triga*, to those of Nice *Stritha*; but because of the beard which they have—and which the Cyranides <note>here referred to as "Kiranus"</note> praises—it is named *Barboni* by the Venetians and *Barbeau* by those of Bordeaux. Rondelet, Book 10, Chapter 3.

*In the same place.* THE STING OF THE STINGRAY <note>original: Τρυγόνος κέντρον</note> ] That is, the *Pastinaca*; for so Pliny, Cornelius Celsus, and Gaza render it. To the Sicilians it is also called *bastonago*, and to others *pastenago*. In Provence it is called *bastango*. For trees are killed by the sting of the stingray according to Pliny, Book 32, Chapter 2, and Aelian, Book 13, Chapters 5 and 6. See Guillaume Rondelet, Book 12, *On Fish*, Chapter 1; and Ulisse Aldrovandi <note>a famous Italian Renaissance naturalist</note>, Book 15, *Ornithology*, Chapter 10, page 236.

**P. 157. THE CHRYSOPHUS.** <note>The Gilt-head Bream</note> ] Thus in the Manuscript and on page 63, but it is properly called *chrysophrys* <note>original: χρύσοφρυς; literally "golden-brow"</note>. See Rondelet, Book 5, Chapter 2, where something is also mentioned regarding the small stones in its head which heal those suffering from consumption <note>tuberculosis</note>, as noted above.

*On the last page. EGGS* <note>original: Ὠά</note> ] Salted fish eggs <note>original: ταρείχα</note>, commonly called **Bottarga**, which excite the appetite. See Rondelet.
